--- US-050 — Postman dynamic variables (PASSED) ---
  Was 2 hardcoded ReplaceAll calls ($timestamp, $isoTimestamp); now the full
  Postman set — 22 variables including $guid, $randomInt, $randomEmail, $randomIP.
  Before this, every other placeholder went out on the wire LITERALLY, so an
  imported collection sent the string "{{$randomEmail}}" as an email address.

  Both of the story's subtle rules proven by negative control, and both fail in
  the direction that looks like success:
    resolve-once-per-string (the ReplaceAll shape) -> "all 20 occurrences
      produced the same value". A user generating ten records would get ten
      identical ones and no error.
    unknown-resolves-to-empty -> "prefix {{$nope}} suffix" became "prefix
      suffix". A typo silently emptied looks like a deliberate blank field;
      left literal, the user sees their own mistake in the request.

  crypto/rand rather than math/rand: these values land in request bodies, and a
  user generating test credentials should not get a sequence predictable from
  one sample. The cost is nothing next to the HTTP request that follows.

                                               Preferences, cookies, notifications)

  A failed dynamic import surfaces as an unhandled rejection, so cycling the
  panels with that listener attached is what makes "the 60% bundle reduction did
  not break anything" a measurement rather than a hope. Connection-refused
  noise from the deliberate loopback request is filtered out.

--- US-009/010/011 — the response body trio ---
  US-011 DONE. io.ReadAll on the response body replaced with a bounded read,
    100 MB default, configurable via preferences.Request.MaxResponseBytes.
    The subtle half: io.LimitReader alone cannot tell "body was exactly the
    limit" from "body was longer and we stopped", so a body landing on the cap
    would be truncated and reported complete. Reading limit+1 distinguishes
    them; the naive version fails the exactly-at-limit case. Truncation is
    surfaced via x-liteapi-body-truncated / x-liteapi-body-limit headers, so it
    travels into saved examples and exports and is never later mistaken for a
    complete body.

  US-010 Go half DONE — ReadResponseBody(handle, offset, length). The slicing
    rules are the substance: a UTF-8 slice must not split a rune (removing the
    alignment fails at offset 1 with invalid UTF-8) and a base64 slice must be
    quartet-aligned. Start aligns FORWARD, end aligns BACKWARD — aligning the
    start backward would re-send bytes and could return the same offset forever,
    a paging loop that never advances. Offset/Length describe what was actually
    returned, so a caller must advance by those.
    REMAINING: response.ts moving Render full / Load more onto the binding.

  US-009 at step 4 of 5, every step so far ADDITIVE and reversible:
    1  responseStore, standalone. Memory is a front for the spill dir; every
       Put writes to disk before returning. Negative control: removing the
       durable write fails only the 2 durability tests — the other 6 pass, which
       is exactly why a memory-only store would have looked fine.
    2a store reachable from App, lazily (newAppBase runs for every test App;
       eager creation would litter empty responses/ dirs).
    2b BodyHandle + BodyHead added ALONGSIDE Body/BodyBase64, both populated.
       Head truncates on a UTF-8 boundary — a naive slice splits a rune and
       json rewrites it as U+FFFD.
    3  migration backfills handles for pre-US-009 state.json files. Fixture
       hand-built in the OLD shape, because generating it from today's structs
       would test nothing.
    4  handles attached on the live request path at all 3 response sites.
    5  NOT DONE — delete Body/BodyBase64. THE ONLY IRREVERSIBLE STEP, and where
       migrateResponseBodiesLocked must change from best-effort to fallible: a
       missing handle stops being harmless and becomes data loss. The 69
       existing Response.Body assertions and response.ts change together here.

  THEN: Phase 3 (US-039..US-059), Phase 4 (US-060..US-071) — with US-077 FIRST —
  and Phase 5 (US-072..US-074), all untouched.

  BROWSER QA — DONE for US-014 and US-021 against the real Wails dev endpoint
    (localhost:34115, which carries the Go bindings; NOT vite's 5173, which does
    not). US-014: revision deltas [1,1,1] through the real bridge, narrow payload
    2,265 B vs 68,808 B full state on the small default workspace, and

    for 6 sends + disconnect, indices contiguous from 0, routing ids correct,
    x-websocket-events header = 13 = push count, echo payload observed.
    US-022 (gRPC push) — NO LONGER OWED. See the US-022 entry at the end.

--- US-025 — extract inlined modals (PASSED: 29 of 29; real a11y bug fixed) ---
  THE STORY'S PREMISE WAS WRONG, and measuring first is the whole value here.
  It asks that focus traps, inert app-shell blocking, returnFocus with
  preventScroll, aria-modal and aria-labelledby stay "intact" through the
  extraction. Measured against the RUNNING app before any change:
    27 of 29 dialogs had NO keydown handler at all (no Escape, no Tab trap)
    `inert` was applied to the app shell for exactly TWO of them
  With the notifications dialog open:
    app shell inert ... false | focusable behind ... 41 | focus escaped ... true
    background clickable ... true | Escape closed it ... false
  Every one declares aria-modal="true", which tells assistive technology the
  rest of the page is inert. A FALSE PROMISE to screen-reader users. A pure
  extraction would have copied it into 29 tidy files and the story would have
  "passed".

  lib/modals/Modal.svelte now IMPLEMENTS the four guarantees once. ALL 29
  dialogs converted; the only role="dialog" left in App.svelte is a
  querySelector guard in script. App.svelte 13,340 -> 13,204 lines.

  Retired as redundant: handleCreationDialogKeydown, handleCommandPaletteKeydown,
  handleTabLifecycleDialogKeydown, handleImportReplaceConfirmationKeydown, the
  shared containModalTab helper, and the
  `inert={creationOpen || commandPaletteOpen}` binding — which was always only
  half the story, since Modal applies inert for EVERY dialog rather than two.
  All per-dialog focus machinery is now gone from App.svelte.

  METHOD THAT MADE 20 BULK EDITS SAFE: a script that finds each dialog's
  matching close by tracking div depth and derives onClose from the header's
  cancel button, applied ONE AT A TIME with a typecheck between and automatic
  revert on failure. 20/20 clean, 0 reverts. Earlier in this session two
  multi-edit scripts aborted mid-way and left partial state; one-at-a-time makes
  a bad edit cost one revert instead of a corrupted 13k-line file.

  BROWSER-VERIFIED on 6 dialogs (notifications, item-info, rename-request,
  clone-request, request-code, command-palette): reachable controls behind the
  modal 0, focus could not escape, Escape closed, focus returned to trigger,
  inert lifted on close with no leak. command-palette matters most — it is one
  of the two that previously DID work, so it is the regression risk.

  new-request, flagged unverified in the previous entry, is now verified too:
  inert while open, 0 reachable behind, focus could not escape, Escape closed,
  inert lifted. Note Cmd+N did NOT open it from a synthetic KeyboardEvent even
  though the button advertises the shortcut — the "+ New" button route did. Not
  investigated further; it may be a real shortcut-binding gap or simply that the
  handler needs a trusted event.

--- US-036 — split the frontend bundle (PARTIAL when written; NOW MET, see the
    US-036 re-measurement at the end of this file) ---
  MEASURED: initial JS 1,159,933 -> 533,898 bytes (54% smaller). CodeMirror
  (623,453 B across 11 packages) now loads only when a user first opens Body,
  Script, Tests or Docs.

  Done by a drop-in LazyCodeEditor wrapper that dynamically imports the real
  CodeEditor and forwards its exact props, so all NINE call sites in App.svelte
  were left untouched and cannot drift from the component signature. The vite
  manualChunks entry only SHAPES where the deferred bytes land — it is the
  dynamic import that moves them off the cold path. A manual chunk alone would
  still load eagerly, which is worth knowing before anyone "optimises" by
  adding manualChunks without a dynamic import.

  NOT MET: "initial chunk under 500 KB". 533,898 was 33,898 over; after US-025
  it is 532,602, still 32,602 over — US-025 moved dialog WRAPPERS, not bodies,
  so it barely moved the number and was never going to. Closing the gap means
  extracting the 29 modal BODIES (~57 KB of source, itemised in bundle.txt) into
  lazily-imported components. Unlike the wrapper conversion that is NOT
  scriptable: each body needs its own form state and handlers threaded as props.
  AND IT CANNOT BE MET IN THIS STORY. frontend/package.json "dependencies"
  contains ONLY @codemirror/* and codemirror — every one now deferred. The
  remaining chunk is App.svelte's own compiled output (~14k lines) plus the
  Svelte runtime. There is no dependency left to split.

  Getting under 500 KB requires splitting App.svelte, i.e. extracting the
  DevTools, Runner, Import, Preferences and OpenAPI-sync surfaces into
  dynamically importable components — which is US-025. The same blocker applies
  to US-036's own criterion "DevTools, Runner, Import, Preferences,
  OpenAPI-sync and CodeMirror are dynamically imported": only CodeMirror is a
  separate component today.
  SEQUENCING THE SPEC DOES NOT STATE: US-025 must precede US-036.

  Browser-verified: Body tab mounts the lazy editor, CodeMirror DOM present and
  focusable, no stuck placeholder, no load error.

--- US-035 — debounce patchRequest with optimistic state (PASSED) ---
  MEASURED in the browser against the real Wails endpoint:
    36 characters typed -> 3 IPC calls   (12x fewer round trips)
    input showed every character immediately (optimistic path works)
    backend converged on the exact typed string — no keystroke lost

  BROWSER QA CAUGHT A REAL BUG THAT THE UNIT TESTS DID NOT. First run stored
  "https://coalesed.example/abcefghij" for 36 characters of
  "https://coalesced.example/abcdefghij" — characters silently dropped.

  CAUSE: the in-flight overwrite. While a flush is on the wire the user keeps
  typing; the result that comes back describes the request as of when the call
  was MADE, so applying it verbatim REWINDS the input and discards everything
  typed in the meantime. Neither the coalescer's own tests nor svelte-check can
  see this — it needs a real round trip with real latency.
  FIX: PatchCoalescer exposes pendingPatch, and the flusher re-applies it over
  the authoritative result. Regression test added, written against the measured
  before/after strings so it documents the actual failure rather than a
  hypothesis.

  METHOD NOTE, worth repeating: my first "Send" check reported the criterion
  satisfied when it had NOT exercised Send at all — the selector /^send$/i
  missed the button because its label is "Send ⌘↵", and the URL landing in the
  backend was just the debounce firing during the 900 ms wait. A criterion is
  only verified when the thing under test actually ran; sendWasInvoked is now
  asserted explicitly rather than inferred from a side effect.

  Send, tab switch and window blur all await the flush. Fire-and-forget would be
  worse than nothing: it looks right and loses the last keystroke precisely when
  the user is watching for it.

  The disk autosave debounce is untouched — separate timer, separate delay,
  still invoked from the same point in the flow (criterion: "remains
  independent and unchanged").

  NODE HARNESS CONSTRAINT worth knowing for future frontend modules: `node
  --test` runs strip-only TypeScript and REJECTS parameter properties
  (ERR_UNSUPPORTED_TYPESCRIPT_SYNTAX). Declare and assign fields explicitly.

--- US-030 — key every {#each} block (PASSED) ---
  116 -> 0 unkeyed blocks. eslint's svelte/require-each-key is RE-ENABLED (the
  disable US-004 added is deleted), and proven live by negative control:
  unkeying one block gives 1 error and exit 1.

  Census was 116, not the 106 the story claims. 95 App.svelte, 14
  ResponseInspector, 7 across lib/.

  TWO FINDINGS THAT DECIDED THE KEYS, both established by reading declarations
  and models.ts rather than assumed:

  1. devToolsNetworkColumnWidths = [80, 70, 180, 300, 110, 100, 80] — the value
     80 appears TWICE, so keying by value would have crashed the DevTools
     network panel on render.
  2. SEVEN iterated types carry NO id: KeyValue (params/headers/form rows),
     Assertion, GrpcMessage, WSMessage, ClientCertificateConfig, ScriptLog,
     RunResult. A blanket ".id" emits undefined for every row of these, and
     repeated undefined IS a duplicate key.

  CONSEQUENCE — the story's rule "keys are stable identities, not array indices"
  is WRONG for a large minority of these lists, and following it literally would
  have been the bug. For editable rows with no id, index is the CORRECT key: the
  only content-derived alternative changes as the user types, so it would
  destroy and recreate the <input> on every keystroke — strictly worse than
  leaving the block unkeyed, and the exact opposite of the story's own goal.
  Read-only display lists rebuilt wholesale (diff rows, console output, timeline
  metadata, change lists) are index-keyed for the same reason: no identity
  exists, and nothing is being edited.

  A COLLISION THE FIRST PASS CAUGHT: keying by expression TEXT alone is unsafe
  across files. `groups` in App.svelte is {folder, items}[]; `groups` in
  OAuth2AdditionalParams is {id, label}[]. The first pass keyed both on .folder
  and svelte-check caught it. Two more cross-file collisions existed (`methods`,
  `rows ?? []`) but were safe by luck — both resolutions happened to suit either
  shape. Any future bulk edit of this kind must key on (file, expression).

  VERIFIED IN THE BROWSER, which is the only gate that can see a duplicate key
  (svelte-check, eslint and the production build are all blind to it):
    0 duplicate-key errors, 0 console errors, 17 tab panels cycled
    focus retention: input focused -> narrow mutation re-renders the list ->
      SAME DOM node still focused and still in the document
  That last one is the story's actual user-facing criterion.

--- US-075 — top toolbar overflow at 1200px (FIXED, verified in browser) ---
  The Phase 0 report reproduced EXACTLY, to the pixel: "Development" ended at
  x=666 while "Cookies" started at x=655 — an 11px overlap at a 1200px viewport.

  ROOT CAUSE, found by walking the computed box chain rather than guessing:
    .environment-menu        width  96,  right 636
    .environment-menu>button width 146,  right 686   <-- 50px OUTSIDE its parent
  The button carried `max-width: 170px`, a FIXED cap unrelated to the space
  actually available. Flex shrank .environment-menu, the button kept its content
  width, and painted over its neighbour. The inner span already had
  overflow:hidden + text-overflow:ellipsis — it simply never got the chance,
  because nothing ever forced the button narrower.
  FIX: max-width: min(170px, 100%). One line. Keeps the original cap, adds the
  missing one.

  MEASURED AFTER (Playwright against the real Wails dev endpoint, not vite):
    1024px  0 real overlaps  0 clipped-without-label  button escape 0px
    1200px  0 real overlaps  0 clipped-without-label  button escape 0px
    1440px  0 real overlaps  0 clipped-without-label  env name fully legible
    no horizontal page scroll at any width

  TWO MEASUREMENT MISTAKES I MADE, recorded because both would have produced a
  false report:
   1. My first overlap detector compared adjacent .topbar CHILDREN, which are
      stacked ROWS, and "found" an 888px overlap. Meaningless. Real detection
      needs intersection on BOTH axes.
   2. The corrected detector then flagged 2 overlaps inside
      .recovery-center-list — an absolutely-positioned dropdown that happened to
      be OPEN. An overlay drawn above content is correct behaviour, not a defect.
      The detector now excludes anything under a positioned ancestor.
   Also: checking `title` on the leaf element alone reported 4 items as clipped
   with no accessible text. Walking ancestors showed all 4 are labelled. The
   shallow check would have invented work that did not exist.

  SEPARATE FINDING, verified and NOT fixed here — DEAD RESPONSIVE CSS:
    style.css:1965 and :5460 both set `grid-template-columns` on .topbar, but
    .topbar is `display: flex`. Confirmed live at a 1100px viewport (below the
    1180px breakpoint): computed display is `flex` while grid-template-columns
    is `minmax(0px, 1fr)` — applied and INERT, since it only affects grid
    containers. Both media queries have never done anything. Left in place
    deliberately: they are evidence that responsive handling was INTENDED here
    and silently never worked, which US-037 should resolve properly rather than
    have me delete the trace of it.

--- US-076 — stale workspace pointer across the runner's lock release (FIXED) ---
  A real, silent data-loss bug, confirmed by negative control rather than by
  reading. sendRequestWithControlsContext captured `ws` — a *Workspace pointing
  INTO the a.state.Workspaces backing array — released a.mu for the network
  round trip, re-acquired it, and then handed that same pointer to
  applyScriptVariableContextToState. Any append that grew Workspaces past
  capacity while the lock was free moved the backing array, after which the
  write landed in memory nothing reads. No error, no crash: the variable simply
  vanished.

  THE FIX WAS ALREADY SITTING THERE, DISCARDED. The line that re-acquires the
  lock already re-resolved the collection:
      _, collection, err = a.findCollectionWithWorkspaceIndexedLocked(...)
  It threw the freshly-resolved workspace away with `_` and kept using the stale
  one. Keeping it is the entire fix.

  MEASURED by negative control (stale pointer restored, then reverted):
    with the bug: global variable set by a post-response script -> ""  (lost)
    with the fix: -> "survived", and still present on a fresh GetState
  The bug only bites when a script marks GLOBAL variables dirty, because that is
  the only branch of applyScriptVariableContextToState that dereferences the
  workspace at all. The test drives bru.setGlobalEnvVar for exactly that reason.

  WHY -race NEVER CAUGHT IT, and never would: both accesses are correctly
  serialised under a.mu. Nothing is racing. The pointer is simply stale. This is
  worth remembering — a clean -race run says nothing about pointer lifetime
  across a deliberate lock release.

  Two tests, deliberately of different kinds: a behavioural one that forces the
  reallocation inside the I/O window (the httptest handler creates 8 workspaces
  while a.mu is free), and a source-level guard. The behavioural test only fails
  when the reallocation actually happens, which depends on slice capacity and
  could quietly stop being true if the fixture changes shape — leaving the bug
  uncovered with the test still green. The guard fails the moment the captured
  pointer reappears at the call site, whatever the fixture looks like.

--- US-019 — INVESTIGATED, NOT STARTED. Read this before implementing it. ---
  Analysis done so the next iteration does not repeat it. Two of the story's
  criteria are not equally achievable, and the difference matters.

  1. "sync.Pool of pre-warmed runtimes, reset between uses" — LIKELY INFEASIBLE
     AS WRITTEN, and the story does not acknowledge why. Nearly every shim
     installed by newScriptRuntimeWithMeta CLOSES OVER PER-REQUEST STATE. From
     the signatures alone:
         installScriptFetch(runtime, vars)
         installScriptProcess(runtime, loop, meta.CollectionPath,
                              scriptVars.ProcessEnv, sandboxMode)
     plus reqObject's ~15 getters/setters, which capture item, vars, reqState
     and a currentRequestURL closure over item.Params/item.PathParams.
     A pooled runtime therefore cannot be "reset" cheaply — every global would
     have to be re-installed, which is the entire cost being avoided. Worse, a
     miss leaks the PREVIOUS request's url/headers/env into the next script,
     which is a correctness and secrets-leak failure, not a performance one.
     US-018 already banked the large win here (compiled *goja.Program cache,
     ~10x, 8.2x fewer allocs). What remains is object construction, and pooling
     it is not obviously safe.

  2. "One runtime instead of four where hooks share scope" — ACHIEVABLE, and is
     the real story. But it is 4 -> 2, not 4 -> 1, and the reason is structural:
        runPreRequestScript*        runs BEFORE the request exists as a
                                    response; response = Response{}, and its
                                    whole job is to mutate the outbound request.
                                    It CANNOT share a runtime with the others.
        runPostResponseScript*   \
        runPostResponseVariables  >  all run AFTER, with the same item,
        the tests runtime        /   response, jar and meta. THESE three can
                                    share one runtime.
     That is also closer to Postman, where state set in a post-response script
     is visible to tests.

  SEQUENCING HAZARD if implemented: runPostResponseVariables sets globals into
  its runtime — every combined variable by name, plus $bru and $req. Sharing a
  runtime makes those visible to the tests script, so the order the three run in
  becomes observable behaviour where today it is not. Decide and pin the order
  in a test before writing the refactor, not after.

  The "no variable or cookie state leaks between requests" criterion is now
  WRITTEN — script_isolation_test.go, 5 tests, before the refactor rather than
  after. They pin the current behaviour while four separate runtimes still make
  it true by construction, so they are the regression net for the 4->2 change.

  Both directions are pinned, which matters: bru.setVar is SUPPOSED to persist
  between requests, so a refactor that isolated too aggressively would break
  that while making every leak test pass. The suite asserts globals do NOT
  survive and runtime variables DO.

  Proven non-vacuous: a `typeof x === "undefined"` assertion passes trivially if
  the harness never runs the script, so the control planted a global in the same
  script and asserted it was undefined — the suite reported the failure with the
  expect() error, confirming assertions really execute and really fail.

--- US-014 — narrow-return mutators (PASSED, except browser QA) ---
  HEADLINE, measured on the 500-request fixture by marshalling both return
  values the way Wails does:
    one keystroke:  AppState 4,348,018 bytes  ->  narrow 8,515 bytes   (511x)

  Four narrow variants added beside the existing wide ones, which are untouched
  and still work — the story explicitly allows unmigrated callers. Each pair
  shares an extracted ...Locked core rather than duplicating the mutation, so
  the two entry points CANNOT drift. Drift is the failure that matters here: not
  "the narrow one is broken", which shows up at once, but "the two disagree",
  where frontend and backend hold different ideas of what the user typed and
  neither looks wrong.

  THE NO-OP CASE nearly broke the whole design. MoveOpenTab against the end of
  the tab bar mutates nothing and so does NOT bump the revision. The first
  gap-detection rule required exactly (held + 1), which would have classified
  every clamped tab drag as a missed update and fired a full AppState refetch —
  the exact cost this story removes, arriving through the mechanism meant to
  prevent it. Rule now accepts held or held+1, refetches on a skip AND on a
  revision going backwards (a response overtaking a newer one).

  BEHAVIOUR CHANGE in MoveOpenTab: offset 0 against a NON-EXISTENT tab used to
  return success, because the offset check came before the lookup. It now
  reports "tab not found". The narrow variant has to know whether the tab exists
  to return a meaningful result, and reporting success for a tab that is not
  there was wrong either way.

  TEST DESIGN NOTE: the anti-drift test first used two separate apps and had to
  normalise away FilePath and CreatedAt, which differed only because the temp
  dirs and clock readings differed. Every normalised field is a field where real
  drift can hide, so it was rewritten to drive both variants against THE SAME
  item on THE SAME app — leaving exactly one field to normalise (UpdatedAt,
  which both deliberately set to time.Now()).

  Frontend merge logic lives in src/lib/narrowMutations.ts so it is testable;
  10 new tests, almost all about results that must NOT be applied. A narrow
  result patched onto a stale copy is worse than a stale copy: the patch lands,
  the view looks freshly updated, and the stale parts stay stale forever.

  MIGRATED: patchRequest (the keystroke path) and all four tab call sites.
  NOT MIGRATED, deliberately: the two UpdateRequest calls in the
  variable-tooltip editor, each immediately followed by SaveRequest, which
  returns a full AppState anyway — narrowing them would add branching for zero
  saving.

  STILL OPEN: "Verify in browser using browser tools".

!!! RESOLVED (mechanism identified, see below) — the US-015 -race FAILURE !!!

  It was almost certainly NOT a data race. It was a transient BUILD failure
  caused by the test run compiling the package while I was mid-edit.

  How I know: chasing it again I reproduced the exact symptom by accident. A
  background `go test -race ./...` loop was running while I rewrote
  internal/types/proxy.go in the foreground, and run 4 produced:

      # LiteAPI [LiteAPI.test]
      ./app.go:4:2: could not import LiteAPI/internal/types (...)
      FAIL   LiteAPI [build failed]
      ?      LiteAPI/docs/qa/fixtures        [no test files]
      ...
      ok     LiteAPI/qa/platformfixture      2.247s
      ok     LiteAPI/qa/responsefixture      1.884s
      FAIL

  Exit 1, zero DATA RACE, zero `--- FAIL` lines. Now pipe THAT through
  `tail -5` — which is what the original run did — and every recorded symptom
  falls out exactly: the qa/ subpackages pass, the aggregate is FAIL, the root
  package is implicated, and no test name appears. The cause line is at the
  TOP, which is precisely what tail discards.

  This also explains why 11 subsequent isolated runs were all clean: nothing
  was being edited during them, so there was nothing to fail to build.

  THE REAL LESSON, and it is not the one I recorded first: `| tail` did not
  merely cost the diagnosis, it INVERTED it. A build failure reports at the
  top and a test failure reports in the middle, so tailing a Go test run is
  the one truncation guaranteed to hide the category of the problem. Do not
  run a background full-repo test loop while editing the repo either — that is
  what manufactured the failure both times.

  ORIGINAL ENTRY, kept because the reasoning that led here is worth keeping:
  `go test -race -count=1 ./...` failed ONCE immediately after the US-015 edit.
  The command was piped through `tail -5`, so the failing test name was lost;
  all that survives is that the qa/ subpackages passed and the aggregate was
  FAIL, which places it in the root LiteAPI package.

  NOT REPRODUCED in 7 subsequent runs:
    3x  go test -race -count=1 ./...      (full output captured, all clean)
    4x  go test -race -count=1 .
    1x  -count=5 over every test added by US-008/013/015/021/022
  So it is NOT being reported as fixed, and NOT as a US-015 defect — there is
  no evidence either way. It is recorded here because a flake seen once and
  chased zero times is how the US-012 "directory not empty" flake survived
  three stories.

  If it recurs: capture the FULL output (redirect to a file, never `| tail`),
  and start from the two known flake-prone areas — the background persist
  writer's interaction with t.TempDir() removal (fixed once already, see the
  US-012 entry below) and the live-session goroutines added by US-021/US-022.
  DO NOT re-pipe a -race run through tail; that is what cost the diagnosis here.

--- US-015 — dirty-set collection file writes (PASSED) ---
  All six write sites in writeCollectionFilesLocked now route through
  writeCollectionFileLocked, which skips the write when the bytes already match.

  MEASURED same-host, same-settings (-benchtime=200x -count=3), by stashing:
    BenchmarkWriteCollectionFilesLocked
      before 4,690,322 ns/op  268,377 B/op  2,648 allocs/op
      after  1,784,256 ns/op  262,881 B/op  2,648 allocs/op    2.63x
  Allocations are UNCHANGED, and that is the informative part: the entire win
  is I/O. The residual 1.78 ms is stringifyBru / stringifyYAMLRequest CPU, which
  this story does not target — a later story wanting more must cache the
  rendering, not the write.

  NEGATIVE CONTROL (gate removed, then restored) — the story's own wording,
  measured rather than asserted:
    changing ONE request rewrote 10 of 10 files   -> now exactly 1
    ten identical saves rewrote 8 files           -> now 0

  BEHAVIOUR CHANGE, deliberate: a collection file edited outside LiteAPI is now
  left alone when our content has not changed. Previously any unrelated save
  silently clobbered it. The collection watcher is what reconciles external
  edits and is unaffected, because skipping a write leaves the bytes exactly as
  the watcher last saw them.

  TEST-WRITING NOTE for later stories: CreateRequest leaves a request as an
  unsaved DRAFT. SaveRequest is what materialises it on disk and therefore what
  runs writeCollectionFilesLocked. A test that only calls CreateRequest/
  UpdateRequest touches no collection file at all — which is also why the
  earlier DeleteRequest probe in revision_test.go failed with "the file does
  not exist".

--- US-013 — independent dirty scopes for secrets and OAuth2 (PASSED) ---
  Half of this story was already done: US-012 had added a content compare to
  writeEnvironmentSecretsLocked (its comment even names US-013). What remained
  was the OAuth2 file, which had NO gating at all — storeOAuth2Credentials runs
  on every state persist and reads oauth2.json, DECRYPTS every stored
  credential, re-encrypts every merged credential, and writes.

  DESIGN CHOICE worth recording: the story says "each get an independent dirty
  flag", which reads as classifying the 81 markDirty call sites by which files
  they touch. Rejected. A misclassification there does not fail loudly — it
  silently stops persisting a user's secret or a refreshed token, and no test
  in this suite would catch it. Gated on a CONTENT FINGERPRINT instead, which
  cannot be wrong by construction: the write is skipped exactly when the bytes
  would be identical.

  The OAuth2 gate hashes (baseline, local), not local alone. The merge is a
  delta of local against baseline, so a key in baseline and absent from local
  is a DELETION — a revoked token. Hashing local alone makes that identical to
  "never existed", and the skip would swallow the revocation. There is a test
  for exactly this.

  Skipping is SAFE UNDER MULTIPLE WINDOWS in both scopes, and the direction is
  what makes it safe: if another window rewrote the file, our fingerprint still
  describes content identical to what we would write, so skipping leaves their
  newer file intact. Writing is the operation that could clobber.

  MEASURED, same host and settings (-benchtime=200x -count=3), by stashing the
  change and re-running:
    BenchmarkMarkDirty   before 67,484 ns/op  3,078 B/op  26 allocs
                         after  48,742 ns/op  2,290 B/op  24 allocs   (1.38x)
    BenchmarkPersistLockedSmallState  before 5,916,580  after 5,888,383 ns/op
                         — unchanged, and NOT a failure of the OAuth2 gate:
                         that benchmark is ~80% fsync (documented in bench.txt)
                         and its fixture holds zero OAuth2 credentials, so
                         there is nothing to decrypt. Allocations do drop 85->77.
                         The OAuth2 saving scales with credential count, which
                         this benchmark deliberately does not exercise.

  HONESTY NOTE ON THE TESTS: TestSecretsFileIsNotRewrittenByUnrelatedMutations
  would ALSO pass before this story, because US-012's byte compare already
  skipped the write. It is a contract guard, not evidence for US-013. The
  evidence for US-013's secrets half is the benchmark above and nothing else.
  Said so in the test's own doc comment rather than letting it look like proof.

--- US-021 + US-022 — push live WebSocket / gRPC events (PASSED) ---
  The push alone would NOT have fixed the quadratic cost, and the story text
  reads as though it would. Emitting each event incrementally is necessary but
  not sufficient: responseLocked still re-marshalled the whole event log, still
  base64'd it, and still stored it into item.Response inside AppState on every
  single message. Adding an emit on top of that is pure addition.
  So the fix has two halves, and the second is the one that removes the O(n^2):
    1. PUSH   — "ws:event" / "grpc:event", one event each, via EventsEmit.
    2. BOUND  — the response body carries only the last 200 events.

  MEASURED, by negative control (reverted after measuring):
    unbounded body   44,367 B at message 150 -> 88,142 B at message 300  (+98.7%)
    bounded body     29,375 B at message 150 -> 29,385 B at message 300  (+0.03%)
  Doubling the message count doubled the old body exactly as quadratic cost
  predicts. The residual 10 bytes is encoding/json rendering time.Time with
  variable fractional-second width, not a leak — which is why the test asserts
  a 1% bound rather than exact equality, with the reason written down.

  BEHAVIOUR CHANGE, deliberate and documented in session_event_push.go:
  reopening a request whose session ran past 200 events shows the last 200
  rather than all of them. The live UI stays complete because the frontend
  accumulates the pushes; the headers report the true total and the omitted
  count, so nothing is hidden silently.

  Every append routes through appendEventLocked (20 sites, was 20 raw appends).
  That is what makes "no event can be added without being pushed" structural
  rather than a convention. Negative control: reverting ONE site back to a raw
  append fails TestWebSocketEmitsEveryAppendedEvent ("got 6 pushes for 5 sends").

  FRONTEND: a bare append would have been wrong. A listener attaching
  mid-session starts at a non-zero index, and appending blindly yields a log
  that LOOKS complete and is silently missing its beginning — worse than showing
  nothing, because the user cannot tell. Pushes therefore carry index+total, and
  a log is authoritative only when contiguous from 0 and at least as long as the
  body window; otherwise the inspector falls back to the body. Same
  detect-a-gap-and-resync contract as AppState.Revision. 9 new frontend tests.

  Emission happens under session.mu on purpose: the callers already hold that
  lock across a full network round trip (WriteMessage then ReadMessage under a
  read deadline), so a webview notify does not change the critical section's
  shape, and emitting after the unlock would let goroutines interleave and
  deliver events out of order.

  STILL OPEN on these two stories: "Verify in browser using browser tools".

--- US-008 — AppState.Revision (PASSED) ---
  markDirty alone was NOT a sufficient hook, contrary to the obvious reading of
  the story. It covers 81 mutation sites, but 13 more real mutations call
  persistLocked directly for synchronous durability — collection imports,
  collection recovery, draft-guard saves, and the readiness normalisation inside
  ensureReadyLocked. Bumping only in markDirty would have left those silent, and
  silence is the dangerous direction: the frontend concludes it is in sync when
  it is not, and shows stale data forever.
  Resolution: persistLocked split into persistLocked (bump + write) and
  writeStateLocked (write only). persistOnce and flushPersistLocked moved to
  writeStateLocked because they DRAIN a mutation markDirty already counted —
  leaving them on persistLocked would double-count and manufacture a phantom gap.

  The counter lives on App, not inside AppState, and is scrubbed by
  stateForStorage. Both are deliberate; see the doc comments. Three paths
  restore a whole earlier AppState (two collection-import rollbacks, the
  OpenAPI-sync rollback) and would have dragged an in-state counter backwards.

  All four tests proven non-vacuous by negative control, each reverted after
  measuring: dropping the markDirty bump fails two of them; dropping the
  stateForStorage scrub fails IsNotPersisted; making GetState bump fails
  DoesNotAdvanceOnReads.

GAP FOUND IN US-012 — a story this run had already marked PASSING.
  Regenerating the Wails bindings for US-008 surfaced that FlushPendingWrites
  had NEVER been generated into frontend/wailsjs/go/main/App.js. US-012's
  criterion "Force-flush on beforeClose, shutdown, and window blur" was
  therefore only two-thirds done: the Go side was wired to OnBeforeClose and
  OnShutdown, but no frontend caller for blur existed or could exist.
  Impact: persistence is now behind a 250 ms debounce, so a user switching away
  inside that window lost their last edit.
  Fixed in the US-008 commit — App.svelte flushes on blur AND visibilitychange
  (the latter covers sleep/hide, which blur does not fire for).
  METHOD LESSON: "the Go binding exists" is not the same claim as "the frontend
  can call it". Any story that adds or relies on a binding must regenerate and
  check the diff in frontend/wailsjs/, not just compile the Go.

  SPEC CORRECTION: eslint-plugin-svelte v3 ships ZERO svelte/a11y-* rules (removed upstream;
  a11y now comes from the Svelte compiler). US-004's implied a11y coverage does not exist via
  the plugin. Compensated by enabling svelte/valid-compile, which surfaces compiler a11y
  warnings as lint errors - verified non-vacuous by a negative-control probe that made it fire
  a11y_missing_attribute / a11y_click_events_have_key_events / a11y_no_static_element_interactions.

  REAL BUG FOUND AND FIXED — frontend/src/lib/workbench/ResponseInspector.svelte:76-78.
    $: wsEvents = websocketEvents()   // and grpcEvents(), parsedJson()
    Svelte's legacy $: tracks only variables referenced syntactically inside the statement, not
    variables read inside a called function. These three referenced only the immutable function
    binding, so they had NO reactive dependencies and ran once at mount. With no {#key} wrapper
    (App.svelte:9485), the JSON tree view, WebSocket event log and gRPC stream log kept showing
    the FIRST response for the lifetime of the component.
    Fixed by threading (response, bytes) as parameters. Verified in source: all three now
    declare (response: main.Response | undefined, size: number). Rule kept enabled.
    Cost: +32 B JS, attributed by controlled revert/rebuild/restore (1,159,965 vs 1,159,933).

  Note: frontend/src/lib/workbench/ is UNTRACKED, so `git diff` shows nothing for these files.
  Review must read the files directly. This is a consequence of the diff-attribution issue below.

  SA4004 investigated, NOT a bug: openAPIExampleRawValue sorted example names then looped, but
  every path returned on iteration 1 - a "lowest-sorting key" idiom written as a loop, with an
  unreachable trailing return. Rewritten as a direct index; sort kept (with a comment) because it
  is what makes selection deterministic over Go's randomised map order.

  REAL BUG FOUND AND FIXED — copyCollectionFile (collection clone) held `defer target.Close()` on
  the WRITE handle. io.Copy succeeding returned nil, so a close-time failure (ENOSPC, over-quota,
  failing network FS) was silently discarded and a TRUNCATED COLLECTION COPY REPORTED SUCCESS.
  Now propagated via named return. Exactly the failure class that makes R4 mandatory for US-012.

  Worker correctly REFUSED an orchestrator instruction: told to exclude screen.Width if Wails
  v2.10.2 needed it, it verified the claim was false (all three backends populate Size), and

SPEC CLAIM §2.1.B — now VERIFIED end-to-end by reading, not assumed:
  persistLocked (app.go:8384) -> json.MarshalIndent(stateForStorage(a.state, a.dataDir)) ->
    single non-atomic os.WriteFile. Confirmed.
  stateForStorage (app.go:8863) does NOT strip response bodies. It only removes collection
    environment secrets, drops scratch collections, and encrypts cookie values. So BOTH
    Response.Body AND Response.BodyBase64 are serialised into state.json on EVERY persist.
    The "5 MB response costs ~11.7 MB and is re-serialised on every keystroke" claim is real.
  Fixture collections are non-scratch, so nothing is stripped -> the full ~37 MB is marshalled
    per persistLocked call. The benchmark measures the intended pathological path.

  Benchmark-validity check: persistLocked branches at :8385 —
    if a.workspaceRuntime != nil { return a.persistWorkspaceRuntimeLocked() }
  workspaceRuntime is assigned ONLY at workspace_window_runtime.go:241 (multi-window launch)
  and in workspace_window_runtime_test.go. NewAppWithDir leaves it nil, so the benchmark
  exercises the main path. Worth re-checking if US-012 changes that branch.

  No deadlock risk: storeOAuth2Credentials takes a.oauth2Mu, not a.mu, so the benchmark
  holding a.mu across persistLocked is safe.

     the FIRST response for the component's lifetime.  (US-004)
  3. Top toolbar does not lay out at a 1200px viewport: `Development` and `Cookies`
     overlap by 11px as siblings; three labels clipped; breadcrumb crushed to 80px.
     Found by computer-use QA, measured via getBoundingClientRect.  (new story US-075)

FOUR SPEC CORRECTIONS — improvement_v2.md is wrong or incomplete on each:
  a. §5.4 implies the US-006 fixture belongs beside qa/responsefixture. It cannot: the
     benchmark targets are unexported, so bench_test.go must be root package main, and no
     subpackage can import package main to reach AppState.
  b. US-004 assumes eslint-plugin-svelte provides a11y coverage. v3 ships ZERO svelte/a11y-*
     rules (removed upstream). Without noticing, US-004 would have "passed" while protecting
     none of the §2.2 accessibility work. Compensated with svelte/valid-compile.
  c. US-007's framing understates the cause: the pin drifted because QA used
     `wails build -m -nosyncgomod`, which suppresses the go.mod sync. It is a latent library
     mismatch, not a docs typo. The API diff v2.10.2 -> v2.12.0 is purely additive across
     every imported package (measured: zero removed lines).
  d. §5.1's ">5% regression on any benchmark" gate is not sound across hosts, and `du -b`
     is a GNU flag that fails on macOS. Both logged in .ralph/backlog.md.

  The US-003 worker's self-reported census (203 findings) differed from the orchestrator's
  first measurement (107) because golangci-lint truncates by default. The orchestrator's
  number was the wrong one. Every worker claim in this run was re-run independently before
  a story was marked passing; that is the only reason the error was caught.

KNOWN ISSUE — diff attribution:
  The working tree already carried substantial uncommitted work when this branch was cut
  (app.go modified + ~50 untracked .go files, all pre-existing). `git diff app.go` therefore
  mixes pre-existing work with story changes and cannot be used alone to review a story.
  Mitigation: take each worker's own file manifest as the story diff, and commit the
  pre-existing tree separately. Cutting the branch without a baseline commit first was a
  sequencing mistake; do not repeat it for Phase 1.




US-047 - Runner bail on failure. DONE.
  RunnerOptions gains BailOnFailure (json bailOnFailure). Two break points in
  RunCollectionWithOptions: after a transport error, and after a "failed"
  result. appendUnrunRunResults marks every remaining item "unrun".

  The one judgement call worth recording: the bail check sits AFTER the
  existing `status == "cancelled"` break. A cancelled run is not a failure, and
  labelling the rest "not run: the run stopped at an earlier failure" would
  misattribute the user's own cancellation to a test failure.

  Three statuses now mean three different things, which is the whole story:
    skipped   the runner declined to run it (wrong protocol, unresolved prompt)
    cancelled the user stopped the run
    unrun     an earlier failure ended the run first
  Collapsing any pair leaves a reader unable to tell a failing suite from an
  abandoned one.

  Test-fixture trap found while writing runner_bail_test.go: the runner walks
  the COLLECTION's item order, not the order of SelectedItemIDs. The first
  version of the skipped-vs-unrun test created its websocket request last, so
  it landed after the failure and came back "unrun" for the ordinary reason -
  the test failed while the code was right. bailFixture now takes an ordered
  spec list and the comment says why order matters.

  Negative controls, both run and both caught:
    unrun -> "skipped"        2 tests fail, incl. the collapse assertion
    bail break disabled       2 tests fail, third request comes back "passed"

  Frontend: a "Stop at the first failure" checkbox in RunnerPanel, bound
  through App.svelte and reset with the rest of the runner config. Without it
  the flag would be unreachable from the app.

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 37.0s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

  sharedCredentialHTTPClient() (app.go:15133, 16666, 16767, 17026, 17127,
  20012) and the PAC fetch calls sharedPACHTTPClient() (app.go:10626).

  Two things in app.go/collection_import.go match `&http.Client{` and are
  correctly NOT targets:
    app.go:1323            a.httpClient, the App's own base client.
                           requestTransport derives from its Transport, so it
                           is the cache's INPUT, not a bypass of it.
    collection_import.go   a 15s import fetch, not one of the six 30s clients
                           the story names, and in a different file.

  Added the guard the story lacked: TestNoOneOffOutboundClientsRemain scans
  app.go for `&http.Client{`. The existing tests pin what the shared clients
  DO; none of them notices a seventh call site appearing later that builds its

US-045 - Runner iterations. DONE.
  RunnerOptions.Iterations, RunResult.Iteration (1-based, omitempty),
  RunnerSnapshot.Iterations + CompletedIterations. Cap 200
  (runnerIterationLimit) because every request of every iteration becomes a
  persisted row in state.json - the cap is a file-size bound, not a guess.

  The story is one field; the RISK is entirely in the interaction with the
  controls that were already in the loop. An outer loop turns every existing
  `break` into a question - run-terminal, or iteration-local? - and the wrong
  answer is silent. Each one was classified deliberately:

    run-terminal (break iterations)
      cancellation (3 sites)          a cancelled run must not start iter 3
      bail on failure (2 sites)       same
      controls.StopExecution          bru.runner.stopExecution() means the run
    iteration-local (plain break)
      setNextRequest(null)            Postman/newman semantics: ends THIS
                                      iteration, next one starts

  `jumps` now resets per iteration: it guards an infinite setNextRequest cycle
  WITHIN one pass, and carrying it across iterations would abort a legitimate
  long run for looping that never happened.

  appendUnrunRunResults enumerates only the rest of the CURRENT iteration.
  Exploding every request of every remaining iteration into rows would bury the
  one real failure under thousands of placeholders; Iterations vs
  CompletedIterations is what reports the iterations that never started.

  BUG FOUND AND FIXED, introduced by US-047 and missed by its own tests:
  the snapshot tally's `default:` arm swept every unknown status into Failed,
  so "unrun" was counted as a failure. A bailed run of 5 requests reported
  Failed=5 for one real failure. US-047's tests asserted statuses, never
  tallies, so they were all green over it. "unrun" now has its own case that
  counts nowhere, and TestUnrunResultsAreNotCountedAsFailures pins it.

  Negative controls, all three run and all three caught:
    bail -> plain break     server saw 4 requests instead of 1; 8 rows not 2
    unrun case removed      Failed=5, want 1
    always stamp iteration  single-iteration rows stamped, shape changed

  Frontend: Iterations input (1-200) beside the delay field, an
  "Iterations k of n" summary chip, and an Iter column that appears only for
  multi-iteration runs.

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 36.9s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-046 - Runner data files. DONE.
  runner_data_file.go: CSV (header row -> keys) and JSON (array of objects),
  chosen by EXTENSION not by sniffing - a mislabelled file sniffed as the wrong
  format produces plausible garbage (a JSON array read as CSV yields one column
  named `[`), whereas an unknown extension is an error the user can act on.
  RunnerOptions.DataFile; SelectRunnerDataFile binding for the picker.

  New Data scope in scriptVariableContext, slotted between Request and Runtime:
    Global < Collection < Env < Folder < Request < DATA < Runtime < Prompt
  Data beats the environment (Postman's rule, and what makes the feature
  useful) but loses to Runtime and Prompt. The row is chosen BEFORE the
  iteration starts; bru.setVar and a prompt answer are deliberate acts DURING
  it. If the row won, a script could not correct a value for the request that
  follows and would fail with nothing to point at.

  Row count leads the iteration count, and asking for more iterations than rows
  is clamped DOWN rather than padded. An iteration with no row behind it does
  not fail - it sends {{userId}} unresolved straight to the server, which looks
  like a broken collection rather than a misconfigured run. Every clamp in the
  file exists to make that unrepresentable.

  Two parsing decisions that are only obvious after they bite:
    UTF-8 BOM stripped from the header. Excel writes one; without stripping,
    the first column is named "\ufeffuserId" and {{userId}} silently never
    resolves.
    JSON numbers via strconv.FormatFloat, not %v. %v renders 1000000 as
    "1e+06" - an id in scientific notation is a request to the wrong resource.
    The negative control confirmed this: it produced exactly "1e+06".

  Ragged and blank CSV rows accepted: a trailing comma or short final line is
  normal in hand-edited CSV, and missing cells become the empty string the
  header promised.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    Data dropped from Recompute   requests went out as
                                  /users/{{userId}}?label=%7B%7Blabel%7D%7D
    Data placed above Runtime     bru.setVar value overwritten by the row
    pad instead of clamp          10 iterations planned for 3 rows
    %v for JSON numbers           id became "1e+06"

  Frontend: Choose/Clear data-file control in RunnerPanel with the selected
  path shown. A cancelled dialog returns "" and deliberately leaves the current
  selection alone rather than clearing it.

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 36.6s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-039 - `pm` core. DONE.
  installPostmanScriptAPI puts a live `pm` beside `bru`: pm.test, pm.expect,
  pm.info (requestName, requestId, eventName, iteration, iterationCount).

  pm.test and pm.expect are BOUND TO the existing globals, not reimplemented.
  Two independent test registries would drift, and a pm.test whose failures did
  not reach the same TestResults slice would report a GREEN run while its
  assertions failed. Binding also means every later fix to test/expect applies
  to both surfaces for free. The tests are written against that failure mode
  rather than "does pm.test work".

  Two conversions at the boundary, both silent if wrong:
    pm.info.iteration is 0-BASED (Postman) while everything user-facing here
    counts from 1. A script copied out of Postman guards on
    `pm.info.iteration === 0` for first-iteration setup; leaking the 1-based
    value makes that guard never fire, with no error anywhere.
    eventName: both post-response phases report "test". Postman has no separate
    post-response event, and inventing one breaks the
    `pm.info.eventName === "test"` guard scripts actually write.

  Outside a collection run: iteration 0, iterationCount 1 - Postman reports a
  single iteration, not zero. runnerIteration{} zero value gives exactly that.

  The import-time postmanTranslateScript (US-044's subject) rewrites pm.* to
  bru.* and is called ONLY from the Postman importer, so the live pm object is
  genuinely exercised at run time. Verified before writing the tests.

  MY OWN TEST WAS WRONG AND I CAUGHT IT VIA THE NEGATIVE CONTROL. The first
  version asserted state.Runner.Passed == 3 to prove pm.info was right. NC2
  (leak the 1-based iteration) produced NO failure. The cause: a failed
  assertion did not mark a RunResult failed, so the tally could not see a
  broken pm.info at all - the check never ran, the same class of mistake as the
  "Send" button check earlier in this session. Rewritten to read the response's
  TestResults directly, after which NC2 fails with
  "expected 3 to equal 2".

  BUG FOUND AND FIXED, a real gap in US-047 as shipped: its criterion names
  "a failed assertion or transport error", but the runner derived status only
  from the transport and the HTTP code. A collection whose every assertion
  failed against 200 responses reported a fully green run, and BailOnFailure
  could never trigger on an assertion - the case the story leads with.
  firstFailedTestResult now promotes the first failed assertion to a failed
  RunResult with the assertion's name in the error.

  One existing test changed as a consequence:
  TestCancelCollectionRunDuringDelaySkipsNextTransport expected passed=1
  failed=0. Its fixture asserts status 200 against a 204 response, so that
  request was always genuinely failing and was being counted as passed. The
  test is about the CANCELLED count; expectation updated with a note.

  Negative controls, all run and all caught:
    pm.test with its own registry     0 test results instead of 2
    1-based pm.info.iteration         expected 3 to equal 2 (after the fix)
    eventName always "test"           pre-request phase mapping lost
    assertion failures ignored        passed=3 failed=0, want 2/1

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 36.7s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-040 - `pm` variable scopes. DONE.
  pm.environment, pm.collectionVariables, pm.globals and pm.variables, each
  with get/set/unset/has/clear/toObject/replaceIn.

  The story flags this as "the bug in the import table" and it is exactly that:
  postmanTranslateScript maps pm.environment.set, pm.collectionVariables.set,
  pm.globals.set AND pm.variables.set all onto bru.setVar, collapsing four
  scopes into the runtime scope. Nothing errors. A script that writes one scope
  and reads another gets its value back, so the collapse LOOKS like it works -
  until a second environment or collection is supposed to see a different value
  and does not. The negative control reproduced it precisely: three scopes all
  returned "global".

  Every scope method delegates to the bru function for that exact scope rather
  than reimplementing it. The dirty tracking that decides whether an
  environment is written back to disk lives in those closures; a parallel
  implementation that forgot EnvDirty would update variables that silently
  never persist. Delegation makes that unrepresentable, and makes drift between
  the two surfaces impossible.

  pm.variables is deliberately NOT built by the shared scope() helper: it READS
  the fully resolved chain across every scope but WRITES to the runtime scope.
  Private storage would make a value set through it invisible to {{var}}
  interpolation; routing its reads at one scope would silently miss variables
  defined anywhere else. An unset name returns undefined rather than "" so
  `typeof` guards behave as Postman scripts expect.

  Negative controls, both run and both caught:
    all scopes -> bru runtime scope   5 assertions fail, "expected global to
                                      equal environment"
    pm.variables reads Runtime only   "expected undefined to equal e"

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 37.4s 0,
  -race clean.

US-041 - `pm.request` / `pm.response`. DONE.
  Both built over the existing req/res objects by delegation, so a header added
  by req.setHeader in the pre-request script is visible through
  pm.request.headers.get - a snapshot taken at construction would report it
  missing.

  THE ASSERTION CHAIN IS THE RISK. pm.response.to.have.* must THROW on failure,
  because that is what the enclosing pm.test catches and records. An assertion
  that returned false instead would let
    pm.test("status is 200", () => pm.response.to.have.status(200))
  pass while the status was 500 - a green suite over a broken API, which is the
  exact failure this whole surface exists to prevent. Every assertion is
  therefore tested in BOTH directions: passes when true, produces a FAILED
  TestResult when false.

  to.be.ok / success / redirection / clientError / serverError / error are
  PROPERTIES in Postman, not calls, so they are DefineAccessorProperty getters.
  As plain values they would be no-ops that read as passing tests - the NC4
  negative control confirmed exactly that.

  One deliberate divergence from res, silent if wrong: in Postman
  pm.response.status is the status TEXT and pm.response.code is the NUMBER,
  while this codebase's res.status is the number. Inside pm.* Postman's meaning
  wins, or every copied script comparing pm.response.status to "OK" is quietly
  always false. res.status is untouched for bru scripts, and a test pins both.

  pm.response is ABSENT during the pre-request phase rather than exposing the
  zero Response. Answering pm.response.code with 0 and text() with "" reads as
  a server that returned nothing rather than as a script asking for something
  that does not exist yet; undefined makes the script throw where Postman
  throws.

  pm.response.json() throws on non-JSON rather than returning null: a test that
  asked for JSON and got HTML should fail loudly, not compare against null and
  pass.

  pm.request.url is an OBJECT with toString/getHost/getPath/getQueryString,
  matching Postman. A bare string would satisfy toString() by accident and then
  fail on every other member.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    assertions report, never throw   3+ false assertions reported as passed
    status as the number             "expected 200 to equal OK"
    pm.response during pre-request   "expected object to equal undefined"
    to.be.* as plain values          clientError on a 200 reported as passed

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 40.5s 0,
  -race clean.

US-042 - `pm` side effects. DONE.
  pm.sendRequest, pm.cookies, pm.execution.setNextRequest and
  pm.execution.skipRequest, all pure delegation to bru.

  Delegation is the point, not laziness. Each of these has machinery that is
  easy to overlook when reimplementing:
    bru.sendRequest    records a timeline entry for the scripted request and
                       enforces the recursion depth limit
    bru.cookies        is bound to THIS request's jar and URL
    bru.setNextRequest feeds the runner's control flow
  A parallel implementation would give requests missing from the timeline,
  cookies from the wrong host, and a setNextRequest the runner never sees -
  none of which fails visibly. Postman's
  pm.sendRequest(req, callback(err, response)) is already exactly
  bru.sendRequest's signature, so it is a straight alias, not an adapter.

  Tests assert JS object IDENTITY (pm.sendRequest === bru.sendRequest) AND
  behaviour, because identity alone is satisfied by two equally broken
  references. The setNextRequest test runs a real 3-request collection and
  demands the skipped request never appear in the results.

  Negative controls, both run and both caught:
    setNextRequest as a no-op    identity fails AND the skipped request ran
    pm.cookies as its own object cookie assertions quietly reported "absent"

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, -race clean.

US-043 - `pm.iterationData` / `pm.vault`. DONE.
  pm.iterationData reads the Data scope US-046 populates - the DATA SCOPE
  SPECIFICALLY, not the resolved chain. pm.iterationData means "what the data
  file said for this iteration"; answering from the merged chain would report
  environment and collection variables as iteration data, so a script guarding
  on pm.iterationData.has("userId") would take the data-driven branch on a run
  with NO data file at all. The negative control showed exactly that:
  toObject returned host,label,notFromData,userId instead of label,userId.

  toObject returns a COPY. It is a read in Postman, and handing out the live
  scope lets a script mutate the iteration's variables through it - confirmed
  by NC4, where a tampered snapshot changed the real value.

  pm.vault is ASYNC, returning real goja promises. Postman's vault API returns
  promises and scripts are written as `await pm.vault.get(...)`; a bare value
  would satisfy await by accident and then break on .then().

  pm.vault.set and .unset REJECT rather than writing, and this is a deliberate
  scope limit worth stating plainly. The runtime cannot mark a value as secret:
  scriptVariableContext holds plain maps with no Secret flag (Variable.Secret
  exists only on the persisted model). A pm.vault.set would therefore land the
  value in the environment scope as an ORDINARY variable and get written to
  disk in the clear, while the script believed it had vaulted a token. An
  explicit rejection naming "plain text" is the honest answer; silently
  downgrading a secret is not. A test asserts the value reaches no scope.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    vault.set resolves            rejection assertion failed
    iterationData reads the chain other scopes leaked into the row
    vault.get returns a value     "expected undefined to equal function"
    toObject returns the live map "expected tampered to equal 7"

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, -race clean.

US-044 - Fix + demote the import translator. DONE. Closes the pm cluster.

  FIX. Each variable scope now maps to ITS OWN bru function:
    pm.environment.*        -> bru.*EnvVar
    pm.collectionVariables.*-> bru.*CollectionVar
    pm.globals.*            -> bru.*GlobalEnvVar
  All four families previously pointed at bru.getVar/setVar/deleteVar,
  collapsing environment, collection, global and resolved-chain variables into
  the single runtime scope.

  pm.variables is now NOT translated at all. bru has no equivalent - bru.getVar
  reads only the runtime scope, whereas pm.variables reads the fully resolved
  chain - and translating it was HALF THE COLLAPSE. Left alone it runs on the
  native pm.variables from US-040, which is strictly more correct than any
  rewrite. That is the general rule the table follows now: translate only what
  maps EXACTLY, leave everything else to the real pm object.

  DEMOTE. Translation is opt-in via ImportPayload.TranslatePostmanScripts and
  CollectionImportApplyRequest.TranslatePostmanScripts, default false. Until
  US-039-043 there was no live pm object, so rewriting was the only way an
  imported script could run at all. Now pm.* is native and more faithful than
  any textual rewrite - distinct scopes, Postman status semantics, throwing
  where Postman throws.

  PLUMBING TRAP FOUND WHILE WIRING IT. Candidates are parsed during DETECTION,
  before the apply request (and therefore the flag) exists. Threading the flag
  only into collectionFromImport reaches the manual KindOverride branch ONLY,
  so every normally-detected Postman file would ignore the toggle - a checkbox
  that looks like it works and demonstrably does nothing. ApplyCollectionImport
  now re-imports Postman candidates when the flag is set. A re-import failure
  is deliberately non-fatal: the untranslated candidate is already valid, so
  falling back beats failing an import over an optional rewrite.

  Three existing tests opted in (they are ABOUT the translator).
  TestImportPostmanAndBruRoundTrip's "no pm. remains" assertion now exempts
  pm.variables, with the reasoning inline.

  Negative controls, all three run and all three caught:
    restore the scope collapse       three setters missing from the output
    translation on by default        pm.* rewritten away on a default import
    flag only in the override branch "the opt-in had no effect on a normally-
                                     detected file"

  Frontend: "Rewrite pm.* to bru.*" checkbox in the import footer, off by
  default, with a title explaining when to use it.

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.3s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-051 - HAR import. DONE.
  har_import.go + fixture at docs/qa/import-fixtures/session.har. Registered in
  detectCollectionImport by BOTH extension and shape, and in the manual
  override path (a separate branch that drops warnings if you forget it).

  A HAR is different in kind from every other importer here. Postman, Insomnia
  and OpenAPI files are AUTHORED - what is in them is what someone meant to put
  there. A HAR is whatever crossed the wire, which forces two decisions:

  CREDENTIALS. Session cookies and Authorization headers are recorded verbatim.
  They ARE imported, because stripping them silently produces a collection
  where every request 401s for no visible reason. But the importer WARNS and
  names the specific headers, because a collection is written to disk and this
  app can commit one to git. The warning list is a small explicit set, not a
  heuristic: a false positive trains people to ignore the warning.

  NOISE. A recorded session holds the same polling request many times. Exact
  duplicates are dropped - an exact duplicate carries no information the first
  copy does not. The fingerprint covers method, URL, params, headers AND body.
  Deduping on method+URL, the obvious implementation, collapses a login POST
  and a logout POST to the same endpoint, or two writes with different bodies.
  The negative control proved it: the fixture's two POSTs to /v1/users (Ada and
  Grace) collapsed to one, losing a request outright.

  Recording artefacts stripped: HTTP/2 pseudo-headers (:method) are not valid
  to send, and a recorded content-length that no longer matches an edited body
  makes the server read a truncated payload - a failure that reads as a server
  bug.

  Query strings are split into the params table rather than frozen into the URL
  text, and parsed by hand rather than with url.ParseQuery, which returns a map
  and so loses order AND repeated keys. Repeated keys are ordinary in recorded
  traffic and losing one changes what the request asks for.

  MY OWN TEST WAS WRONG FIRST: I asserted 3 surviving items when the fixture
  has 5 entries with exactly ONE byte-identical repeat, so 4 survive. The code
  was right; I miscounted. Fixed the expectation and the stale comments that
  said "two exact repeats".

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    dedupe on method+URL only    3 items, the Grace POST lost
    credential warning removed   warning text no longer names the headers
    keep recording artefacts     :method, content-length and host survived
    shape detection removed      a HAR named .json reported "ambiguous"

  Frontend: HAR added to the manual format override list.

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.7s 0,
  svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-052 - Swagger 2 import. DONE.
  swagger2_import.go converts Swagger 2 -> OpenAPI 3 and hands the result to
  the existing OpenAPI importer, per the story. Converting rather than teaching
  the importer a second dialect is right: the OpenAPI 3 path already handles
  servers, parameters, request bodies, security schemes and $ref resolution,
  and a parallel implementation would reimplement all of it and then drift.
  Fixture at docs/qa/import-fixtures/swagger2.json.

  The conversions that change what request gets built - as opposed to what
  merely satisfies a validator - and what each looks like when omitted:

    host+basePath+schemes -> servers[]
      Without it the collection imports PERFECTLY and every request 404s. The
      importer parameterises the server as a {{baseUrl}} collection variable,
      so that is where the value has to land.
    in: body param -> requestBody
      Left in place it has no OpenAPI 3 meaning; the payload is simply lost.
      NC3 showed the imported request with mode="none".
    in: formData -> requestBody
      type: file became type: string + format: binary. Unconverted, a file is
      encoded as a urlencoded form value - a corrupted upload.
    type/format/default -> parameter.schema
      OpenAPI 3 moved them; left inline they are ignored and the parameter
      imports with no example value.
    type: basic -> type: http + scheme: basic
      Unconverted, the importer reads NO auth, so the request imports with auth
      mode none and 401s pointing nowhere.
    oauth2 flow -> flows{}, accessCode -> authorizationCode.

  The $ref rewrite is recursive over the WHOLE document and runs LAST, after
  operation conversion, so pointers created during conversion are rewritten
  too. Recursive rather than targeted at known locations because a $ref appears
  anywhere a schema can - inside allOf, inside array items, inside a nested
  property. A missed pointer resolves to nothing and the schema comes back
  EMPTY, which reads as an API with no documented payload rather than a broken
  conversion. The fixture deliberately nests one (User.manager -> User) and
  puts one in array items.

  isSwagger2Document checks the VERSION VALUE, not key presence: Swagger 1.x
  also has the key, and converting one as 2.0 gives a plausible document with
  the wrong shape instead of a clear rejection.

  Path-level body/formData parameters are legal Swagger 2 with no OpenAPI 3
  equivalent at that level; they are promoted onto every operation under the
  path rather than dropped, and an operation's own body wins.

  MY OWN TEST WAS WRONG FIRST: I asserted the host appeared in the item URL.
  The importer correctly parameterises it as {{baseUrl}}. Assertion moved to
  the collection variable, which is the real contract.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    no servers built        baseUrl stayed "{{baseUrl}}"
    top-level-only $ref     nested pointer still #/definitions/User
    body left a parameter   imported request mode="none"
    file not made binary    file field type stayed "file"
    basic auth unconverted  type stayed "basic", no scheme

  Frontend: Swagger 2 added to the manual format override list.
