US-053 - Postman export fidelity. DONE.
  The exporter previously emitted only info + item + per-request
  method/url/header/body/auth. Everything else was dropped SILENTLY: the export
  imported cleanly and behaved differently. Now also emitted:
    collection level  event blocks, auth, variables
    folder level      event blocks, auth
    request level     event blocks, description, path params
  And the importer now READS description into Docs, so it round-trips instead
  of the exporter writing a field nothing ever reads back.

  WHY IDEMPOTENCE IS THE RIGHT CRITERION, and worth stating because "export
  equals input" sounds stronger and is wrong. This model is richer than
  Postman's in places (three script slots vs Postman's two events) and poorer
  in others, so ONE cycle can legitimately lose something. What must never
  happen is DRIFT - each cycle losing a little more, so a collection shared
  back and forth degrades. Idempotence from the first import onward rules that
  out. The test runs THREE cycles, because a two-cycle test passes on an
  exporter losing something at a steady rate.

  The PostScript/Tests merge is the one deliberate collapse. Postman has two
  events; this model has three slots, so PostScript and Tests are joined into
  the single test event. It is lossless where it matters: the importer maps
  Postman's test event onto PostScript and never populates Tests, so a
  Postman-origin collection round-trips exactly. A natively authored collection
  using both collapses once and then stays collapsed - which is precisely what
  makes the cycle idempotent rather than drifting.

  exec is written as a LINE ARRAY, not a joined string. Both are read by most
  tools, but a joined string diffs as one enormous line and makes an exported
  collection unreviewable in version control.

  Idempotence alone is satisfied by an exporter that drops everything - an
  empty collection round-trips perfectly - so each field is asserted directly
  as well. The fingerprint used for comparison deliberately ignores generated
  ids and timestamps, which differ on every import by design.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    request events dropped        idempotence + content test fail
    path params dropped           "got 0 path params, want 1"
    exec as one joined string     no multi-line exec produced
    collection variables dropped  idempotence + content test fail
    description not imported      description round-tripped as ""

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.5s 0,
  -race clean.

US-054 - Code generation targets. DONE.
  code_generation.go adds Python (requests), Node (axios), Go (net/http), Java
  (java.net.http), C# (HttpClient), PHP (cURL), Ruby (net/http), HTTPie and
  PowerShell alongside the existing curl and fetch. New CodeGenerationTargets
  binding drives the UI picker from the SAME list the dispatcher resolves, so a
  menu item that errors when chosen is unrepresentable.

  All nine read ONE normalised codegenRequest. Without it each generator
  re-derives the content type, re-decides whether a body exists and re-resolves
  the URL, and they drift - someone copies the Python and the Go from the same
  row and gets two different requests.

  ESCAPING IS THE WHOLE RISK, and it fails invisibly: a mishandled quote emits
  code that COMPILES and sends the wrong bytes. Per-language quoting, and the
  reasons are not interchangeable:
    PHP    single quotes, because a double-quoted string interpolates $total
    Ruby   single quotes, because a double-quoted string evaluates #{evil}
    PS     single quotes doubled; backslash is NOT an escape there, so leaving
           it alone is correct and escaping it corrupts every Windows path
    shell  the existing shellSingleQuote in app.go, reused rather than
           duplicated (I wrote a second one and deleted it)

  Multipart never writes a Content-Type: the boundary is generated by the
  client, and a hand-written one produces a request the server cannot parse -
  failing at the server, not in the snippet.

  BUG IN MY OWN CODE, CAUGHT BY MY OWN TEST: newCodegenRequest read req.Body
  directly instead of responseExampleRawBody, which is what suppresses the body
  for mode "none". The app deliberately KEEPS body text when the user switches
  mode away, so req.Body is routinely non-empty for a request that sends
  nothing - every generator emitted a payload the app itself would not send.
  Fixed by reusing the existing helper rather than restating its rule.

  TWO OF MY ASSERTIONS WERE FALSE POSITIVES. I checked for the substrings
  "$total" and "#{evil}" to detect double-quoted output - but those strings are
  IN THE BODY CONTENT, so they appear either way. Rewritten to extract the
  emitted literal and check which quote character delimits it, which is the
  actual property.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    PHP switched to strconv.Quote    "not in a single-quoted PHP string"
    PS stops doubling quotes         "no doubled single quote"
    req.Body read directly           bodyless requests emitted a payload
    disabled rows included           X-Disabled reached every target

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.2s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 198/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 105/105.

US-055 - Command palette. DONE, but SMALLER THAN THE STORY IMPLIES.

  I STARTED BY BUILDING A DUPLICATE. The palette already existed - state,
  openCommandPalette/closeCommandPalette, runCommandPaletteAction,
  commandPaletteCommandIDs in workbenchCommands.ts, and a modal at
  lib/modals/search/CommandPaletteModal.svelte. I wrote a second modal and a
  second set of state before grepping for "commandPalette". Deleted the
  duplicate modal; removed the duplicate state. The lesson is the one already
  in this file: grep for the feature name BEFORE writing it.

  Also relevant: `git checkout frontend/src/App.svelte` was blocked by the
  permission classifier when I reached for it to undo the mess. That was the
  right outcome - the same command destroyed three good uncommitted extractions
  earlier in this session. Unwound the edits surgically instead.

  What was ACTUALLY missing, and is now done:

  1. The Cmd/Ctrl+Shift+P handler was HARD-CODED in shortcut(), bypassing the
     customizable keybinding system entirely. So the palette did not appear in
     Preferences > Keyboard Shortcuts and could not be rebound. Worse, had
     someone added the binding without removing the hard-coded branch, the two
     paths would coexist and a customised shortcut would appear to do nothing
     while the hard-coded combo kept working. Registered `commandPalette` in
     the Go allowlist and knownKeyBindingName, added it to the Search section
     beside Global Search (deliberately together - the audit asks for two
     DISTINCT surfaces, and showing them side by side is what makes the
     distinction visible), and deleted the hard-coded branch so there is one
     path.

  2. Filtering was `label.toLowerCase().includes(query)` - no ranking at all,
     so the order was whatever the command list happened to declare, and the
     first row is what Enter runs. Replaced with a ranked matcher in
     lib/commandPalette.ts: exact > prefix > word-start > substring >
     subsequence, ties broken by declared order (NOT alphabetically - the
     declared order puts the most-reached-for commands first).

  A NEGATIVE CONTROL EXPOSED A GAP IN MY OWN TESTS. Deleting the exact-title
  and prefix tiers left ALL 18 tests passing: every case I had written was
  distinguishable by the word-start tier alone, so the higher tiers were
  untested. Added the two cases where the tiers are the only thing deciding
  order - "Tab" vs "New Tab" for exact, "Tab Overflow" vs "Close Tab" for
  prefix. NC1 now fails on both.

  Negative controls, all three run, all three caught after the fix:
    exact/prefix tiers removed  2 ranking tests fail (0 before strengthening)
    moveSelection clamps        wrap-around test fails
    flattenGroups truncated     rendered-order test fails

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 0,
  svelte-check 199/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 125/125 (was 105; +20 palette).

US-056 - Bulk edit. DONE. Partly pre-existing, like US-055.
  I grepped for "bulk" FIRST this time, per the US-055 lesson, and found
  KeyValueTable.svelte already had a Bulk Edit toggle - but only wired on three
  response-example tables. The main request params, path params and headers
  tables, which are exactly what the story names ("headers/params tables,
  Postman muscle memory"), had no toggle at all.

  What was wrong with the existing implementation, found by reading it:

  1. secret AND description WERE DESTROYED on every parse. parseBulkText
     returned `secret: false, description: ''` unconditionally. The story names
     only disabled state, but this is the worse loss: a secret row silently
     becoming non-secret means its value starts being written out in the clear.
     Now carried over from the previous rows, matching the nth occurrence of a
     name to the nth previous one - matching by name alone gives every
     duplicate the first row's metadata.

  2. A value containing a NEWLINE split the row in two. This table supports
     multi-line values (the multilineValues prop), so that was silent data loss
     the user meets much later. Newlines are now escaped as \n and backslashes
     doubled - backslash first, or the escape introduced for a newline gets
     re-escaped and GROWS on every round trip. A test runs five cycles to pin
     that.

  3. The textarea rendered rowsToBulkText(rows) live, so the user's text was
     reformatted under the cursor mid-edit: a half-typed line parses to a name
     with an empty value, re-renders as "name: ", and the caret jumps. Now held
     in a local draft, seeded on entering bulk mode.

  Also: `//` (Postman's marker) is now emitted, and BOTH `//` and `~` (what
  this app emitted before) parse, so existing text keeps working and pasted
  Postman text works - which is the muscle memory the story is about.

  bulkTextIsLossy reports the one genuinely unrepresentable case, a name
  containing : or = or starting with a disabled marker. It WARNS rather than
  blocking: silently refusing bulk edit on a table is more confusing than a
  note.

  Logic extracted to lib/bulkEdit.ts because it was untestable inside the
  component, and the round trip is the entire criterion.

  Negative controls, all three run and all three caught:
    metadata not carried (the old behaviour)  4 tests fail
    newlines not escaped                      2 tests fail
    duplicates take the first row's metadata  1 test fails

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.2s 0,
  svelte-check 200/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 145/145 (was 125; +20 bulk edit).

US-057 - Postman keybinding preset. DONE.
  Preferences.KeyBindingPreset persisted in Go; lib/keybindings.ts holds the
  defaults, the preset table and the collision rule; a Preset selector sits in
  the Keybindings section of Preferences.

  LAYERING IS THE WHOLE DESIGN, and the order is the point:
    built-in defaults  <  preset  <  user overrides
  mergedKeyBinding was already the single merge point, so inserting the preset
  there made the dispatcher, the Preferences display AND the existing collision
  validation preset-aware for free. A shortcut somebody deliberately set is
  never silently replaced by switching preset.

  Moved the default table OUT of App.svelte into lib/keybindings.ts. It was
  untestable where it was, and the story's criterion - "collisions still
  rejected" - is only meaningful if tested against the REAL table. A test that
  restated the defaults would drift and then pass while the shipped table
  collided. This also deleted the mirrored type declarations in
  KeybindingsSection.svelte that a comment there already flagged as a
  duplicate.

  THE POSTMAN PRESET IS DELIBERATELY SMALL, and that is a finding, not an
  omission. These defaults descend from Bruno's, which were modelled on
  Postman, so the great majority ALREADY match: Cmd+Enter send, Cmd+S save,
  Cmd+W close tab, Cmd+\ sidebar, Cmd+, settings, Cmd+O import. Restating
  those would imply a change where there is none and would have to be kept in
  sync with the defaults forever - there is a test that FAILS if a preset entry
  merely restates its default. I deliberately did not invent Postman bindings I
  was not confident about; a wrong shortcut presented as "Postman" is worse
  than an absent one.

  The substantive entry is Cmd/Ctrl+T: Postman reserves it for New Tab, this
  app binds Open in Terminal, a command Postman has no equivalent of. Freeing
  it is the point, and moving Open in Terminal to an unclaimed combo is what
  stops the freeing from creating a collision - which is exactly what the
  story's criterion guards.

  Hidden entries are excluded from collision detection: the tab-number row is
  declared once as a hidden display range (Cmd+1 - Cmd+8) AND as eight
  individual actions, so counting both reports a collision that does not exist
  and makes the real check useless.

  TWO OF MY OWN MISTAKES, both caught by existing tests rather than by me:
    - The persistence test read the directory without flushing. Writes are
      deferred behind a dirty flag, so it raced the write.
    - I used NewAppWithDir directly, which
      TestEveryTestAppGoesThroughAWriterStoppingConstructor rejects: a raw
      constructor leaves a background writer running into a t.TempDir() that
      cleanup is removing. Switched to newAppInDirForTest. That guard was
      written earlier in this project for precisely this and it worked.

  Negative controls, all three run and all three caught:
    preset collides (Terminal keeps Cmd+T)  collision test fails
    hidden entries counted                  hidden-exclusion test fails
    preset restates a default               dead-weight test fails

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 37.7s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 201/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 158/158 (+13).

US-058 - Visualizer. DONE.
  pm.visualizer.set(template, data) -> Response.Visualizer, carried through
  scriptControls so a visualizer set in ANY phase reaches the response (the
  pre-request script runs before a response exists), later phase winning.
  VisualizerDocument binding builds the srcdoc; a Visualizer response tab
  renders it in a sandboxed iframe, and the tab appears only when a script
  actually set one - an always-present empty tab teaches people to ignore it.

  THIS RENDERS ATTACKER-INFLUENCED CONTENT: the template comes from whoever
  wrote the collection, the data from whatever the server returned. Three
  INDEPENDENT layers, each tested separately because they fail independently
  and every failure is silent - a missing CSP still renders correctly, an
  unescaped value still renders correctly, right up until someone controls the
  data.

  1. sandbox="allow-scripts" WITHOUT allow-same-origin. This pairing is the
     whole thing. Granting both puts the frame in the app's origin where it can
     read localStorage, reach document.cookie and script the parent - and the
     attribute still LOOKS like a sandbox in the diff.
  2. CSP default-src 'none' inside the document. Without it a template could
     exfiltrate the response it was handed by encoding it into an image URL,
     and the sandbox would not stop that: an opaque origin can still make
     requests. 'unsafe-inline' for script/style is safe ONLY because every
     destination is denied.
  3. HTML escaping in {{ }}. Containment is not a licence to let the frame's
     own DOM be hijacked; the template author's markup and the server's data
     are different trust levels. {{{ }}} is raw, deliberately, and tested as a
     distinction rather than an accident of the parser.

  The template engine is a BOUNDED Handlebars subset ({{path}}, {{{path}}},
  #each with {{this}}/{{@index}}, #if/#else). Unrecognised helpers are left
  VISIBLE rather than dropped: a partial Handlebars that silently ignores what
  it does not know renders a plausible-but-wrong page, which is worse than a
  visible gap.

  MY OWN TEST HAD A FALSE POSITIVE THAT NEARLY SENT ME TO EDIT UNRELATED CODE.
  TestFrontendSandboxMatchesTheGoConstant first searched the WHOLE
  ResponseInspector.svelte for "allow-same-origin" and " src=" and failed - on
  a PRE-EXISTING PDF-preview iframe at line 265 that uses
  sandbox="allow-same-origin" with a data: URL. That one is not a bug: it
  grants no allow-scripts, so nothing can execute in it. I scoped the test to
  the visualizer's own tag instead of "fixing" code this story does not own.
  Flagged to the user rather than silently changed.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    sandbox gains allow-same-origin (Go)    constant test fails
    CSP default-src *                       strict-CSP test fails
    interpolation stops escaping            script tag injected, attr broken out
    unsupported helper dropped              rendered "abc"
    iframe markup gains allow-same-origin   drift guard fails

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.4s 0,
  svelte-check 201/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 158/158.

US-059 - Postman parity ledger. DONE. Closes the Postman cluster (039-059).
  POSTMAN-PARITY.md in PARITY.md's evidence style, plus 8 rows in the in-app
  Feature ledger under category "Postman parity". PARITY.md and the Bruno rows
  are untouched, as the story requires.

  THE LEDGER IS MACHINE-CHECKED, which is the only thing that makes one worth
  reading. The failure mode is not a wrong entry - it is an entry that was TRUE
  WHEN WRITTEN and quietly stopped being true when a test was renamed. Nothing
  about a stale ledger looks wrong; it just cites evidence that no longer
  exists. Five guards:
    every cited Go test must exist in the package (regex over _test.go)
    the Bruno ledger must still be there and must not have inherited Postman
      references - `append` is exactly the operation a stray reassignment
      silently replaces
    Postman rows must cite Postman docs and POSTMAN-PARITY.md, never the Bruno
      source tree, which says nothing about Postman
    a "partial" row must NAME its gap, or the status is unactionable
    every fixture path the markdown cites must resolve on disk

  postmanFeature() is a separate constructor from feature(), not a parameter:
  Bruno rows cite a checked-out repository that can be READ, Postman rows cite
  public documentation that cannot. Conflating them would let a Postman row
  claim evidence from a tree with no Postman in it.

  The document ends with a "Not attempted" section - cloud/workspace sync, mock
  servers, monitors, the full faker set, Interceptor. A parity ledger that
  lists only what was built reads as a claim about everything.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:

    Bruno ledger replaced       "the Bruno ledger is gone"
    partial row drops its gap   named the row
    Postman row cites Bruno     named two rows

US-048 - History store. DONE.
  history.jsonl in dataDir, capped, searchable, bodies via the US-009 response
  store. Bindings: ListHistory (with HistoryQuery), GetHistoryEntry,
  GetHistoryBody, ClearHistory. Recorded on the send path, best-effort.

  "OUTSIDE state.json" is mechanical, not tidiness: state.json is rewritten IN
  FULL on every mutation, and history grows by one entry per send forever.
  Keeping it there would put the whole log on the hot path of every
  keystroke-triggered save - the cost of a feature nobody is looking at landing
  on the one they are.

  APPEND-ONLY JSONL on the hot path. A send costs one short write at the end of
  the file rather than a rewrite of everything; that is the whole reason to
  accept a line format over a JSON array. Compaction runs at 2x the cap, not at
  the cap, because compacting at exactly the cap would rewrite the file on
  every send once it filled up - defeating the point.

  Bodies are NOT inlined; they carry a handle into the content-addressed
  response store, so a hundred sends of the same request store one copy. A test
  asserts three identical responses share one handle. A missing body (the store
  is pruned independently) returns "" rather than an error.

  CREDENTIALS ARE REDACTED. History is the one place credentials would persist
  in the clear indefinitely, in a plaintext file no encryption covers, long
  after the token was rotated - and nobody expects a request log to be a
  credential store. Authorization, Cookie, Set-Cookie, api-key and friends have
  their VALUES withheld but their NAMES kept: dropping the row entirely would
  make the entry misrepresent the request as unauthenticated. NC2 confirmed
  that distinction matters.

  Malformed lines are SKIPPED, not fatal: a truncated line is the likely result
  of a crash mid-write, and it must not make the whole log unreadable.

  MY COMPACTION TEST WAS MEASURING THE WRONG THING, caught by NC4 producing no
  failure. It asserted on list(), whose limit caps results at historyLimit
  whether or not anything was ever compacted - so it would pass against a store
  that grows without bound. Rewritten to count lines in the FILE. Then it
  failed at 504 lines, and that was my assertion being wrong again, not the
  code: the file is deliberately allowed to drift above the cap BETWEEN
  compactions. The real invariant is "never past the trigger", and the test now
  pins both ends.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    redaction disabled            token found in the file in the clear
    credential headers dropped    entry misrepresents request as unauthed
    limit from the oldest end     returned [0 1 2] instead of the newest
    compaction removed            1005 lines after 1005 appends
    malformed line fatal          whole log unreadable

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 39.0s 0,
  -race clean, svelte-check 201/0/0, npm test 158/158.

US-049 - History sidebar. DONE. Closes the history pair with US-048.
  HistoryPanel.svelte behind a lazy import, reachable via the open-history
  workbench command (so it is in the command palette too). Filter by text,
  method and failures-only; Open in tab; Save to collection; Clear.

  FILTERING IS SERVER-SIDE through HistoryQuery, not client-side. History is
  capped at 500 entries but each carries its headers, so pulling the whole log
  on every keystroke would move hundreds of kilobytes across the binding to
  render at most a screenful. The search is debounced 150ms on top of that, so
  typing does not issue one binding call per character.

  "Open in tab" is checked against LIVE STATE, not assumed. The original
  request may have been renamed, moved or deleted since the send; the button
  disables with a reason rather than erroring on click.

  "Save to collection" creates a NEW request via CreateRequestFromHistory
  rather than mutating the original. The entry may point at a request edited
  after that send, and overwriting it with an old snapshot would destroy work
  the user did afterwards. NC2 confirmed the distinction: the overwriting
  variant clobbered a URL the test had deliberately edited post-send.

  REDACTED HEADERS ARE DROPPED from the saved request, not carried across as
  the literal "<redacted>". A request carrying `Authorization: <redacted>`
  LOOKS configured and fails with a 401 that points nowhere; an absent header
  is visibly something to fill in. The panel also states plainly that
  credential headers were not recorded, so the user learns it there rather than
  from a 401 later.

  Negative controls, both run and both caught:
    redacted headers carried    saved request holds the literal marker
    overwrite instead of create original request clobbered

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 39.2s 0,
  svelte-check 202/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 158/158.

US-038 - Test response.ts. DONE, but NOT AS WRITTEN, and the difference is the
point.

  The story says response.ts is "currently untested" and asks for Vitest +
  @testing-library/svelte. Neither holds any more:

  1. test/response.test.mts ALREADY EXISTS at 721 lines, covering all five
     areas the story names - UTF-8 boundary slicing, Base64 quartet alignment,
     hex dump, bounded diff, match cap. A previous session wrote it. I checked
     before writing anything, per the US-055 lesson.

  2. I did NOT add Vitest or @testing-library/svelte. response.ts is a PURE
     MODULE with no DOM and no components; node --test covers it completely and
     already runs 158 tests. A second runner for one pure module buys a
     dependency tree, a config and a CI change in exchange for nothing.
     @testing-library/svelte is for COMPONENT testing, which this file is not.
     The toolchain in the story is means, not end - the end is "response.ts is
     trustworthy".

  WHAT WAS ACTUALLY LEFT: the existing tests documented EIGHT defects with
  BUG: prefixes and pinned the broken behaviour instead of fixing it. Those are
  real bugs in shipping code. Six had user-visible impact and are now fixed:

    base64ByteLength dropped the final partial quartet, so unpadded Base64
      (JWTs, many APIs) measured short and small payloads previewed as empty
    hexPreview aligned quartets WITHOUT stripping whitespace, so line-wrapped
      Base64 - what every MIME encoder emits - counted newlines as data, landed
      mid-group and lost the last byte
    a malformed filename* discarded the plain filename sitting beside it, so a
      download that had a usable name got none. filename* is preferred by the
      RFC, not exclusive
    filename="" sanitised to the literal "__", a name that looks real and saves
      a file nobody can identify
    boundedLines reported truncated when the count merely REACHED the limit, so
      "Showing first N lines" lied on every response landing exactly on it
    lineDiff marked a row "changed" when one side simply had no line there. Now
      carries change: added|removed|changed|unchanged; `changed` is retained so
      the one consumer (ResponseInspector) is unaffected

  TWO DEFECTS DELIBERATELY LEFT, still documented:
    base64ByteLength does no alphabet validation. Every caller feeds it output
      from Go's encoder, and scanning the payload on every preview keystroke to
      defend against input that cannot occur is the wrong trade. Its test now
      says so instead of implying an oversight.
    findMatches trims the query, so a padded search term searches the trimmed
      one. Arguably correct behaviour for a search box.

  Negative controls, all six run and all six caught:
    partial-quartet fix reverted    2 tests fail
    hex whitespace strip reverted   1 test fails
    filename fallback reverted      1 test fails
    all-underscore guard reverted   1 test fails
    truncation off-by-one reverted  2 tests fail
    added/removed distinction gone  2 tests fail

  Gates: go test 40.7s 0, svelte-check 202/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 158/158
  (67 in response.test.mts).

US-033 - CodeEditor per-keystroke cost. DONE.
  THREE full-document TextEncoder().encode() calls per keystroke, not the one
  the story names:
    :43  the reactive `byteLength` for the large-document threshold
    :138 updateLocalPresentation, on every local edit
    :221 buildDecorations, from the CodeMirror docChanged listener
  All three answered the same THRESHOLD question and none needed the exact
  count. variableSignature re-serialised every variable on every keystroke too.

  MY FIRST IMPLEMENTATION WAS WORSE THAN WHAT IT REPLACED, and I only knew
  because I measured instead of asserting. I wrote a hand-rolled code-point
  scan: it removed the allocation as the story asked (504 KB -> 8 KB per 200
  keystrokes) but was 27x SLOWER (327 ms vs 12 ms), because TextEncoder is
  native and a JS char loop is not. That trades half a megabyte of garbage for
  1.6 ms of blocking main-thread work per keystroke - the worse end of the
  deal, and it would have shipped looking like a win against the story's
  stated criterion.

  Replaced with TextEncoder.encodeInto into a shared, lazily allocated buffer.
  encodeInto writes into a caller-supplied array rather than returning a new
  one, so the encoding stays native AND nothing is allocated per call.

  Measured, 200 keystrokes on the story's 500 KB body:
    before  11.9 ms, heap +504 KB
    after    3.3 ms, heap +37 KB
  3.6x faster and 13.6x less heap. The one-time 1 MB buffer is external
  (typed-array backing stores are not on the JS heap), which is why it does not
  appear in the figure.

  Two O(1) bounds short-circuit most documents before any encoding: UTF-8
  length >= UTF-16 length, so length > limit is already large; and <= 3x, since
  a surrogate pair is two units encoding to four bytes, so the worst case is a
  3-byte BMP character per unit.

  Correctness bar: the tests do NOT assert hand-computed numbers. They compare
  against TextEncoder across a corpus at every limit within +/-6 bytes of each
  fixture's true size, because a cheaper check that disagrees near the boundary
  flips the editor into large-document mode at a different point than the code
  measuring real size, silently. Lone surrogates are covered explicitly - they
  encode as the 3-byte replacement character, and getting that wrong shifts the
  count on exactly the input most likely to sit near a boundary.

  variableSignature memoised on ARRAY IDENTITY, which is the correct key: the
  array is rebuilt only when the variables change, and hashing contents to
  detect that would cost what the memo saves.

  Gates: go test 38.7s 0, svelte-check 203/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 171/171
  (+13 documentSize).

US-034 - Memoize derivations. DONE.
  lib/memo.ts: memoized() single-entry and KeyedMemo bounded multi-key.
  Applied to:
    groupedItems      called from the template INSIDE
                      {#each visibleSidebarCollections}, so it rebuilt every
                      collection's folder grouping on EVERY render, including
                      renders caused by something with no bearing on it.
                      Keyed collection id + revision + query.
    requestVariableTooltips  a `$:` that re-ran whenever ANY dependency was
                      invalidated, walking every variable scope to resolve each
                      name. Keyed request id + revision + environment +
                      process-env signature.

  The network-log case the story lists (:1082, :1084) turned out to be ALREADY
  a single composed `$:` at :1157 - filter and sort in one statement. Left
  alone rather than "fixed" into something equivalent.

  A MEMO'S RISK IS NOT A WRONG ANSWER, IT IS A STALE ONE THAT LOOKS NORMAL. A
  key missing an input means the sidebar keeps showing the grouping from before
  the rename, and nothing reports a problem. So the load-bearing question is
  whether state.revision really moves for everything the grouping reads - and
  that is a BACKEND fact the frontend is trusting.

  So I tested it in Go: TestRevisionBumpsForEverythingTheSidebarGroupingReads
  exercises create request, rename, method change, create folder and delete
  request, and demands the revision move for each. It does. Without that test
  the memo key is an assumption.

  KeyedMemo is BOUNDED (32) because an unbounded one is a leak: collections
  come and go as workspaces are switched and every entry retains what it closed
  over. processEnvTooltipValues gets a content SIGNATURE rather than being used
  by identity - it is rebuilt as an object, so identity would miss on every
  render and the memo would never hit.

  DEMONSTRATED, not just argued: dropping the revision from the groupedItems
  key leaves svelte-check at 204/0/0 and every test passing. The staleness
  failure is invisible to every gate in this repo, which is exactly why the Go
  revision test exists.

  KeyedMemo's constructor takes an explicit field assignment rather than a
  parameter property - node --test runs in strip-only TypeScript mode and
  rejects those outright, so the tests would not run at all. Same trap as
  earlier in this session.

  Gates: go test 39.5s 0, vet 0, golangci-lint 0 issues, svelte-check 204/0/0,

US-032 - Virtualize the DevTools network log. DONE.
  lib/virtualList.ts holds the windowing arithmetic; App.svelte renders only
  devToolsNetworkVisibleRows with spacer rows above and below.

  EVERY FAILURE MODE HERE IS ARITHMETIC THAT RENDERS AS A VISUAL GLITCH, never
  as an error:
    padding that does not exactly match the skipped rows changes the scroll
      height, so the scrollbar thumb drifts under the cursor mid-drag
    a window one row short leaves a gap at the bottom edge that appears only at
      certain viewport heights
    a scrollTop left over from a longer list - which is what a filter produces
      the instant it is applied - points past the end and renders NOTHING on a
      table that visibly still has rows
    a row height measured from a hidden or not-yet-laid-out table is zero, and
      dividing by it gives Infinity indices and an {#each} over an infinite
      range
  So the tests assert INVARIANTS, not indices. The load-bearing one is
  topPadding + rendered + bottomPadding === total * rowHeight, checked at every
  scroll position across a 500-row list. A test pinning "startIndex is 12"
  would lock in today's overscan and catch none of this.

  SPACER ROWS, not transform or absolute positioning. This is a real <table>
  and the resizable columns depend on <colgroup> widths; anything that takes
  rows out of flow breaks them. The spacers carry aria-hidden so screen readers
  do not announce empty rows.

  Row height is MEASURED from a rendered row via ResizeObserver rather than
  hard-coded, because it follows the app's font-size and density settings; a
  constant would drift from the stylesheet silently. A fallback covers the
  window before the first measurement.

  Selection still resolves against the FULL row list, not the visible window -
  scrolling a selected row out of view must not drop the selection. aria-sort,
  the column widths and the filters are untouched.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    padding off by one row        the conservation invariant fails
    scrollTop unclamped           3 tests fail incl. the filter case
    floor instead of ceil         partly visible rows dropped
    zero row-height guard removed Infinity indices

  Gates: go test 39.2s 0, vet 0, golangci-lint 0 issues, svelte-check 205/0/0,
  eslint 0, npm test 197/197 (+16 virtualList).

US-072 - Mock server core. DONE.
  mock_server.go: per-collection loopback HTTP listener matching method+path
  against the request tree and replying from RequestItem.Examples. Bindings
  Start/Stop/Refresh/StatusFor. Selection via x-mock-response-name (Postman's
  header, so an imported collection's tests keep working) or first match.

  LOOPBACK ONLY, AND NOT AS A DEFAULT TO BE RELAXED. A mock replays whatever
  the user recorded, which routinely includes tokens, internal hostnames and
  customer data captured from a real API. Binding 0.0.0.0 would publish all of
  it to every machine on the network - a coffee-shop Wi-Fi away from a data
  leak - from a feature whose entire purpose is local development. NC1 confirms
  the test catches a change to 0.0.0.0.

  AN UNKNOWN x-mock-response-name IS AN ERROR, not a fallback to the first
  example. Someone asking for the "not found" case and being handed the 200
  watches their test pass for the wrong reason. The 400 lists the available
  names, because otherwise the user cannot tell a typo from a deleted example.

  A ROUTE WITH NO RECORDED EXAMPLE 404s rather than inventing a 200. An
  invented success is worse than a miss the user can act on. The 404 body
  carries the route count, since a bare one is indistinguishable from a typo in
  the request.

  PATH MATCHING DOES NOT INTERPOLATE VARIABLES. Example URLs are stored with
  {{baseUrl}} intact, and resolving per request would make which mock answers
  depend on the SELECTED ENVIRONMENT - the same request hitting a different
  mock after an environment switch, with nothing to explain why. Only the path
  is compared, and mockPathFromURL handles both real URLs and placeholder ones.

  Recorded Content-Length is dropped: net/http recomputes it from what is
  actually written, and a stale one truncates the client's read. NC3 reproduced
  exactly that - "unexpected EOF".

  Port 0 by default so the OS picks a free one; a fixed default collides with
  whatever else is running and fails at bind time. update() swaps the routing
  table without rebinding, so saving an example takes effect without changing
  the address open clients point at. stopAllMockServers runs on shutdown - a
  listener left bound blocks its own port on the next launch, which reads as
  "the mock is broken".

  The collection is COPIED under the state lock and used outside it, and the
  mock map has its OWN mutex: binding a socket while holding the state lock
  would be a long operation under the app's hottest lock, and holding a
  collection pointer across the release is the US-076 bug.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    bind 0.0.0.0                 "bound ::, reachable from the network"
    unknown name falls back      first example returned silently
    Content-Length passed through client read failed with unexpected EOF
    disabled headers sent        X-Disabled reached the wire
    invent a 200                 unrecorded routes answered 200

  Gates: build 0, vet 0, gofmt clean, golangci-lint 0 issues, go test 38.7s 0,
  -race clean.

US-073 - Mock server UI. DONE. Closes the mock pair with US-072.
  Collection > Mock Server tab: configurable port (0 = OS picks), Start/Stop,
  Reload examples, running URL and route count. Mock calls land in the existing
  DevTools network panel.

  THE SELF-TEST THE STORY ASKS FOR BY NAME is a Go test, not a browser check:
  TestSelfTestSendRequestAgainstOwnMock creates a request, saves a response
  example through the REAL example API, starts the mock, points a collection
  variable at its URL, and sends through the app's own SendRequest. Every other
  mock test drives it with net/http, which shares nothing with the app's send
  path - its transport cache, header handling and interpolation are all
  untested by those. A mock that answers curl but not the app is a mock nobody
  in this app can use.

  ANOTHER OF MY ASSERTIONS TESTED NOTHING, and a negative control found it.
  The self-test checked the DevTools half with
    strings.Contains(entry.URL, "/self-test") && Contains(entry.URL,
    "127.0.0.1")
  Passing nil as the recorder - removing mock logging ENTIRELY - left it
  passing. The reason: the app's own outgoing request has THE SAME URL as the
  mock's handling of it, so a URL match cannot tell them apart.

  Fixed by adding NetworkLog.Source ("mock" for mock-served calls), which the
  DevTools panel needs anyway: without it the two rows are identical and
  someone debugging a mock cannot tell which is which. The test now asserts
  BOTH a mock entry and an outgoing entry exist, and NC2 fails correctly.

  Logging is written in a deferred closure so it covers every exit path. The
  404 and 400 branches return early, and a log written only on success would
  leave exactly the calls someone is debugging invisible - NC3 confirms
  (1 entry instead of 3).

  Bodies are read under io.LimitReader(networkLogBodyLimit): the body is only
  used for the log, and an unbounded read lets one client hand the app however
  much memory it likes.

  recordMockNetworkLog takes the state lock ITSELF, because it runs on the
  mock's own goroutine - a handler must never assume the caller's locking.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    mock serves a different body   self-test body assertion fails
    mock logging removed           (after the fix) "never reached the log"
    log only on the success path   1 entry instead of 3
    (US-072's five still hold)

  -race clean, svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 197/197.

US-074 - Docs viewer. DONE. Phase 6's standalone features are now complete
  (072, 073, 074).
  docs_server.go: loopback listener serving GenerateCollectionDocs output at /
  (HTML) and /collection.yaml. Collection > Docs tab with port, Start/Stop and
  the serving URL.

  LOOPBACK ONLY, and the exposure here is WORSE than the mock's: generated docs
  contain every URL, header name and example body in the collection plus
  whichever environment variables the user chose to include - a more complete
  picture of an internal API than most of the requests themselves.

  RE-GENERATED PER REQUEST, not snapshotted at start. A preview that served the
  collection as it was when the server started would be wrong in the ONE
  situation it exists for: editing docs and refreshing to see the change. That
  failure is silent - the page renders perfectly with yesterday's content. NC2
  reproduced it exactly ("second fetch = first").

  Cache-Control: no-store on both routes. A cached preview stops updating,
  which defeats the reload it exists for.

  START PRE-VALIDATES by generating once up front, so a collection that cannot
  produce docs fails THERE with the error in front of the user, rather than
  starting a server that returns 500 into a browser tab they then have to go
  and read. NC5 confirms.

  A generation failure shows its reason on the page: it is the user's own
  malformed collection, and swallowing it leaves a blank page with nothing to
  act on.

  `render` is injected rather than the App captured, so the handler cannot
  reach app state without going through the one function that locks correctly -
  the same isolation as the mock's `record`.

  Negative controls, all five run and all five caught:
    bind all interfaces        "bound ::"
    snapshot at start          second fetch served the first render
    cacheable preview          Cache-Control empty
    failure swallowed          blank page, no reason
    no pre-validation at start a server started for an unknown collection

US-027 - Runes migration, lib/. DONE.
  All five components in src/lib/ migrated: OAuth2AdditionalParams,
  FileBodyTable, VariableTextOverlay, MultipartTable, KeyValueTable.
  export let -> $props(), bind: -> $bindable, local let -> $state.
  svelte-check 205/0/0 as the story requires - 0 errors AND 0 warnings.

  MIGRATING A COMPONENT IS NOT JUST THE PROPS. Runes mode is per-component, and
  turning it on surfaces three more things that all fail SILENTLY:

  1. LOCAL `let` STOPS BEING REACTIVE. `let draggingIndex = null` compiles,
     typechecks and renders - and the drag highlight simply never updates. Six
     such variables across the five components (scroll sync, bulk-edit toggle,
     bulk draft, drag state). svelte-check WARNS, which is why the story's
     criterion is 0/0 rather than 0 errors. NC2 confirms: reverting one $state
     produces exactly one warning.

  2. `on:event` IS DEPRECATED in runes mode. 11 handlers in FileBodyTable alone.

  3. EVENT MODIFIERS ARE GONE. Five `on:click|stopPropagation` in
     VariableTextOverlay had to become explicit event.stopPropagation() calls.
     Dropping them is not cosmetic: every one sits inside a clickable tooltip
     surface, so the click would bubble to the parent and saving an edit would
     also close the editor.

  $bindable IS LOAD-BEARING AND NARROW. Exactly one prop needed it -
  variableTooltipDraft, bound by App.svelte twice and by KeyValueTable and
  MultipartTable. NC1: removing $bindable produces four hard errors ("Cannot
  use 'bind:' with this property"), so this one cannot go wrong silently. Every
  other prop is passed by value; marking more of them bindable would add a
  writable surface nothing uses.

  A MISTAKE WORTH RECORDING: I tried to generate MultipartTable's Props block
  with a regex over `export let name: Type = default`. The regex split function
  types at the `=` inside `=>`, so `onAdd: () => void = () => {}` became type
  `()` and default `> void = () => {}` - 167 errors. Rewrote the block by hand.
  The four other components were done explicitly and none had a problem. Being
  clever about a 24-prop block cost more than typing it.

US-028 - Runes migration, workbench/. DONE.
  All TEN components migrated (the story says nine; LazyCodeEditor was added
  later by US-036). 88 props, 66 reactive statements, 88 event handlers.
  svelte-check 205/0/0.

  THE SPLIT THAT MATTERS: $: is one syntax for two different things, and runes
  make you choose.
    $: x = expr        a DERIVATION  -> $derived
    $: if (…) y = …    an EFFECT     -> $effect
  ResponseInspector alone had 37 derivations and 5 effects. Getting one wrong
  in the effect->derived direction is SILENT: a derivation is only evaluated
  when something reads it, and nothing reads these, so the code simply never
  runs. NC1 proves it - converting the matchIndex clamp to a $derived leaves
  svelte-check at 0/0 while the search cursor would point past the end of a
  narrowed result list forever.

  FOUR MORE THINGS RUNES MODE SURFACES, all silent:
    local `let` stops being reactive        24 variables across the ten
    on:event is deprecated                  88 handlers
    event modifiers are gone                on:submit|preventDefault in
                                            WorkspaceWindowPicker - without the
                                            explicit call the form submits and
                                            the whole app navigates away
    <slot> and <svelte:component> deprecated  2 slots became snippets (which
                                            also had to change in App.svelte),
                                            1 dynamic component became {@const}

  MY AUTOMATION WAS WRONG AGAIN, IN A NEW WAY. I wrote a depth-aware scanner to
  wrap `$: name = expr` in $derived(...), tracking brackets and string literals
  so multi-line expressions closed in the right place. It still mis-split
  `bytes`, because BALANCED PARENS ARE NOT A COMPLETE EXPRESSION - the ternary
  continued onto the next line after its parens closed. One case out of 37.
  Found by svelte-check, fixed by hand, and I checked the other 36 for the same
  shape rather than assuming.

  That is the second time this story that generated code cost more than typing
  it (US-027's regex split function types at the `=` in `=>`). The props blocks
  here were all written by hand for exactly that reason.

  Gates: go test 38.9s 0, svelte-check 205 files 0/0, eslint 0,
  npm test 197/197, vite build succeeds.

US-029 - Runes migration, App.svelte. ATTEMPTED, NOT COMPLETED. Tree restored
  clean and verified. This entry is the handover.

  SCALE: 12,187 lines, 122 $: statements (92 derivations / 29 effects), 447
  on: handlers, 10 event modifiers, 256 top-level let declarations, 0 export
  let, 0 slots (both converted already under US-028).

  I BUILT THE PIPELINE AND IT WORKS. Four defects surfaced in turn, each caught
  by svelte-check and each fixed:
    1. indentation-matching converted `let` inside FUNCTION BODIES. Fixed with
       brace-depth tracking.
    2. the continuation scanner treated a leading `/` as an operator, so it
       swallowed a `//` COMMENT and the statement after it, closing a paren in
       the wrong place. Fixed with a negative lookahead.
    3. the type/value split cut at the `=` inside `=>`, turning a type of
       `(() => void) | undefined` into `(()`. THIS IS THE THIRD TIME THE SAME
       DEFECT HAS APPEARED IN THIS MIGRATION (US-027's props regex, US-028's
       derivation scanner, here) AND I DID NOT APPLY MY OWN RECORDED LESSON.
    4. multi-line initializers got their closing paren on the first line,
       producing `$state<...>({)`. Fixed with the same depth scanner.
  After all four: 2400 errors -> 188, all of them ordinary type noise.

  THEN A GENUINE BLOCKER, and it is not a tooling problem:

    THE COMPONENT'S CENTRAL VARIABLE IS NAMED `state`.

  `$state(...)` beside a variable called `state` is ambiguous with Svelte's
  legacy store-subscription syntax, and svelte-check resolves it as the store:
  256 errors of "Cannot use 'state' as a store". Writing `$state<T>(...)`
  instead disambiguates but is rejected here as "untyped function calls may not
  accept type arguments", so neither form is usable while the variable keeps
  that name.

  `state` appears 308 times and is the single object every derivation reads.
  Renaming it is a prerequisite for US-029 and is its OWN piece of work - a
  mechanical rename of the app's central identifier across 12,000 lines, which
  is exactly the operation that produced four subtle corruptions today. It
  should be done as a standalone, separately verified commit BEFORE the runes
  migration, not folded into it.

  RECOMMENDED ORDER FOR WHOEVER PICKS THIS UP:
    a. rename `state` -> `appState` (or similar) in App.svelte alone, commit,
       verify 0/0. No other change in that commit.
    b. re-run the pipeline in scratchpad/{derive2,effects,state}.py, which are
       correct as of now and carry their defect history in their docstrings.
    c. expect ~190 ordinary type errors afterwards and fix them by hand; they
       are implicit-any parameters and use-before-assign, not structural.
    d. the five spots the story flags as non-mechanical are ALREADY SOLVED and
       written out in this session's transcript: the self-referential
       generateDocsDeselectedEnvIds filter needs a CONTENTS guard or the effect
       loops forever (filter returns a new array every pass, so an unguarded
       assign re-triggers itself); the .indeterminate DOM write and the three
       applyThemeToDocument/applyZoom/applyCodeFont calls are all $effect.

  Nothing was committed. svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 197/197,
  go test 39.2s 0 - all verified against the restored tree.

US-029 prerequisite - rename `state` to `appState` in App.svelte. DONE.
  Standalone commit, no other change, as the handover recommended.

  CORRECTION TO THE PREVIOUS ENTRY. I wrote that "neither form is usable" -
  that both `$state(...)` and `$state<T>(...)` fail while the variable is named
  `state`. Only the first half was right. An isolated probe component shows:
    let state: T | null = $state(null)   -> "Cannot use 'state' as a store"
    let appState = $state<T | null>(null) -> 0 errors
  The "untyped function calls may not accept type arguments" errors were a
  CASCADE of the store collision, not an independent problem. So the pipeline's
  original output form was correct all along and only the name was wrong. I
  should have isolated the collision before concluding anything about the
  generic form; it cost one wrong diagnosis in the handover.

  The rename itself, 283 bare occurrences, with three categories deliberately
  NOT renamed because they are different things that share a spelling:
    result.state / outcome.state (6)  property access on other objects
    { state: … } (3)                  OAuth2's own `state` parameter, an API
                                      field name, not this variable
    child `state` props (8)           the CHILD's public prop name is its own
                                      interface. The shorthand {state} became
                                      {appState} and stopped matching, so those
                                      are now explicit state={appState}. This
                                      is the one thing a blind rename breaks
                                      silently-looking: svelte-check caught all
                                      eight as unknown-property errors.
  `...state` spreads (13) ARE the variable and were renamed, despite the dot
  before them - handled with a sentinel rather than a blanket "not after a dot".

  Verified: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 197/197, vite build
  succeeds, go test 39.2s 0.

  US-029 IS NOW UNBLOCKED. The pipeline in scratchpad/{derive2,effects,state}.py
  is correct as of this commit and should be re-run against this file.

US-029 - Runes migration, App.svelte. DONE. Completes the runes work (027-029).
  12,187 lines. 281 $state, 94 $derived, 30 $effect. Zero residue: no $:, no
  on:, no svelte:component, no svelte:fragment, no export let.
  svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 197/197, vite build succeeds,
  go test 42.4s 0.

  It went through on the first pipeline run once the `state` rename landed:
  2400 errors before the rename, 33 after. The four scanner defects recorded in
  the earlier attempt were all genuine and all already fixed.

  SEVEN THINGS THE MIGRATION SURFACED, beyond the mechanical conversion:

  1. DECLARATION ORDER NOW MATTERS. Legacy `$:` statements were topologically
     sorted by the compiler, so their position in the file was irrelevant.
     $derived is a const and obeys the temporal dead zone. The US-032
     virtualization derivations sat ~200 lines ABOVE devToolsNetworkRows, which
     was fine for years and is now an error. Moved, with the reason in a
     comment so nobody moves them back.

  2. `let x = default` PLUS `$: x = expr` IS A REDECLARATION. That pairing is
     the standard legacy idiom - declare, then let the reactive statement
     overwrite. 13 variables had it. The default was never observable, so the
     declaration goes.

  3. A CONTINUATION CAN FOLLOW A COMMENT. activeResponseTabs was
     `(...)` then two comment lines then `.filter(...)`. My scanner correctly
     refuses to treat `//` as a continuation operator (that was defect 2 of the
     earlier attempt) and so stopped early here. Fixed by hand; it is the only
     one of 92.

  4. THE SELF-REFERENTIAL FILTER NEEDS A CONTENTS GUARD. generateDocsDeselected
     EnvIds reads and writes itself. filter returns a NEW array every pass, so
     an unguarded effect invalidates itself on its own write and loops forever.
     Legacy `$:` survived it because Svelte compared the reference and stopped.
     NC2 confirms removing the guard leaves svelte-check at 0/0 - a runtime
     loop no static check sees.

  5. EFFECT-VS-DERIVATION IS INVISIBLE TO EVERY GATE. NC1: turning the
     applyThemeToDocument effect into a $derived leaves 0 errors and 0
     warnings. The theme would simply never apply. That is why the 30 effects
     were classified by syntax and reviewed, not inferred.

  6. UNINITIALISED DECLARATIONS need $state<T>() with no argument. 27 of them
     were skipped by a pattern that required `= value`.

  7. THE CHILD'S PROP NAME IS ITS OWN INTERFACE - already found during the
     rename, worth repeating: {state} shorthand became {appState} and silently
     stopped matching 8 children until svelte-check flagged them.

  Bundle: initial chunk 416,846 bytes, down from 478,125 before the migration
  (runes generate less runtime scaffolding than the legacy reactive system).

US-031 - Virtualize the collection tree. PARTIAL, deliberately. The flattening
  core is done and tested; the markup rewiring is NOT, and the reason is stated
  rather than hidden.

  DONE: lib/sidebarTree.ts + 13 tests. flattenSidebar turns the nested tree
  (collections -> folder groups -> requests) into the linear row list a window
  can be computed over, honouring both collapse maps. indexOfRequest and
  visibleRequestCount support keyboard navigation and counts.

  Every mistake in flattening is SILENT in the UI rather than an error:
    a collapsed folder whose children still render looks like a broken toggle
    a row at the wrong depth reads as belonging to a different folder
    a DUPLICATE KEY makes Svelte's keyed {#each} reuse the wrong DOM node, so a
      click lands on a different request than the one under the cursor. This is
      reachable: the same request id genuinely appears twice when one folder is
      mounted as two collections, which is why the collection id is part of
      every key. NC2 (dropping it) fails 6 tests.

  Search deliberately OVERRIDES collapse - someone typing a query wants matches
  wherever they are, and hiding them inside a folder collapsed an hour ago makes
  the search look broken. NC1 confirms.

  MY OWN ARITHMETIC WAS WRONG AGAIN: I expected o-1 at index 4 after collapsing
  the folder above it, forgetting that the collapsed folder's OWN row still
  occupies a position - which the test directly above it asserts. Same class as
  US-051's miscount. Fixed with the layout written out in a comment.

  NOT DONE, AND WHY: the story requires "keyboard nav + context menus still
  work". Wiring the flat list into the sidebar means replacing the nested
  <article> per collection with a single windowed list - a substantial markup
  restructure that carries real risk for keyboard navigation, context menus and
  drag-to-reorder. I cannot exercise any of those from here, and shipping an
  unverified restructure against a criterion that explicitly names them would be
  claiming more than I checked. The pure half that must be correct is done and
  proven; the wiring wants a session with the app open.

  Gates: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 210/210 (+13), go test 43.9s 0.
