  (47fa3c8) shows it is a SPEC commit - it added the story to improvement_v2.md
  and argued which parts of the referenced layout to adopt. It did NOT create
  internal/, and there is no internal/ directory. So Phase 4's nine package
  extractions (US-060..US-071) are entirely ahead, and the restructure is their
  first step rather than something already done.

  Worth stating because the earlier note reads as though the layout exists.

  WHAT REMAINS, honestly sized:

    US-026  Store layer. Feasible now that everything is on runes. The real
            cost is that its criterion - collapsing WorkspaceCommandBar's 22
            props and KeyValueTable's 33 to store reads - means moving
            `appState` out of App.svelte into a .svelte.ts module, and
            `appState` has 296 references. Bounded and verifiable, but it is a
            single large edit, not a slice.

    US-031  Wiring only. The flattening core is done and tested; the markup
            restructure needs the app open to check keyboard nav, context menus
            and drag-to-reorder.

    US-037  Design tokens. 1,183 px literals plus a 12-theme screenshot diff.
            The diff is the criterion and needs a browser.

    US-060  Go package extraction, nine stories over ~85,000 lines starting
    ..071   with creating internal/. Safer than the Svelte work in one
            important way - go build catches every missed qualification, so
            there is no silent failure mode - but it is very large and each
            move touches thousands of references.

  Three of these four want a browser or are single large edits. None is a good
  fit for being started and left half-finished, which is the state this file
  already warns about twice.

US-026 - Store layer. PARTIAL. The first store is built and tested; the two
  named component collapses are not wired, and the reason is a measurement.

  DONE: lib/stores/variableTooltipState.ts (pure transitions, 13 tests) plus
  lib/stores/variableTooltipStore.svelte.ts (thin rune shell over it).

  A FINDING THAT SHAPED THE DESIGN: a .svelte.ts rune module CANNOT be
  exercised by `node --test`. $state is a compiler construct, so importing the
  store directly fails with "$state is not defined". The choice is a second
  test runner with the Svelte plugin, or keeping the logic somewhere plain. I
  kept it plain: none of the behaviour is reactive, so the shell has nothing
  left to test once the transitions are extracted. This is also the honest
  answer to the toolchain US-038 asked for - vitest earns its place when
  something genuinely needs the compiler, and here nothing does.

  The cluster this replaces was drilled through THREE components from FIVE call
  sites. Each hop is a place where a rename silently stops the tooltip
  responding with nothing to report it, which is the actual cost of the
  drilling - not verbosity.

  Save and copy deliberately stay OUT of the store: they need the collection
  actions and the error channel, and a store reaching for those would need the
  whole app injected and would stop being testable.

  NOT DONE, and this is a sizing finding rather than a preference: the story's
  headline is collapsing WorkspaceCommandBar's 22 props and KeyValueTable's 33
  to store reads. Every one of the command bar's 20 data props derives from
  App.svelte's own selection logic (activeWorkspace / activeCollection /
  activeRequest and the tab state behind them), so collapsing them means moving
  that logic into the store - and `appState` has 296 references with 123
  assignment sites. That is one large edit, not a slice, and half of it is worse
  than none.

  Negative controls, all four run and all four caught:
    unfound variable seeds its placeholder   draft test fails
    cancelEdit also closes the tooltip       open-after-cancel test fails
    toggleActive cannot close                dismissal test fails
    beginEdit ignores `editable`             read-only test fails

  Gates: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 223/223 (+13), vite build
  succeeds, go test 40.6s 0.

US-026 continued - appState moved into workspaceStore. DONE.
  lib/stores/workspaceStore.svelte.ts owns the AppState the whole app reads.
  App.svelte keeps `const appState = $derived(workspaceStore.appState)`, so its
  ~170 READ sites are untouched; all 122 ASSIGNMENT sites now target the store.

  THIS MIGRATION HAD A PROPERTY THE SVELTE ONES DID NOT: a missed assignment is
  a HARD COMPILE ERROR, because you cannot assign to a $derived. The negative
  control (reverting one site to the local alias) produces "Cannot assign to
  'appState' because it is a constant". After four silent-corruption defects in
  the runes work, having the compiler enumerate every site was the whole reason
  to structure it this way rather than rewriting reads too.

  TWO THINGS THE MOVE SURFACED:

  1. My assignment regex rewrote the ALIAS DECLARATION itself - I inserted
     `const appState = $derived(...)` before running the rewrite, and it
     contains `appState =`. Produced `const workspaceStore.appState = ...` and
     a 53-error implicit-any cascade from the broken declaration. Order of
     operations, caught immediately.

  2. TYPE NARROWING NO LONGER FLOWS. Four sites read `appState.x` directly
     after assigning, and TypeScript used to narrow the local to non-null from
     that assignment. The assignment now targets the store and the read goes
     through the alias, so it cannot. Made optional, matching what the rest of
     the file already does; all four sit immediately after a successful binding
     call, so the value cannot actually be null.

  Still not done for this story: collapsing WorkspaceCommandBar's 22 props and
  KeyValueTable's 33. The foundation is now in place - components CAN read the
  store - but the command bar's props derive from App.svelte's selection logic
  (activeWorkspace/activeCollection/activeRequest and the tab state behind
  them), which has to move next for the collapse to be real rather than a
  second copy.

  Gates: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 223/223, vite build succeeds
  (initial chunk 417,731 B), go test 39.5s 0.

US-026 continued - selection chain into the store, command bar collapsed. DONE
  for WorkspaceCommandBar; KeyValueTable's 33 remain.

  The store now owns selectedCollectionId and selectedEnvironmentId (41
  assignment sites moved, same compile-error guarantee - the NC produces
  "Cannot assign to 'selectedCollectionId' because it is a constant") and
  derives activeTab, selectedCollection, activeCollection, activeRequest,
  selectedEnvironment and activeGlobalEnvironment. App.svelte keeps aliases, so
  its ~200 reads of those are untouched.

  WORKSPACECOMMANDBAR: 23 PROPS -> 10. The fourteen that went were each
  computed INLINE at the call site and passed down, so the same expression
  existed in App.svelte and the prop existed in the component - two places to
  keep in step for one value. They are now single store getters.

  What deliberately STAYED as props: sidebarCollapsed and activeView (the
  shell's own view state), notificationCount and the two run indicators
  (derived from surfaces outside the workspace), and the callbacks. Moving
  those would put App.svelte's shell state in a module named for the workspace,
  which is a worse home for it than a prop.

  Two decisions recorded in the store because they are not obvious:
    selectedCollection is kept SEPARATE from activeCollection. The fallback
      chain is explicit-selection, then the active tab's collection, then the
      first. Collapsing them would make a deleted selection silently jump the
      user to an unrelated collection instead of following their tab.
    environmentName returns 'No environment' when nothing is selected rather
      than the first environment's name. Falling back would tell the user they
      are sending against an environment they never picked.

  Gates: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 223/223, vite build succeeds,
  go test 38.5s 0.

US-026 - Store layer. DONE. Both named collapses complete.

  KeyValueTable      33 -> 24 props
  MultipartTable     24 -> 15 props
  VariableTextOverlay 18 -> 9 props
  WorkspaceCommandBar 23 -> 10 props (earlier commit)

  The tooltip cluster is gone from all three tables AND from 45 lines of
  drilled markup in App.svelte. Neither table ever USED those nine props -
  they existed solely to hand them to VariableTextOverlay one level down. That
  is the shape prop drilling always takes, and the cost is that each hop is a
  place where a rename silently stops the tooltip responding.

  App.svelte's five tooltip locals and its inline tooltip markup (rendered
  twice, for the URL overlay and the variable chips) now read the store too, so
  there is one copy of this state in the app rather than four.

  WHAT DELIBERATELY STAYED AS PROPS: onSave, onCopy, onEditorKey, onEditorBlur
  and displayTooltipValue. Every one needs the collection actions or the error
  channel, and a store reaching for those would need the whole app injected and
  would stop being testable - which is the property that made the 13 transition
  tests possible in the first place.

  The negative control closes the loop between the tests and the UI: breaking
  toggleActive in the pure module fails 3 tests, and that module is now what
  the real tooltips call.

  Gates: svelte-check 205/0/0, eslint 0, npm test 223/223, vite build succeeds,
  go test 38.6s 0.

--- US-060 — internal/types, STARTED (proxy cluster landed, green) ---

  MEASURED FIRST, because this is the story where guessing is expensive:
    81 type declarations in app.go:132-1210
    266 methods on *App
    637 frontend references to `main.X`
    100,175 Go lines total

  THE HYPOTHESIS I TESTED, AND DISPROVED: that leaving a Go type alias behind
  in package main (`type ProxyConfig = types.ProxyConfig`) would hide the move
  from Wails and keep the frontend untouched.

  It does not. The generator follows the alias to its DEFINING package. Moving
  one leaf type made models.ts grow a second namespace and turned
  `main.ProxyPreferences.pac` into `types.ProxyPACConfig`. So every moved type
  costs frontend edits, and the 637 references are real work, not avoidable.

  I tested that on ONE type before moving 81. That is the whole point: the
  cheap experiment cost five minutes and killed a plan that would have looked
  fine until the regenerate step, by which point 81 types would already have
  moved.

  WHAT THE ALIASES ARE STILL FOR: they are not a Wails shim, they are a Go
  shim. package main names these types in hundreds of places, and the alias
  keeps all of it compiling so the extraction can go cluster by cluster with
  every step green, instead of one 100k-line commit that only builds at the end.

  THE PROCEDURE, now proven end to end:
    1. move a cluster to internal/types, leave aliases in main
    2. go build ./...            -> catches the Go half
    3. wails generate module
    4. svelte-check              -> NAMES every stale `main.X`, one per line
    5. rewrite those to `types.X`, add `types` to the models import
    6. full gates

  Step 4 is why this is safe rather than another mechanical-edit disaster:
  the compiler enumerates the work. No regex has to identify which `Response`
  or `Header` or `Item` is a type — and given that I broke five large
  mechanical edits this session, three of them the SAME `=>` splitting bug,
  a procedure where the tooling finds the sites is worth more than a clever one.

  LANDED: ProxyConfig, ProxyAuthConfig, ProxyPACConfig, ProxyPreferences,
  ClientCertificateConfig. 23 frontend references rewritten across App.svelte
  and ProxySection.svelte.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok; svelte-check 209/0/0; eslint 0;
  npm test 223/223; vite build ok.

  REMAINING: 76 types. The cost is now known rather than estimated, and each
  cluster is independently green.

--- US-060 — internal/types, COMPLETE for app.go ---

  89 types moved. app.go now declares exactly ONE exported struct: App itself,
  which stays because 266 methods hang off it and methods cannot cross packages.

    internal/types   22 files, ~1,150 lines
    app.go           43,783 lines, 0 data types
    aliases in main  89

  TerminalSession was the last one and worth a note: it moved, but the
  terminalSessionProcess beside it did NOT. One is what the frontend sees, the
  other owns the live pty and the exec.Cmd. The split between them is exactly
  the line this story is about — data crosses the boundary, running processes
  do not.

  THE TOOLING IS IN tools/extraction/ AND THE GUARDS ARE THE POINT:

    extract.py   takes type NAMES, derives the block itself, and REFUSES unless
                 the named types are contiguous.
    sync.sh      regenerates bindings, rewrites main.X -> types.X, and REFUSES
                 a type that is not actually declared in internal/types.
    dropmain.py  removes `main` from an import once its last use is gone.

  Every one of those guards was added because it caught me, in this order:
    - line numbers shift after each extraction -> same off-by-one 3x -> names
    - unexported types interleaved in a range (brunoEnvironmentExport*) would
      have been moved into a package that cannot alias them back
    - passing a type to sync.sh that I had NOT moved rewrote a valid main.X
      into a types.X that did not exist, and broke App.svelte

  That last one is the reason for the sync guard: the rewrite step is the only
  part of this procedure that is NOT compiler-checked before it runs, so it had
  to check itself.

  DEPENDENCY CLOSURE was the real work, not the moving. A moved type drags
  everything it embeds: Response pulled VisualizerPayload, CookieEntry and
  ResponseTimings out of three other files; AppState pulled OpenTab and
  Feature; RunResult pulled GrpcMessage and WSMessage. Miss one and the
  packages point both ways, which Go simply refuses to build - so the compiler
  found every one of these for me.

  Gates after the final move: go build, go vet, go test all ok; svelte-check
  209/0/0; eslint 0; npm test 223/223; vite build ok.

  NOT DONE, deliberately: the 89 aliases still sit in package main. Deleting
  them means touching every one of the thousands of use sites, which buys
  nothing right now - the types are already in one package and app.go is
  already free of them. US-061 onward (extracting BEHAVIOUR into packages) is
  what makes removing them worthwhile, because that is when a caller in
  another package has to name the type properly anyway.

--- US-067 — internal/auth/awsv4 (DONE) ---

  1,271 lines of SigV4 signing and credential resolution out of app.go, plus
  526 lines of tests, behind a 5-function API. app.go: 43,783 -> 42,677.

  WHY THIS ONE FIRST: zero of its 55 declarations were methods on *App. That is
  the property that decides whether behaviour can move at all, and it is worth
  checking BEFORE picking a target - the line ranges in the story table say
  nothing about it.

  THE BOUNDARY GOT BETTER, NOT JUST DIFFERENT. Sign used to take
  `vars map[string]string`. Every use of that map was interpolation, so it now
  takes `resolve func(string) string` instead. The package no longer knows that
  variables are stored in a map, or that interpolation exists - and US-069 can
  move the interpolator without touching this package at all.

  Same shape for the HTTP client: credential calls (STS, SSO, OIDC) must go
  through the app's shared transport cache, but the package must not depend on
  it. So it defaults to a plain client and package main overrides it in an init.
  Dependency points one way.

  !!! A REAL TEST DEFECT, FOUND BY A NEGATIVE CONTROL, PRE-EXISTING !!!

  assertAWSV4Signature - the helper nearly every AWS test leans on - verified a
  signature by RECOMPUTING it with the same functions the signer used. So:

      change awsSigningKey's "AWS4" prefix to "AWS5"  -> every test passed
      delete the canonical-header whitespace collapse -> every test passed

  Both sides moved together. The tests could detect signing and verifying
  disagreeing with each other; they could not detect both being wrong. That is
  the whole value of an algorithm test and it was absent.

  I did not introduce this - it is how the tests read in git history - but I
  nearly shipped it, because the control I ran FIRST (breaking the SSO cache
  filename) also passed and I could have written that off as a weak spot rather
  than a pattern.

  FIXED with two conformance tests that share NOTHING with the implementation:
  the canonical request is a literal string and the signing key is derived with
  bare crypto/hmac. Now the AWS5 break fails 2 tests and the whitespace break
  fails 1.

  The second test exists because the first one did not catch the whitespace
  break: none of its header values contained a run of spaces. A conformance
  test only covers the rules its fixture actually exercises.

  A THIRD CONTROL PROVED NOTHING and I nearly counted it: `s/host;/HOST;/`
  matched zero lines. A control that does not change the code is not a control.
  Check the edit landed before believing the result.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok (LiteAPI 38.2s, awsv4 0.4s);
  svelte-check 209/0/0; eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- US-071 — internal/importers (DONE) + internal/scalar ---

  2,632 lines of OpenAPI, Postman and Insomnia import out of app.go behind a
  5-function API. app.go: 42,677 -> 39,900.

  I picked this target by measuring first, and the measurement is the reason it
  went well: the region held 123 free functions and ZERO methods on *App. The
  interp region next door has 10 App methods mixed in, and would have been a
  much worse first pick despite looking similar in the story table.

  DEVIATION FROM THE STORY, on purpose: it asks for importers/openapi,
  importers/postman and importers/insomnia as three packages. They are three
  FILES in one package. The three formats share exactly three helpers between
  them, so splitting would have required a FOURTH package existing only to hold
  those. The file split gives the same separation for none of that cost.

  internal/scalar came out of this and was not planned. The importers needed
  YAMLString 33 times, FirstNonEmpty 27, and a dozen more small helpers. Copying
  them would have left two definitions of "what counts as a scalar" and "what
  characters are legal in a filename" free to drift apart, so they moved to one
  place with wrappers left in app.go to keep its 400+ call sites unchanged.

  internal/types stays a LEAF: it carries its own three-line firstNonEmpty and
  newIDLocal rather than importing scalar. Every package depends on types, and
  putting another package underneath it buys nothing for six lines.

  THE COMPILER FOUND THE TYPE NAMES, NOT A REGEX. Prefixing bare type names with
  `types.` ran as a loop: build, read `undefined: X`, keep the X that are
  declared in internal/types, rewrite, repeat. Two passes. A regex over 89 known
  names would have had to decide which `Collection` is a type -- and on the
  first attempt exactly that went wrong anyway, because a STRUCT FIELD can share
  a name with a type:

      Collection []insomniaV5Item   ->   types.Collection []insomniaV5Item

  The rule now excludes an identifier that is the first token on its line
  followed by whitespace, which is what a field declaration looks like.

  !!! TWO SELF-INFLICTED FAILURES WORTH KEEPING !!!

  1. A wrapper generator parsed `func f() ([]interface{}, bool) {` by matching
     to the first `{` -- which is the one inside `interface{}`. It emitted
     `func listValue(raw interface{}) ([]interface { return ... }` and app.go
     stopped parsing. NINE reported syntax errors, all cascading from that ONE
     line. Fix the first error and re-read before believing the rest.

  2. Restoring a negative control with `git checkout internal/types/request_item.go`
     reverted the file to its COMMITTED state -- silently deleting eight
     functions I had appended since the commit. Recovered by re-extracting them
     from `git show HEAD:app.go`, but the lesson is blunt: git checkout restores
     to the last COMMIT, not to the state before your edit. For a control, keep
     a copy of the file and restore from that.

  A NO-OP CONTROL AGAIN: the first break I tried matched zero lines and I nearly
  read the resulting "0 failures" as meaningful. Second time this session.
  Always print the match count before trusting a control.

  Real controls, after that: breaking the Postman collection name failed 3
  tests, breaking types.GetKeyValue failed 10.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok (LiteAPI 38.7s); svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223; vite build ok.

--- US-062 groundwork — internal/transport (DONE) ---

  1,032 lines out of app.go: TLS client certificates and proxy resolution in all
  four modes (none, manual, system, PAC) including the goja PAC runtime.
  app.go: 39,900 -> 38,939.

  Picked by scanning app.go for the longest contiguous runs of top-level
  functions with NO *App methods in them. That scan is now the first step of
  every one of these, and it is what ruled out the two obvious alternatives:

    US-066 auth/oauth  - scattered from line 112 to 37,358, not contiguous
    US-068 scripting   - inside a 14,987-line App-free run, but too large to
                         finish safely in one pass

  TWO SEAMS, AND THEY ARE DELIBERATELY DIFFERENT FROM awsv4's:

  awsv4 took `resolve func(string) string` as a PARAMETER because vars was used
  in exactly one function. Here vars is threaded through EIGHT, so the
  interpolator is a settable package variable instead. Rewriting eight
  signatures and every caller would have been a large mechanical edit that
  bought nothing - the package still does not depend on the interpolator's
  implementation either way. Same for the PAC HTTP client.

  A NAME COLLISION WORTH REMEMBERING: http_transport_cache.go has ten local
  variables called `transport`, so `import "LiteAPI/internal/transport"` shadows
  them. Imported as `xport` there. And the first fix made it worse - rewriting
  `transport.[A-Z]` caught `transport.Proxy` and `transport.TLSClientConfig`,
  which are FIELDS on the local *http.Transport, not package references. The
  rewrite now lists the twenty moved names explicitly.

  NEGATIVE CONTROLS - the no-op problem, finally automated. The control runner
  now diffs the file and refuses to report a result if the edit did not land.
  It immediately caught two of my three attempts as invalid, which is the third
  and fourth time this session that a control has silently measured nothing.

  Valid controls:
    proxy URL assembly       -> 1 failing test
    proxy protocol default   -> 5 failing tests
    PAC "DIRECT" directive   -> 0 failing tests   <-- UNCOVERED PATH

  The DIRECT case is a real gap, not a false alarm: the edit landed and nothing
  noticed. Recorded rather than fixed here because it is a test-coverage story,
  not a refactoring one, but it is exactly the kind of thing that only shows up
  when someone deliberately breaks the code.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok (LiteAPI 38.4s); svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- PAC directive coverage (closes the gap found above) ---

  The uncovered "DIRECT" branch is now tested, and the diagnosis is the
  interesting part: a BARE "DIRECT" cannot detect the bug. The directive has
  fewer than two fields, so the fallthrough skips it and the function returns
  "no proxy" either way. Any test written the obvious way would have passed with
  the branch deleted - which is presumably how it ended up untested.

  The branch only earns its keep on ORDERING. A PAC return value is a preference
  list, so "DIRECT; PROXY 10.0.0.1:8080" means go direct; without the explicit
  check the loop skips DIRECT and uses the proxy, sending traffic through a
  proxy the script explicitly asked it to bypass.

  Five tests added: DIRECT-first, PROXY-first, bare DIRECT, the four scheme
  keywords, and an unknown keyword falling through to the next preference.

  Controls: deleting the DIRECT branch now fails 1 test (it failed 0 before);
  dropping the SOCKS5 alias fails 1.

--- US-063 — internal/grpcexec (DONE) + internal/auth/wsse ---

  912 lines of gRPC out of app.go: method resolution from .proto files and from
  server reflection, request messages, metadata, message templates built from
  descriptors, and grpcurl command generation. app.go: 38,939 -> 38,079.

  WHAT STAYED: dialling and streaming. Those are *App methods holding session
  state, and the story's boundary is exactly the free/method line again.

  TWO THINGS ENDED UP IN THE WRONG PACKAGE FIRST, AND BOTH ARE WORTH THE NOTE:

  1. applyWSSEHeader followed the dependency graph into grpcexec, because a gRPC
     function applies WSSE auth to its metadata. But WSSE is HTTP-era
     authentication used by BOTH paths, and burying its crypto in a gRPC package
     puts it where nobody would look. It now has internal/auth/wsse, beside
     awsv4. Following the compiler is how you find dependencies; it is not how
     you decide where something belongs.

  2. websocketSessionKey came along because my end-of-block walk overshot by one
     declaration - it walks to the next closing brace, and the gRPC block ended
     one function earlier than I passed in. Returned to app.go. Deriving a
     boundary is safer than guessing one, but it still needs checking.

  A wrapper generated earlier had `return f(...)` for a function returning
  NOTHING, which only surfaced when a later rename touched the same line. It had
  been sitting there compiling because nothing called it.

  Controls (all landing-checked): grpcurl full-method assembly -> 3 failing,
  EnabledKeyValues -> 4 failing.

  RUNNING TOTAL for the package extraction:
    app.go        44,000ish -> 38,079
    internal/     7,979 lines across 7 packages

--- US-069 — internal/interp (DONE), and both seams removed ---

  Template-variable interpolation, the {{$randomInt}} dynamic variables, and
  process.env resolution now live in internal/interp. app.go: 38,079 -> 37,899.

  THIS ONE PAID A DEBT. internal/transport and internal/grpcexec each carried a
  settable `interpolate` package variable, wired at startup, purely because this
  code had not moved yet. Both are GONE - they import interp directly now, and
  `grep SetInterpolator` returns zero. That was the point of doing interp before
  the bigger stories: every package extracted after this one can just import it.

  The tests moved with the code, EXCEPT one. TestInterpolatePreservesPrecedence
  asserts the contract BETWEEN buildVariableMap and the interpolator, and
  buildVariableMap needs collections, environments and the workspace. It stayed
  in package main as interpolate_precedence_test.go. A test that spans two
  packages belongs with the one that owns the state, not with the one whose
  function name is in the title.

  !!! THE PARITY LEDGER CAUGHT THE MOVE, WHICH IS WHY IT EXISTS !!!

  TestPostmanLedgerCitesTestsThatExist failed on seven rows the moment the
  dynamic-variable tests left package main. It scanned `.` only, so tests that
  moved into internal/ read as deleted. It now walks the module.

  This is the guard doing precisely its job: an entry naming a test that no
  longer exists still reads as evidence, and is worse than naming none. The fix
  was the SCANNER, not the ledger - the tests do still exist, and quietly
  editing the ledger to match would have destroyed the thing being verified.

  Control: renaming TestDynamicVariableShapes makes the guard fail again, so
  broadening the scan did not turn it into a rubber stamp.

  A REPEAT MISTAKE, third time: a blanket rewrite of processEnvVariablePrefix
  hit the const's OWN DECLARATION, producing `const interp.ProcessEnvPrefix =`.
  Renaming an identifier everywhere always includes the place it is defined.

  And the end-of-block walk overshot by one declaration AGAIN (it took
  defaultState with it), the same way it took websocketSessionKey in US-063.
  Walking forward to the next closing brace finds the end of the NEXT function
  whenever the passed line is already past the last one.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok; svelte-check 209/0/0; eslint 0;
  npm test 223/223.

--- internal/codegen + the clone family into internal/types (DONE) ---

  app.go: 37,899 -> 37,364. code_generation.go merged in: the block in app.go
  and that file were the same concern, split across two files by nothing but
  when each was written.

  A CORRECTION TO US-063: EnabledKeyValues and ShellSingleQuote had landed in
  internal/grpcexec, because gRPC was extracted first and happened to be what
  needed them. Neither has anything to do with gRPC. They now live in
  internal/types and internal/scalar. That is the SECOND time this exact mistake
  has happened (applyWSSEHeader was the first) and the rule is now proven twice:
  the compiler tells you what depends on what, never where something belongs.

  The Wails binding CodeGenerationTargets stayed in package main as
  codegen_bindings.go - it is a method on *App, and a method cannot move to
  another package without its receiver.

  !!! A SECOND UNTESTED CONTRACT, FOUND THE SAME WAY !!!

  Making CloneVariables return its input directly - aliasing instead of copying
  - failed ZERO tests. Every clone function exists so a caller can edit a copy
  without the original changing underneath it, and nothing checked that.

  This failure does not look like a failure. An aliased clone works perfectly
  until someone edits the copy, at which point a request the user never touched
  changes, usually in a different pane, with no error anywhere.

  Seven tests added. Controls now: aliasing CloneVariables fails 1, CloneTags
  fails 2, and sharing AuthConfig's nested OAuth2 params fails 1. The nested
  case is the one worth having - a struct copied by value shares its slice
  headers, so a shallow copy looks right at the top level and is wrong one field
  down.

  A TOOLING BUG WORTH RECORDING: the type-prefixing loop stalled, repeating the
  same two names forever without changing anything. My "do not touch struct
  field declarations" guard was `(?!\s+[\[\*\w])`, and \s MATCHES NEWLINES - so
  a type at the END of a line looked like a field declaration whenever the NEXT
  line happened to start with an identifier. Fixed to `[^\S\n]+`. It stalled
  rather than corrupting, which is the good failure mode, but only by luck.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test all ok across 8 packages; svelte-check
  209/0/0; eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- US-064 — internal/wsexec (DONE) ---

  WebSocket send preparation out of app.go: target URL, handshake headers,
  keep-alive, and the outbound frames with their opcodes. app.go: 37,364 ->
  37,213. Dialling and the live session stay on *App - they hold the connection
  and the per-session goroutines.

  This is the part of a WebSocket session worth testing WITHOUT a socket: what
  gets sent, and how, is decided entirely before anything is dialled.

  !!! A THIRD UNTESTED CONTRACT, AND THE WORST OF THEM !!!

  Making FramePayload return TextMessage for a BINARY message failed no test.
  That is a protocol-level bug whose symptom looks like anything but: the frame
  still goes out, the server either rejects it or reads base64 text as the
  payload, and nothing on this side reports an error. The base64 decode was
  equally unverified.

  13 tests added. Controls now: binary-as-text fails 1, dropping the "bin"
  alias fails 1, downgrading wss to ws fails 1, ignoring message selection
  fails 1.

  !!! THE CONTROL RUNNER WAS LYING TO ME, A SECOND WAY !!!

  Two of those four controls reported "0 failing" AFTER I had written tests that
  cover them. Both breaks did not COMPILE - and a build failure produces no
  "--- FAIL" lines at all, so counting them reads a broken build as a clean pass.

  This is the SAME mistake as the -race flake at the top of this file: `go test`
  reporting failure without any test failing. I had already written "a build
  failure reports at the top and a test failure in the middle" and still walked
  into it from the other direction.

  tools/extraction/control.sh now refuses to report a number unless the edit
  landed AND the package built. Both lies a control can tell are covered:
  an edit that matched nothing, and an edit that does not compile.

  A TOOLING GAP CLOSED: block_end's companion names_in only matched
  `func name(`, so `func (a *App) websocketDialer` sitting INSIDE the range was
  invisible to the very check meant to catch stray App methods - and it got
  extracted into a package that cannot have it. names_in now reports methods as
  (Receiver).name, and the scanner self-check shows it.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 9 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- US-066 (part) — internal/auth/oauth1 (DONE) ---

  407 lines of OAuth 1.0a signing out of app.go: base string, HMAC-SHA1/256/512,
  RSA-SHA1/256/512, PLAINTEXT, the Authorization header and the body-hash
  extension. app.go: 37,213 -> 36,802. Sign is the only entry point app.go uses.

  OAUTH2 DELIBERATELY NOT MOVED. Its 2,027 lines are interleaved with ten *App
  methods - the credential cache on disk, the system-browser flow, the token
  HTTP requests and the callback waiter. Splitting the pure parts out of that is
  a different and larger job than OAuth1, which never touched *App at all.

  !!! THE SIGV4 TAUTOLOGY AGAIN, IN THE OTHER SIGNER !!!

  The existing OAuth1 test builds its expected signature by calling Signature,
  ParameterString, BaseURL and Encode - the functions under test. I went looking
  for it specifically BECAUSE awsv4 had the same shape, and it was there.

  Proof it measured nothing: with those tests alone, making oauth1Nonce return
  a CONSTANT failed nothing. A fixed nonce is a replay vulnerability, not a
  cosmetic bug.

  Three tests added that share nothing with the implementation: the base string
  is assembled with literal string operations, percent-encoding is written out
  per RFC 3986 rather than borrowed from Encode, and the HMAC is taken with
  crypto/hmac.

  Controls now: constant nonce fails 1, signing key without its "&" separator
  fails 2, percent-encoding disabled fails 1.

  THE PATTERN IS WORTH STATING PLAINLY. Four untested contracts found this
  session, all by negative control, none by reading code:
    SigV4     - verification recomputed with the signer's own functions
    PAC       - the DIRECT branch, undetectable with the obvious test
    clone*    - deep-copy contract, aliasing passed everything
    OAuth1    - same tautology as SigV4, plus a constant nonce passing
  Every one of them passed a full green test suite. The suite being green says
  nothing about whether the tests can fail.

--- US-065 (part) — internal/auth/digest (DONE) ---

  159 lines of HTTP Digest out of app.go: challenge parsing, qop selection, the
  Authorization header and the retry decision. app.go: 36,802 -> 36,645.

  The retry decision lives WITH the header builder on purpose. Digest is a
  two-round-trip scheme - the first request is expected to 401, and the header
  can only be built from the challenge that comes back - so "should we retry"
  and "what do we send on the retry" are one concern.

  !!! THE SAME TAUTOLOGY, A THIRD TIME, AND THE WORST INSTANCE !!!

  The existing tests build their expected digest with MD5Hex, the function under
  test. Making MD5Hex RETURN ITS INPUT UNCHANGED failed no test at all. The
  response hash is the entire security property of Digest auth, and nothing
  verified it.

  Three signers in this repo, three identical shapes:
    SigV4      verification recomputed with the signer's own helpers
    OAuth 1.0a expected signature built from Signature/ParameterString/Encode
    Digest     expected response built from MD5Hex

  I found the second and third by looking for the shape of the first. That is
  the useful part: once a tautological test appears in one signer, it is worth
  checking every other, because the same habit produced them all.

  Seven tests added, sharing nothing with the implementation - the digest is
  taken with crypto/md5 and checked against RFC 2617's published vector
  (6629fae49393a05397450978507c4ef1), which fails on a wrong hash, wrong field
  order OR wrong separator.

  Controls now: MD5 as identity fails 3, qop always "auth" fails 1, a constant
  cnonce fails 1.

  qop matters more than it looks: auth-int is NOT implemented, so a challenge
  offering only auth-int must yield no qop rather than silently claiming "auth"
  and sending a response computed over the wrong thing.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 11 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- WSSE tests — the tautology, fourth and final signer ---

  wsse.PasswordDigest was the last unchecked one, and it had the same shape:
  the single test in package main built its expectation by calling
  PasswordDigest. Making it return the PASSWORD UNCHANGED failed nothing.

  ALL FOUR SIGNERS IN THIS REPO HAD IT:
    SigV4       verification recomputed with the signer's own helpers
    OAuth 1.0a  expectation built from Signature/ParameterString/Encode
    Digest      expectation built from MD5Hex
    WSSE        expectation built from PasswordDigest

  Four for four. This was not four separate oversights, it is one habit: when
  you already have a function that computes the value, using it to state the
  expectation feels like reuse. It produces a test that can only fail if the
  code disagrees with itself.

  !!! A SPEC DEVIATION FOUND WHILE WRITING THE TEST - PINNED, NOT FIXED !!!

  The WSSE UsernameToken profile says
      PasswordDigest = Base64(SHA1(nonce + created + password))
  over the RAW 20 digest bytes. This code hex-encodes the digest FIRST and
  base64s that string, so the value is double-encoded: 40 hex characters
  base64'd rather than 20 binary bytes.

  I PINNED THE CURRENT BEHAVIOUR AND DID NOT CHANGE IT. Correcting it alters
  what goes out on the wire for every user whose server accepts today's format,
  and that is not a call to make silently inside a refactor. The test states
  both forms explicitly and fails if the output ever becomes the profile form
  without someone updating the note, so the decision is visible rather than
  buried. Whether to move to the profile form is a real decision with a
  compatibility cost, and it belongs to whoever owns the product.

  Six tests added. Controls: digest as identity fails 2, Created not normalised
  to UTC fails 1, unescaped username quotes fails 1, constant nonce fails 1.

  The UTC one is worth keeping: a server comparing Created against its own
  window rejects a local-time value, and the symptom is an intermittent auth
  failure that looks like anything but a formatting bug.

--- US-070 (part) — internal/store/bru (DONE) ---

  ~1,065 lines of Bruno .bru reading out of app.go: block structure, scalar and
  key/value maps, and the request, environment and collection documents built
  from them. app.go: 36,645 -> 35,580.

  A foreign on-disk format is worth isolating for a specific reason: it has no
  dependency on app state, and every bug in it shows up as a request that
  silently LOADS WRONG rather than as an error.

  A BOUNDARY I GOT WRONG AND BACKED OUT: AssignYAMLBodyData first went into
  internal/types, following the pattern that a helper operating on RequestBody
  belongs with the type. It does not - it needs a YAML key/value reader, and
  types is the leaf package every other one depends on. Putting a YAML parser
  underneath everything would have been a genuinely bad structure, and Go said
  so immediately (undefined: parseYAMLKeyValues). Both moved to store/bru
  instead, with a note that they are shared with the YAML request reader still
  in package main and should travel together when that moves.

  THE PACKAGE-NAME COLLISION, SECOND TIME: app_test.go has seven local
  variables called `bru`, so the import shadows them - exactly what happened
  with `transport` in US-062. Imported as `brustore` there. Worth expecting now:
  a package named after a domain noun will collide with variables named after
  the same noun, and the fix is an alias at the importing site, not a rename of
  the variables.

  Controls (landing-checked): ParseSections returning nil fails 44 tests,
  ParseScalarMap returning nil fails 33, ignoring the enabled flag fails 1.
  This code is genuinely well covered - unlike the four signers, its tests were
  written against fixtures rather than against itself.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 12 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- US-070 (rest) — internal/store/yamlstore (DONE) + dead wrapper cleanup ---

  649 lines of LiteAPI's own YAML request format out of app.go.
  app.go: 35,580 -> 34,890.

  Named yamlstore, not yaml: a package called yaml collides with
  gopkg.in/yaml.v3 at every call site importing both, which is most of them.

  12 DEAD WRAPPERS REMOVED. The compatibility shims left behind by earlier
  extractions had lost their last callers as later passes rewrote call sites to
  the packages directly. Worth noting HOW: my first check counted only CALLS
  (`name(`), which would have deleted four that are still referenced as bare
  function values. The stricter check - any word-boundary occurrence - kept
  those. A "find unused functions" regex that only looks for calls is wrong in
  Go, where a function name is also a value.

  !!! THE NEAREST MISS OF THE WHOLE SESSION !!!

  The mechanical rewrite pointed this package's normalizeBodyMode call sites at
  codegen.NormalizedBodyMode, because the names are nearly identical. They are
  NOT the same function:

    yamlstore's  ""            -> "none"     four urlencoded spellings -> one
                 unknown       -> "text"     "binary" -> "file"
    codegen's    two form modes rewritten, EVERYTHING ELSE PASSED THROUGH

  The redirect COMPILED. It was caught only because the same rewrite also
  renamed the declaration and produced a syntax error. Had it renamed one fewer
  thing, a request with no body would have loaded with mode "" instead of
  "none", silently, and the suite would have stayed green - I verified that by
  making exactly that change afterwards: 0 tests failed.

  So: three tests added pinning the full canonicalisation. Controls now: "" not
  becoming "none" fails 2, dropping the urlencoded aliases fails 1, "binary" not
  mapping to "file" fails 1.

  THE LESSON IS ABOUT SIMILAR NAMES, NOT ABOUT REGEXES. Two functions in
  different packages with near-identical names and DIFFERENT behaviour is a trap
  that survives compilation. Before redirecting any call to a similarly-named
  function in another package, read both bodies. The compiler will not help.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 14 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- internal/cookiejar (DONE) — US-061 groundwork ---

  442 lines of cookie storage and sending rules out of app.go: domain and path
  matching, __Host-/__Secure- prefixes, SameSite, expiry, and what a response is
  allowed to set. app.go: 34,890 -> 34,424.

  These are SECURITY rules and their failure mode is the quiet kind: a domain or
  path match that is too loose sends a cookie somewhere it should never go, the
  request succeeds, and nothing in the UI shows it happened.

  !!! FIVE OF THEM WERE UNTESTED !!!

    cookiePathMatch always true       -> 0 failing
    PrefixValid always true           -> 0 failing
    Expired always false              -> 0 failing
    domain match by unanchored suffix -> 0 failing
    HostOnly ignored                  -> 0 failing

  Only the domain-match function had any coverage at all (2 failing), and even
  that did not pin the anchoring: replacing `host == domain || HasSuffix(host,
  "."+domain)` with a bare HasSuffix passed. That bare version sends a cookie
  for example.com to notexample.com and evil-example.com.

  Eight tests added. All five controls now fail 1-2 tests each.

  The path case is the one I would not have thought to write without the
  control: /api must NOT match /apikeys. A HasPrefix without the segment
  boundary looks correct and is wrong for exactly one shape of path.

  A NEW SHAPE OF RENAME BUG: renaming cookieID -> cookiejar.ID rewrote a
  PARAMETER named cookieID in DeleteCookie(cookieID string). Previous instances
  hit declarations and struct fields; this one hit a parameter. The general
  rule now has three faces: renaming an identifier everywhere catches its
  declaration, its struct-field namesakes, AND any parameter or local that
  shares the name. Only the last two compile.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 15 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223.

--- internal/scalar tests — the leaf package had none ---

  internal/scalar is what almost every other package depends on, and it had no
  tests at all. Control found ShellSingleQuote entirely unverified: replacing
  its escaping with a plain pair of quotes failed NOTHING.

  That one is not just a correctness gap. ShellSingleQuote quotes values into
  the curl and grpcurl commands the app generates FOR THE USER TO PASTE INTO A
  SHELL. A value containing an apostrophe does not render oddly - it ends the
  quoted argument, and everything after it is read by the shell as syntax.

  15 tests added. Controls: escaping removed fails 2, SanitizeFilename
  passthrough fails 2, the empty-name default fails 2, rejecting
  interface-keyed maps fails 1.

  !!! I WROTE THE SAME BAD TEST AGAIN, AND THE SUITE CAUGHT ME !!!

  My first injection test asserted the result did NOT contain "'; rm". It failed
  on correct code, because the POSIX escape idiom ('"'"') legitimately contains
  those bytes. This is exactly the US-054 mistake from earlier in this session:
  searching for a string that appears in the CONTENT rather than checking the
  property.

  Replaced with a ROUND TRIP: the test parses the quoted word the way a shell
  would and asserts it comes back as the original string, for six payloads
  including $(whoami) and backticks. That is the actual property - not "the
  dangerous text is absent" but "a shell reads this as one literal word".

  Worth noting it failed LOUDLY rather than passing vacuously. A wrong assertion
  that fails is cheap; the four signer tautologies were wrong assertions that
  passed, which cost far more.

  PACKAGES STILL WITHOUT THEIR OWN TESTS: grpcexec, importers, store/bru.
  All three are exercised heavily through package main's suite - bru's controls
  failed 44 and 33 tests - so they are covered, just not locally.

--- Assertion evaluation tests — the worst gap of the session ---

  Control: making endsWith behave as startsWith failed NO test. The individual
  assertion operators were unverified.

  This is the worst shape of bug this codebase can have, and it is worth being
  precise about why. LiteAPI is an API TESTING tool. An operator that silently
  behaves as a different operator makes a user's assertion report PASSED when
  the response did not satisfy it. There is nothing to notice: no error, no
  warning, a green run. A tool that reports green wrongly is worse than one
  that crashes, because the crash gets fixed.

  Nine cases added covering every operator, plus the pairs that actually
  DISTINGUISH them - "hello" starts with "hel" but does not end with it, and
  vice versa. A test that only asserts endsWith("hello","llo") is true cannot
  tell endsWith from a broken implementation that checks the prefix.

  Also pinned: an UNKNOWN operator must fail, not pass. A typo in the operator
  field defaulting to true would report success forever.

  And: a DISABLED assertion is passed through untouched, not marked passed - a
  row the user switched off must not count towards a green run.

  Controls now: endsWith->startsWith fails 8, notEquals inverted fails 7,
  unknown operator passing fails 7.

  RUNNING TALLY of untested contracts found by negative control this session:
    SigV4 verification tautology          PAC DIRECT branch
    OAuth 1.0a tautology + constant nonce  Digest MD5 tautology
    WSSE tautology                         clone* deep-copy contract
    WebSocket binary frame opcode          5 cookie security rules
    ShellSingleQuote escaping              assertion operators
    YAML body-mode canonicalisation

  Sixteen, in code that was fully green the whole time.

--- Recovery path containment tests — and a control that was NOT a bug ---

  Control found validateRecoveryWorkspaceID untested: disabling it failed
  nothing. I nearly wrote that up as a path-traversal hole.

  IT IS NOT ONE. The workspace id is SHA-256 hashed before it becomes a
  directory name, so "../../etc/passwd" cannot escape regardless of what the
  validator does. The validator is defence in depth; the hashing is the control.

  This is worth recording as its own lesson: a control failing zero tests means
  the behaviour is UNVERIFIED, not that it is BROKEN. Reading the code before
  reporting is what separates the two, and I have been finding real gaps often
  enough this session that assuming the worst would have been easy.

  SO THE TESTS PIN THE PROPERTY, NOT THE MECHANISM. Six tests assert that every
  derived path stays inside its root, for nine hostile ids including
  "../../etc/passwd", absolute paths, Windows separators, a newline and a
  512-character id. They do not assert that hashing is used.

  That distinction has teeth. Controls:
    remove the HASHING (use the raw id as the path)  -> 4 findings
    accept any entry id (drop the UUID check)        -> 9 findings
    accept a blank workspace id                      -> 5 findings

  A test written against the validator would have passed while someone removed
  the hashing. This one fails whichever way containment is lost.

  Two more properties pinned while there: different workspaces never share a
  root (one workspace's recovery data overwriting another's), and the same id
  always resolves to the same root (recovery data becoming unreachable after a
  restart).

--- Draft-guard validation tests — the close-the-app data path ---

  Control: both uniqueUnsavedDrafts and validateUnsavedDraftsLocked were
  unverified. Disabling either failed no test, and the draft_guard_test.go
  filter genuinely runs 7 tests, so this was not a filter artefact - I checked,
  because a -run pattern matching nothing reports 0 exactly the same way.

  These sit on the path that runs WHEN THE USER CLOSES THE APP WITH UNSAVED
  WORK. The failure modes are the kind nobody sees coming:
    - a draft listed twice gets written twice
    - a draft failing validation silently is a request whose edits never reach
      disk while the app reports a clean save

  Seven tests added. Controls: dedup disabled -> 2 findings, dedup keyed on the
  item id alone -> 4, the no-draft check disabled -> 2.

  THE SECOND CONTROL IS THE INTERESTING ONE. Keying dedup on ItemID alone looks
  harmless and is not: the same item id in two different collections is two
  different requests, and collapsing them loses one request's edits entirely.
  The test that catches it is four lines and I would not have written it without
  asking "what if the key were simpler".

  Also pinned: first-occurrence ORDER survives dedup, so drafts save in the
  order the user's tabs are in rather than whatever a map iteration produces.

--- Response timing units + a sweep that found things ALREADY covered ---

  Control: swapping Milliseconds() for Microseconds() failed no test. The field
  is TotalMs and the UI renders it as milliseconds, so a unit slip shows a 200ms
  request as 200000ms - wrong to anyone who looks, and nothing fails.

  Five tests added. Controls: wrong unit -> 6 findings, download measured from
  the request start instead of the first byte -> 2, the availability flag
  always set -> 2.

  The download one is the subtle one. A zero duration and an UNMEASURED phase
  are the same number, so DownloadAvailable is the only thing separating "this
  took no time" from "Go never reported this". Measuring from the start instead
  of the first byte quietly attributes the entire wait to download, and the
  number still looks plausible.

  THE SWEEP ALSO FOUND THINGS PROPERLY COVERED, which is worth recording so the
  session's tally is not read as "everything was broken":

    shared-state cookie encryption disabled     -> 2 failing  (covered)
    shared-state preference encryption disabled -> 2 failing  (covered)
    workspace symlink resolution skipped        -> 1 failing  (covered)
    recovery entry-id UUID validation disabled  -> 1 failing  (covered)
    assertion comparison always true            -> 1 failing  (covered)
    grpcurl full-method assembly                -> 1 failing  (covered)
    unix socket path extraction                 -> 1 failing  (covered)
    bru ParseSections / ParseScalarMap          -> 44 / 33    (covered well)

  The workspace symlink one deserves a note: canonicalWorkspaceIdentity resolves
  symlinks so two paths to the same workspace get the SAME lock. Without it an
  alias would take a second lock and two windows could open one workspace at
  once. That IS tested, and it is the kind of subtle property that usually is
  not - someone thought about it.

--- US-068 — internal/scripting (DONE). The largest story in the plan. ---

  14,829 lines: the goja runtime, the pm.* and bru.* API surfaces, the Node
  shims, the test/assertion vocabulary and the sandbox.
  app.go: 34,424 -> 19,701. It has now gone from ~44,000 to under 20,000.

  I had deferred this twice as "too large to finish safely". What made it
  possible was that the earlier extractions had already moved most of its
  dependencies out: cookies, interp, scalar, types, codegen. The external-need
  count had fallen from 22 to a handful.

  The export surface was found by a COMPILER LOOP, not by reading: build, read
  `undefined: X`, capitalise X in scripting, qualify it in main, repeat. Three
  passes for the build, three more for the tests.

  !!! THE REAL LESSON: THIS PACKAGE CONTAINS JAVASCRIPT, AND A GO REGEX
      CANNOT TELL !!!

  Three separate corruptions, none caught by the compiler, all caught by tests:

  1. `new types.Response(...)` INSIDE the fetch shim's JS source. 4 sites.
     Symptom: ReferenceError at <eval>:288.
  2. The lodash shim's `merge` renamed to `Merge` by the export loop, so
     `_.merge` became undefined. 3 sites.
  3. `"types.Response"` and `"types.Environment"` inside DOUBLE-QUOTED Go
     strings listing JS-visible global names. My first scan only checked
     backtick strings and missed these entirely.

  Every one compiled cleanly. Go has no idea that a string is a program.

  AND TWO OF MY OWN SCANS WERE WRONG BEFORE THEY WERE RIGHT. A naive backtick
  pairing reported 167 corruptions (it was reading Go struct tags as raw
  strings) and later reported 39 renamed JS identifiers (same cause). The
  correct scanner - the one tracking raw strings, interpreted strings and
  comments, the same logic as tools/extraction/split.py - found 4, then 1.
  A scanner that over-reports is not conservative; it hides the real hits.

  Controls after: breaking pm.test assertions fails 5.

  Gates: go build, go vet, go test ok across 16 packages; svelte-check 209/0/0;
  eslint 0; npm test 223/223; vite build ok.

--- internal/openapisync (DONE) — US-034 follow-on ---

  1,011 lines of OpenAPI spec re-sync out of app.go, including the merge rules.
  app.go: 19,701 -> 18,657.

  The merge rules are the reason this is worth isolating. A re-sync must pick up
  the spec's STRUCTURAL changes without discarding the VALUES the user typed
  into the request. Getting that backwards silently reverts someone's work, and
  the request still looks fine.

  Controls confirm both directions are covered: discarding user values on
  re-sync fails 1 test, discarding JSON body edits fails 1.

  internal/types stays a leaf, again. AssignExampleIDs needed deterministicID,
  so types now carries a local copy alongside firstNonEmpty and newIDLocal.
  That is three small duplications to keep the dependency graph acyclic, and it
  is the right trade: types is imported by every other package, and putting
  scalar underneath it would put a package between types and everything else.

  RUNNING TOTAL for the whole extraction effort:
    app.go   ~44,000 -> 18,657 lines   (down 58%)
    266 *App methods remain, which is the natural floor: a method cannot move
    to another package without its receiver, and App is what Wails binds.

    internal/scripting   14,829     internal/importers    2,632
    internal/auth/awsv4   1,797     internal/types        ~1,400
    internal/transport    1,116     internal/store/bru    ~1,065
    internal/openapisync  1,011     internal/grpcexec       912
    internal/store/yamlstore 649    internal/cookiejar      442
    internal/interp        ~400     internal/codegen       ~380
    internal/scalar         220     internal/auth/oauth1    407
    internal/auth/digest    159     internal/auth/wsse       41

--- US-070 completed — the .bru WRITE side joins the read side ---

  1,208 more lines out of app.go: .bru serialisation, environment import/export,
  and the Postman parity ledger. app.go: 18,657 -> 17,314.

  Read and write live in ONE package on purpose. A format has exactly one
  definition of what a block looks like, and splitting the reader from the
  writer is how the two drift until a file this app wrote can no longer be read
  back by it.

  AN IMPORT CYCLE, AND WHAT IT ACTUALLY MEANT. bru began importing yamlstore
  while yamlstore already imported bru. Go refused it, and Go was right: the
  functions causing it (ParseYAMLEnvironmentContent, parseImportedGlobalEnvironments,
  environmentFromYAMLMap) read YAML, so they belonged in yamlstore all along. I
  had put them in bru earlier only because bru happened to need them first. The
  cycle was the compiler pointing at a misplacement, not an obstacle to route
  around.

  !!! THE STRING-BOUNDARY MISTAKE, TWICE IN ONE FILE !!!

  1. `"Imported types.Environment"` - the prefixer hit a string literal again,
     the same class as the scripting JS corruption.
  2. Then my FIX was worse. A regex meant to strip qualifiers "inside strings"
     matched `", scalar.FirstNonEmpty(auth.APILocation, "` - which is Go code
     BETWEEN two string literals, not a string. It stripped 16 real call sites.
     Caught by the compiler; restored by re-qualifying, which was unambiguous
     because FirstNonEmpty appears nowhere in the prose.

  The lesson is the one from the scripting extraction, sharpened: a regex cannot
  tell a string from code, and a regex written to CLEAN UP after that mistake
  cannot either. Only a real scanner can, and I have one in
  tools/extraction/split.py - I should have reached for it both times.

  I ALSO FIXED THE TOOL THIS TIME. move_helper.py's signature parser anchored on
  the FIRST '{', which is inside `[]interface{}` and `map[string]interface{}`.
  That produced `func yamlVariables(...) []map[string]interface { return ... }`
  - the identical failure as listValue in US-071, which I recorded and then did
  not fix. It now anchors on the last brace of the signature line.

  Controls: the bru block writer emitting nothing fails 3; dropping existing
  secrets on merge fails 2.

=== EXTRACTION STATUS — a full -race pass, and where the floor is ===

  go test -race -count=1, checking ALL THREE signals (races, test failures AND
  build failures - the lesson from the flake at the top of this file):

    ./internal/...   exit 0, 0 races, 0 failures
    .  (main)        exit 0, 0 races, 0 failures, 75.1s

  FINAL SHAPE
    app.go        ~44,000 -> 17,314 lines  (down 61%)
    internal/     29,466 lines across 17 packages, + 2,656 lines of tests
    808 Go tests

  WHERE THE FLOOR IS. Of app.go's remaining 17,314 lines, roughly 8,341 are
  inside methods on *App. Those cannot move: a method needs its receiver, and
  App is what Wails binds. The other ~8,974 are free functions, but the easy
  boundaries are gone - the next candidate (collection disk I/O, 619 lines) has
  EIGHTEEN external dependencies pulling in a web of collection-config readers.

  That is the honest stopping point for mechanical extraction. What is left
  needs the App methods themselves broken up first, which is a different and
  much more invasive change than moving free functions between packages.

  WHAT MADE THE BIG ONE POSSIBLE. US-068 (scripting, 14,829 lines) failed my own
  feasibility check twice before succeeding. Nothing about it changed - what
  changed is that cookies, interp, scalar, types and codegen had moved out from
  under it, so its external-need count fell from 22 to a handful. Ordering was
  the whole trick: extract the leaves first and the trunk becomes movable.

--- US-031 (partial) — MEASURED IN A REAL BROWSER AT LAST ---

  The blocker on this story was never the code, it was verification: the
  criterion names a 500-request fixture, a DOM node count and keyboard nav, and
  none of that can be checked by reading. It turns out `wails dev` serves the
  app on localhost:34115 WITH the Go bridge attached, so an ordinary browser can
  drive the real application - 188 bound methods, live state, real collections.
  That unblocked it.

  METHOD: generated a 500-request fixture on disk (20 folders x 25, the same
  shape as the sidebarTree unit tests use, so the two agree on what "500
  requests" means), opened it through the app's own OpenCollection (441ms), and
  measured.

  BASELINE, all folders expanded:
      6,251 sidebar DOM nodes
      3,154 sidebar buttons
  Of those buttons, ~3,000 were per-row CONTEXT MENUS. Every request row keeps
  its Reveal/Code/Info/Rename/Clone/Delete buttons mounted, because <details>
  does NOT remove its children when closed - it hides them. Half the sidebar's
  DOM was menus nobody could see.

  AFTER rendering the menu body only while its disclosure is open:
      3,239 nodes   (-48%)
        687 buttons (-78%)
          0 menu buttons present while closed

  VERIFIED IN THE BROWSER, not inferred:
    500 request rows still render
    opening a menu yields exactly the 6 actions; closing removes them again
    clicking a row opens the request (URL became https://example.test/f3/i7)
    tab order is row -> its disclosure -> next row
    Enter on a row activates it
    the disclosure is focusable and opens from the keyboard with all 6 actions

  So the story's "keyboard nav + context menus still work" is now MEASURED.

  WHAT IS NOT DONE, PLAINLY: the node count is REDUCED, not BOUNDED. 3,239 is
  still O(number of requests). Truly bounding it needs the row-window
  virtualization - flattenSidebar and computeWindow exist and are unit-tested,
  but wiring them means flattening ~198 lines of three-level nested markup
  (collection -> folder -> request, each with its own header, drag targets and
  menus). That is a large rewrite and I did not attempt it in the same pass as
  a change I could verify cleanly.

  Fixture and dev server were both removed afterwards; the workspace is back to
  just Sample API.

  ONE REAL FINDING ON THE SIDE: the regenerated Wails bindings had drifted.
  CodeGenerationTarget became codegen.Target when internal/codegen was
  extracted, and I had not regenerated since. `wails dev` did it, svelte-check
  passes against the new shape, and the corrected binding is committed.

--- US-031 COMPLETE — and it uncovered a real bug the story did not mention ---

  Row windowing wired into the sidebar. Measured against the 500-request
  fixture in the running app:

      6,251 sidebar DOM nodes  ->  469     (bounded, not merely reduced)
        500 rows always mounted ->  35-47  depending on scroll position

  The node count no longer depends on collection size, which is what the story
  asked for.

  HOW, without rewriting 198 lines of nested markup: only the INNERMOST request
  loop is windowed. Each group asks for its own slice using its offset in a flat
  row coordinate (sidebarGroupOffset walks the same order the markup renders, so
  the two cannot disagree), and rows outside the slice become two spacer divs so
  the scrollbar keeps the size it would have if everything were rendered.

  !!! THE SIDEBAR COULD NOT SCROLL AT ALL, AND THAT WAS PRE-EXISTING !!!

  While verifying, section.collections reported scrollHeight == clientHeight
  (921 == 921) with 14,683px of content. Setting scrollTop did nothing. With a
  large collection, EVERY REQUEST PAST THE FIRST SCREEN WAS UNREACHABLE.

  I did not assume it was mine. I stashed my change, reloaded, and measured the
  original: 500 rows rendered, scrollHeight 921, scrollTop stuck at 0, article
  clipped at 831px. Identical. Pre-existing, and my windowing had merely
  inherited it.

  CAUSE: `.collections { display: grid; overflow: auto }` with
  `.collections article { overflow: hidden }`. A grid with a definite height
  gives each article an equal share of the track; the article then clips its
  overflow instead of growing, so the section never overflows and never scrolls.

  FIX: align-content: start + grid-auto-rows: min-content, so each collection
  sizes to its content and the section scrolls. After: scrollHeight 14,760 vs
  clientHeight 921, and scrolling to the bottom reaches Req 19-24 - the 500th
  request, which no user could previously get to.

  RE-VERIFIED ON WINDOWED ROWS: menu opens to exactly 6 actions and closes to 0,
  a row is focusable, Enter activates it (url became .../f10/i5), and tab order
  is row -> disclosure -> next row. So "keyboard nav + context menus still work"
  holds after windowing, not just before it.

  This is the second time this session that verifying a change surfaced a defect
  the change was not about. The first was the -race flake. Both came from
  measuring rather than reasoning.

--- US-037 (substantially done) — tokens migrated, verified across all 12 themes ---

  41 tokens added (--space-*, --font-size-*, --radius-*) and 606 px literals
  migrated to them. app-wide, in one sweep.

  WHY THE TOKENS ARE NAMED BY PIXEL VALUE (--space-8, not --space-md): this
  migration has to render IDENTICALLY in 12 themes, and a value-named token
  makes every replacement provably a no-op. Renaming to a semantic scale is a
  different change with a different risk, and it belongs where the design is
  decided rather than inside a mechanical sweep.

  WHAT WAS DELIBERATELY NOT MIGRATED. 618 px literals remain, and they are the
  right ones to leave:
      min-height, width, max-height, grid-template-columns, box-shadow offsets
  Those are one-off LAYOUT dimensions - "this panel is 560px" is not a scale
  value, and forcing it into a token would be cargo-culting the pattern. The
  count also includes the 41 token definitions themselves.

  Migration was PROPERTY-AWARE, which is the part that matters. A blind
  "8px -> var(--space-8)" would have rewritten `font-size: 8px` and `border: 1px`
  into spacing tokens. Only gap/padding/margin/inset take space tokens,
  only font-size takes type tokens, only border-radius takes radius tokens.

  !!! VERIFIED BY COMPUTED-STYLE DIFF, WHICH IS STRICTER THAN THE SCREENSHOT
      THE STORY ASKED FOR !!!

  For all 388 elements x 15 box/type properties x 12 themes, the computed values
  were fingerprinted before and after:

      baseline  hash -1563842672  (identical in all 12 themes, as expected:
                                   the themes vary colour, not spacing)
      after     hash -1563842672  in all 12

  A screenshot diff can be defeated by antialiasing noise or pass on a
  sub-pixel shift. Computed styles cannot.

  AND THE CHECK ITSELF WAS CONTROLLED. A fingerprint that never changes proves
  nothing, so I shifted ONE token by ONE pixel (--space-8: 8px -> 9px) and
  confirmed the hash moved, then restored it and confirmed it came back. Same
  discipline as the Go negative controls: verify the instrument before trusting
  the reading.

--- US-019 — MEASURED. The analysis above was right about the risk and WRONG
    about the size of the prize. ---

  I previously wrote "US-018 already banked the large win here... what remains
  is object construction, and pooling it is not obviously safe." The first half
  understated things. Runtime construction is not a rounding error:

      BenchmarkNewScriptRuntime          ~0.5-0.6 ms    734 KB    8,482 allocs
      BenchmarkPostResponsePhaseRuntimes ~1.5-1.7 ms  2,203 KB   25,448 allocs

  The post-response phase builds THREE runtimes back to back (response
  variables, post script, tests). Collapsing those to one would save roughly
  1.5 MB and 17,000 allocations PER REQUEST. That is worth doing.

  Benchmarks committed as internal/scripting/runtime_cost_test.go so the number
  can be re-checked rather than re-argued.

  WHAT STILL BLOCKS IT, AND IT IS NOT FEASIBILITY. The three runtimes are built
  from DIFFERENT variable maps:
      RunPostResponseVariables            scriptVars.Combined
      RunPostResponseScriptWithJarStateMeta   vars
      EvaluateRuntimeTestsWithJarStateMeta    vars
  and RunPostResponseVariables additionally sets every combined variable as a
  GLOBAL, plus $bru and $req. Sharing one runtime therefore changes what a
  post-response script and a tests script can SEE. That is a semantics change,
  not a mechanical refactor.

  It is probably a change in the RIGHT direction - in Postman, state a
  post-response script sets is visible to tests, and today it is not - but
  "which variables should a tests script see" is a product decision about
  script compatibility, not something to settle inside a performance refactor.
  The order the three run in also becomes observable where today it is not.

  MY RECOMMENDATION, stated plainly: do the 3 -> 1 consolidation, accept the
  visibility change deliberately, and pin the new semantics in tests FIRST -
  what a tests script sees from the post script, and in what order. The
  regression net already exists (script_isolation_test.go, 5 tests, written
  before any of this). What does not exist is a decision about the semantics,
  and that is not mine to make silently.

  The sync.Pool half of the story remains infeasible as written, for the reason
  recorded above: every shim closes over per-request state, so a pooled runtime
  would leak the previous request's url, headers and env on any missed reset.

--- US-022 — gRPC event push. DONE. The blocker was imaginary. ---

  Recorded months of entries ago as "STILL OWED — needs a local gRPC streaming
  server". It did not. app_test.go has run one the whole time:
  startReflectedTestService, with a real FullDuplexCall bidi handler already
  used by three other tests.

  So the blocker was a missing TEST, not missing infrastructure, and I had
  written it down in a way that made it look like an external dependency. Worth
  remembering: "blocked on X" deserves re-checking before it is inherited,
  because the note outlives the condition that produced it.

  Two tests added, mirroring the WebSocket pair that was verified long ago:
    - every appended event is pushed, with CONTIGUOUS indices anchored to the
      real slice position (that anchoring is what lets the frontend detect it
      missed one) and total = index+1
    - the push count equals the number of events actually appended, so the
      frontend and the timeline cannot disagree about what went over the wire

  CONTROLS, all landing-checked:
      grpc push silently dropped (if false)  -> 2 failing
      index pinned to 0                      -> 2 failing
      semantically identical rewrite         -> 0 failing

  That last one matters as a control OF the controls: a no-op edit fails
  nothing, so the two failures above are the tests reacting to real changes
  rather than to any edit at all.

  Also clean under -race.

  THE PLAN IS NOW COMPLETE except for one thing that is not mine to decide:
  US-019's 3->1 runtime consolidation changes what post-response and tests
  scripts can SEE. Measured, recommended, and left for a product call.

      DevTools    ConsoleTab, PerformanceTab, TerminalTab, RequestDetailsPanel
