--- US-019 — the decision is now VISIBLE. Four semantics tests added. ---

  The consolidation still needs a product call, but the part of my own
  recommendation that did NOT need one is now done: "pin the new semantics in
  tests FIRST — what a tests script sees from the post script, and in what
  order."

  The existing script_isolation_test.go pins pre-request -> tests isolation,
  cross-request isolation, bru.setVar persistence, sandbox mode and the cookie
  jar. It does NOT pin the three things a 3->1 consolidation would actually
  change. Those now have tests:

    post-response global is NOT visible to tests      (isolation today)
    post-response bru.setVar IS visible to tests      (the supported channel)
    the phases run post-script THEN tests             (order, unobservable today)
    a tests-script global does not survive the request

  WHY THIS IS WORTH DOING BEFORE THE DECISION, not after: today those four
  sentences are invisible. Nothing anywhere states them, so a consolidation
  would change them silently and user scripts would discover it in the field.
  With the tests present the change becomes a DIFF — whoever consolidates sees
  exactly which visibility rules they are altering and has to update the
  sentences deliberately.

  CONTROLLED, not assumed. I simulated the consolidation's visible effect by
  planting the post script's global into the tests runtime — which is precisely
  what one shared runtime does — and the isolation test failed. So these are a
  real tripwire, not documentation that happens to pass.

  The second test is the one that keeps the set honest: a consolidation that
  isolated too hard would break the setVar handoff while making every isolation
  test greener. Both directions are pinned, the same way the original US-019
  suite pinned bru.setVar persistence against over-isolation.

--- Coverage sweep — and a REAL BUG in PAC timeRange ---

  Measured coverage properly for the first time. The naive per-package number
  is misleading: `go test ./internal/...` reports importers, bru, grpcexec and
  openapisync at 0.0%, which reads as untested. They are not — they are
  exercised through package main's suite. With -coverpkg=./internal/... the
  real figure is 78.2% overall and those packages sit at 80-85%.

  Worth remembering: per-package coverage measures where the TESTS live, not
  what the CODE runs. Reading it the other way would have sent me rewriting
  tests that already exist.

  The lowest real numbers were transport (61%) and yamlstore (58%). Every
  uncovered function in transport was a PAC date/time predicate — the functions
  a proxy auto-config script calls to decide routing. Ten of them, zero tests.

  !!! pacTimeRange WAS WRONG FOR THE TWO MOST COMMON FORMS !!!

      if len(args) <= 3 { return seconds == point(args) }

  So timeRange(9, 17) — the standard way a PAC script says "business hours" —
  matched only at exactly 09:17:00, and timeRange(9) only at 09:00:00. A script
  gating on business hours would essentially never match and would fall through
  to whatever it returned otherwise. The symptom is not an error: it is traffic
  quietly going direct when policy said proxy, or through a proxy that should
  not have seen it.

  Fixed to the semantics every PAC implementation uses: 1 arg covers the whole
  hour, 2 args run from hour1:00:00 through hour2:59:59, and the 4-argument form
  is inclusive to h2:m2:59. The 3-argument h,m,s instant keeps equality, which
  is the one form it was right for.

  FOUND BY WRITING THE TEST AGAINST THE SPEC, NOT THE CODE. Had I read the
  implementation and asserted what it did, this would have passed and stayed
  broken. That is the same failure mode as the four signer tautologies, reached
  from the opposite direction.

  AND ONE OF MY OWN EXPECTATIONS WAS WRONG. I first asserted the range ended at
  17:00:00. The code — once fixed — was right that it runs to 17:59:59, because
  "9 to 17" naming a single instant would make the form useless. I corrected the
  TEST there, and said so, rather than bending the code to a bad assertion.

  Controls: reverting to exact-instant matching fails 1, collapsing the
  single-hour range fails 1, accepting any weekday name fails 2.

--- PAC fallback path tested — the net under a broken PAC file ---

  pacReturnDirectives was the other 0% function worth chasing. It is the
  FALLBACK: when a PAC script cannot be evaluated (syntax error, or a feature
  goja does not provide), pacDirectivesForURL scrapes the `return "..."`
  statements out of the source text so the file's stated intent still applies.

  That net matters because of what happens without it. A broken PAC file that
  yields no directive does not fail loudly — traffic goes DIRECT, bypassing a
  proxy the file exists to enforce.

  Six cases added: both quote styles, semicolon lists split, several returns in
  order, no-return yielding nothing, a genuinely unparseable script recovering
  its directive, and — the other direction — evaluation winning over scraping,
  so a script whose logic returns DIRECT is not overridden by an earlier PROXY
  line in its own source.

  Controls: disabling the fallback fails 1, discarding the evaluation result
  fails 1, not splitting semicolon lists fails 1.

  transport uncovered functions: 10 -> 4. The remaining four
  (transportWithPACProxy, pacLocalIPAddress, pacDateRange, pacDateBetween) all
  need either a live network interface or a controlled wall clock, and
  pacDateRange reads time.Now() directly rather than taking it as a parameter
  the way pacTimeRange does. Testing those properly means changing the
  signatures, which is a refactor rather than a test, so I have left them and
  said so rather than writing something that passes without asserting anything.

--- A SECOND PAC BUG: dateRange's year branch was unreachable ---

  Same family as the timeRange bug, found the same way — by testing the intended
  behaviour rather than the written behaviour.

      startDay, startErr := strconv.Atoi(...)
      endDay, endErr := strconv.Atoi(...)
      if startErr == nil && endErr == nil {
          return day >= startDay && day <= endDay     // returns for ANY two ints
      }
      ... if startYear > 31 && endYear > 31 { ... }   // UNREACHABLE

  The day comparison returned for any two integers, so the year branch below it
  could never run. dateRange(2020, 2030) was evaluated as "day >= 2020", which
  is false on every date there has ever been. A PAC script gating on a year
  range never matched and never said so.

  Confirmed by probe before fixing: dateRange(2020, 2030) at 2026-07-26
  returned false while every other form returned true.

  FIXED by telling days and years apart on magnitude, which is the only signal
  PAC gives and exactly what the dead `> 31` guard was reaching for — it was
  just written below an unconditional return.

  12 cases added covering days, day ranges, month names, month ranges, year
  ranges and the trailing GMT marker. Controls: restoring the dead branch fails
  1, removing the year branch fails 1, consuming GMT as a bound fails 1.

  transport uncovered functions: 10 -> 2. The last two need a live network
  interface (pacLocalIPAddress) or a real transport chain
  (transportWithPACProxy).

  TWO REAL BUGS FROM ONE COVERAGE MEASUREMENT. Both were in code with no tests,
  both silent, both in the path that decides where a user's traffic goes. The
  coverage number itself was never the point — it just pointed at the ten
  functions nobody had ever asserted anything about.

--- Credential preservation across an OpenAPI re-sync — was 0% covered ---

  mergeOAuth1Preserving, mergeOAuth2Preserving and mergeAWSV4Preserving each had
  zero tests. Their whole job is to keep what the USER typed when a spec is
  re-imported over their request.

  The failure mode is the worst kind of silent: the re-sync appears to succeed,
  the request still says oauth2, and the client secret is simply gone. Nothing
  errors. The user finds out when the next send returns 401.

  The sibling merges (headers, params, JSON bodies) were already covered — an
  earlier control confirmed discarding user values there fails a test. These
  three were the gap.

  Six tests added. Controls: taking the OAuth2 secret from the spec fails 1,
  the AWS secret key fails 1, the OAuth1 token secret fails 1.

  !!! AND A CONTROL CAUGHT MY OWN TEST BEING TOO WEAK !!!

  Reversing the precedence — FirstNonEmpty(spec, existing) instead of
  (existing, spec) — failed NOTHING at first. Because I set each credential on
  ONE side only, the tests could not tell "existing wins" from "either wins".

  That is exactly the endsWith/startsWith flaw from the assertion operators,
  in a different place: an assertion that does not DISTINGUISH the behaviour
  from its plausible alternative measures nothing, even though it passes and
  looks specific.

  Fixed by setting both sides and asserting the user's value survives. Now
  reversing precedence on ClientID fails 1 and on the AWS region fails 1 — and
  the case is the realistic one, because a spec re-import really does carry a
  client id that must not overwrite the user's.

  0%-covered functions across internal/: 30 -> 27. Coverage 78.5%.

--- .bru assertion parsing — the parser/evaluator contract nobody checked ---

  parseBruAssertions and bruAssertionOperator were both at 0%. They sit on a
  CROSS-MODULE contract that nothing verified: whatever operator the parser
  emits has to be one the evaluator understands, or the assertion silently
  evaluates FALSE.

  That path is real. A Bruno collection imported into LiteAPI carries assertions
  written in Bruno's vocabulary, and an operator that survives the parse but
  means nothing to the evaluator becomes a check the user wrote that ALWAYS
  fails, with nothing saying it was never understood.

  Six tests added, including one that asserts the contract directly: every
  shorthand the parser claims to translate must land on an operator the
  evaluator accepts. The evaluator's vocabulary is duplicated in the test rather
  than imported, because bru must not depend on scripting — and that test is
  precisely what catches the copy drifting.

  Controls: dropping the eq translation fails 3, mapping neq to equals fails 2,
  truncating a multi-word value to its first token fails 2, loading assertions
  disabled fails 2.

  The multi-word case is the one worth having. `contains hello world` truncated
  to `contains hello` still PASSES on a response the user meant to reject — a
  green check that verifies less than it claims.

  0%-covered functions: 30 -> 25. Coverage 78.6%. 18 packages green.

--- JSON Schema type matching — 0% covered, and "integer" is the subtle one ---

  jsonSchemaTypeMatches backs pm.response.to.have.jsonSchema and the ajv-style
  validation the script API exposes. Getting it wrong makes a schema assertion
  report the OPPOSITE of the truth — the same family as the assertion operators,
  where endsWith could behave as startsWith and nothing noticed.

  20 cases added. The one worth spelling out is "integer": JSON has a single
  numeric type, so a decoded document hands every number over as float64 and
  integer-ness is a property of the VALUE, not the Go type. A matcher answering
  on type alone would call 1.5 an integer. The code gets this right
  (math.Trunc(n) == n); nothing was checking that it did.

  Also pinned: an UNKNOWN schema type must not match. Defaulting to true would
  make a typo like "strng" validate everything it was written to constrain.

  Controls: integer accepting fractions -> 2 findings, object matching arrays
  -> 3, an unknown type passing -> 3.

  THREE OF MY FIRST CONTROLS WERE REFUSED BY THE RUNNER and that was correct:
  one did not compile (removing math.Trunc left the math import unused), two
  did not land (multi-line patterns need perl -0). Without the landing and
  compile checks all three would have reported "0 failing" and I would have
  read a working matcher as untested. That guard has now paid for itself
  repeatedly.

  0%-covered functions: 30 -> 24. Coverage 78.7%.

--- Multipart preservation across re-sync — the last of the preserving merges ---

  mergeFormPartListPreserving was the last uncovered member of the family. Same
  shape as the credential merges: the SPEC knows which parts exist, the USER
  knows what goes in them. A re-sync that adopts the spec wholesale wipes the
  file paths and values someone entered by hand — and the request still looks
  correct afterwards, because all the parts are there. They are just empty.

  Four tests. The one that earns its place is repeated names: a multipart body
  may legitimately repeat a name (several files under "file"), and the merge
  walks a per-name CURSOR so each repeat takes its own existing value. Matching
  by name alone would give every repeat the first one.

  Controls: not preserving the file path fails 2, freezing the cursor fails 1,
  ignoring preserveValues fails 1. The cursor control is the proof that the
  repeated-name test is doing work rather than restating the simple case.

  Also pinned in both directions, as with the credentials: preserveValues=false
  must adopt the spec unchanged, and a part the spec no longer declares must be
  DROPPED — otherwise a re-sync could never remove anything.

  openapisync 0%-covered functions: 6 -> 1. Overall 30 -> 23. Coverage 78.9%.

--- OAuth2 additional-parameter loading — 0% covered ---

  parseYAMLOAuth2AdditionalGroup and its inner parser load the extra headers,
  query parameters and body fields a user attached to an OAuth2 flow — the ones
  a provider demands and a spec cannot know: audience, resource, tenant, prompt.

  The failure is silent the way file loading always is. A parameter that loads
  with the WRONG PLACEMENT gets sent in the wrong part of the token request, and
  the provider answers with a generic invalid_request that names no field. One
  that loads disabled is simply never sent, and the flow fails for a reason the
  UI does not show.

  Six tests. The grouped form is the one that matters: three named buckets
  (headers / queryparams / body) each implying its own placement, so a bucket
  read with the wrong fallback sends a header as a query parameter.

  Controls: forcing every bucket to body fails 1, ignoring an explicit sendIn
  fails 1, dropping the secret flag fails 1, dropping the enabled flag fails 1.

  Also pinned: a row with NO enabled key defaults to enabled. Defaulting the
  other way would silently stop sending every parameter written before that flag
  existed — the kind of change that looks harmless in a diff and disables a
  user's auth on upgrade.

  0%-covered: 30 -> 21. Coverage 79.0%.

!!! SEVEN USER-VISIBLE STRINGS CORRUPTED BY MY OWN EXTRACTION !!!

  Found while writing tests for the environment loader. EnvironmentFromYAMLMap
  fell back to naming an unnamed environment "types.Environment".

  The type-prefixing sweep rewrote bare type names to `types.X` across ~30,000
  lines. A regex cannot tell a Go identifier from the same characters inside a
  string, so seven user-visible names were rewritten and SHIPPED:

      "Untitled Collection"   -> "Untitled types.Collection"
      "Insomnia Collection"   -> "Insomnia types.Collection"
      "Environment %d"        -> "types.Environment %d"
      "Imported Environment"  -> "Imported types.Environment"    (x2)
      "Environment"           -> "types.Environment"
      "Import/Environment/…"  -> "Import/types.Environment/…"    (ledger prose)

  Every one is a label shown after an import. Nothing failed, nothing logged,
  every gate stayed green — the tests assert on ids and counts, never on the
  fallback name a collection gets when its source has no title.

  I had "fixed" this class TWICE already (the scripting JS shims, then
  write.go's strings) and both times believed I was done. I was not, because
  both fixes were the same shape as the bug: a regex looking at text it cannot
  parse.

  THE FIX IS A TRIPWIRE, NOT ANOTHER SWEEP. extraction_string_integrity_test.go
  scans every non-test .go file with a REAL string scanner — tracking raw
  strings, interpreted strings and comments — and fails on any package
  qualifier inside a literal. It found the seventh one immediately, in ledger
  prose my manual grep had skimmed past.

  Controlled: re-introducing "types.Environment" makes it fail.

  THE LESSON, stated plainly because it cost seven defects: when a mechanical
  edit can corrupt data the compiler does not check, the remedy is a CHECK that
  runs forever, not a more careful pass. I wrote three careful passes. The
  check found what all three missed.

--- Was the FRONTEND corrupted the same way? No, and the reason generalises. ---

  The same extraction rewrote ~637 frontend references, so the same question
  applies. Checked against the BUILT BUNDLE rather than the source, because the
  bundle is what ships:

      types.X inside any string literal in dist/assets/*.js:  0

  THE ASYMMETRY IS THE LESSON. The two rewrites were not equally dangerous:

      Go:        Collection      -> types.Collection        (BARE WORD)
      frontend:  main.Collection -> types.Collection        (ANCHORED)

  The Go rewrite matched a common English word, so it hit "Untitled Collection",
  "Insomnia Collection", "Imported Environment" — real prose. The frontend
  rewrite required the literal prefix `main.`, which appears in TypeScript and
  essentially never in UI text. It could not corrupt a label even in principle.

  So: an anchored rename is qualitatively safer than a bare-word rename, not
  just marginally. When a mechanical edit must match a bare identifier that is
  also an ordinary word, that is the moment to reach for a scanner instead of a
  regex — or to accept that a tripwire will be needed afterwards.

  I ALSO MADE THE SAME SCANNING MISTAKE A THIRD TIME while checking this: my
  first frontend scan reported 138 "hits" that were TypeScript code between two
  quotes, exactly like the 167 and 39 false positives earlier. Checking the
  built artifact sidestepped the parsing problem entirely, which is what I
  should have reached for first.

--- fs.writeFile byte conversion — data corruption at rest, 0% covered ---

  scriptFSBytesFromInterfaceSlice and scriptFSBytesFromIndexedObject turn what a
  script hands to fs.writeFile — a Uint8Array, an ArrayBuffer view, or a plain
  array of numbers — into the bytes actually written to disk.

  The failure is corruption at rest. A conversion that truncates, misreads a
  numeric type or substitutes zeroes writes a file that EXISTS, has a plausible
  size, and contains the wrong bytes. Nothing errors and the script reports
  success. Nobody notices until the file is read back by something that cares.

  7 tests. Two are worth calling out:

    - goja hands numbers over as int, int64, float64 OR json.Number depending
      on how they were written. All four must survive; the test spells "Hello"
      using a different Go type for each letter, so dropping any one case is
      visible.

    - A HOLE in the array must become a zero byte, not a shorter file. Writing
      fewer bytes than asked silently mangles any offset-sensitive format —
      the file still opens, the header still parses, the data is shifted.

  Also pinned: both guards. A negative length would panic on make([]byte, n),
  and without the 64 MB cap a single script call could allocate the process to
  death.

  Controls: ignoring json.Number fails 1, removing the negative-length guard
  fails 1, removing the size cap fails 1, an off-by-one in the byte read fails 2.

  0%-covered: 30 -> 19. Coverage 79.2%.

--- Multipart LOADING (the read side of what was tested on the merge side) ---

  ParseYAMLMultipart was at 0%. It is the counterpart to
  mergeFormPartListPreserving tested earlier: one loads parts from disk, the
  other keeps them across a re-sync. Both were untested; now neither is.

  The load has a branch worth pinning: `type: file` changes WHICH FIELD the
  saved value lands in. A file part carries FilePath, a text part carries Value.
  Read the wrong one and the request still shows a part with the right name — it
  just sends the path as literal text, or sends nothing.

  A file path may also be stored under filePath, path OR value depending on
  which tool wrote the file. Missing one loads a part pointing at nothing.

  Five tests. Controls: reading file parts as text fails 2, honouring only the
  filePath key fails 1, dropping contentType fails 1, ignoring the enabled flag
  fails 1.

  A control was refused for not compiling (`if false` left partType unused), and
  rewriting it to compare against a dead string landed properly. That guard has
  now caught roughly a dozen invalid controls across this session — each one a
  "0 failing" I would otherwise have believed.

  0%-covered: 30 -> 18. Coverage 79.3%.

--- GraphQL and environment loading — yamlstore now has NO 0% functions ---

  The last two untested loaders in the package.

  applyYAMLGraphQL: the mode is set UNCONDITIONALLY, before the body is read, so
  a file with no body block still loads AS a GraphQL request. That matters —
  loaded as raw text instead, the editor opens it wrong and the user's query is
  one save away from being discarded. Also pinned: "post" in a hand-edited file
  upper-cases to POST, and absent keys do not clobber what the item already has.

  ParseYAMLEnvironmentContent: name, colour, variables and the secret flag. A
  lost secret flag renders the value unmasked in the UI.

  ONE TEST IS THERE FOR A SPECIFIC REASON. TestEnvironmentContentWithNoNameAtAll
  pins the literal "Environment" — the exact string my extraction corrupted to
  "types.Environment" and shipped. The integrity tripwire catches the pattern
  across the tree; this one catches THIS string behaviourally, from the outside,
  by loading a file and reading the name back. Two independent nets on the same
  defect, because it got through three careful passes.

  Controls: dropping graphql variables fails 1, not setting graphql mode fails
  2, not upper-casing the method fails 1, ignoring the env fallback name fails
  1, and re-corrupting the literal fails 1.

  store/yamlstore 0%-covered functions: 4 -> 0. Overall 30 -> 16. Coverage 79.4%.

--- Script error reporting — the one that could report GREEN on failure ---

  ScriptErrorResponse, scriptPromiseRejectionMessage and
  scriptFormInterfaceString were all at 0%.

  ScriptErrorResponse carries a property worth guarding hard: a script that
  THROWS must produce a FAILED test result. Flip that boolean and a broken
  pre-request script reports as a PASSING test. This is an API testing tool, and
  reporting green wrongly is the one outcome worse than crashing — the same
  failure the assertion operators had, in a different place.

  Six tests. Also pinned, because they are the difference between a useful
  failure and a baffling one:
    - the Error string names BOTH the phase and the cause, so the user knows
      which script broke rather than just seeing "boom"
    - Headers is an empty map, not nil, because the UI and the JSON encoder both
      walk it and nil renders as null where an object is expected
    - SentAt is set, or the timeline places the failure at the epoch
    - a promise rejected with undefined/null still yields a message rather than
      a blank failure

  Controls: reporting PASSING fails 1, omitting the phase fails 1, a nil headers
  map fails 1, a zero SentAt fails 1, a blank rejection message fails 1.

  0%-covered: 30 -> 13. Coverage 79.5%.

  WHAT IS LEFT AT 0% IS NOW ALMOST ALL ENVIRONMENT-BOUND: DNS lookups, OS
  version, per-platform CWD, a live network interface, a real transport chain.
  Those need either the network or a signature change to be testable, and I
  would rather say so than write tests that call them and assert nothing.

--- The last two PURE uncovered functions: scopes and drift fingerprints ---

  Both turned out to be about DETERMINISM, and both had the same latent hazard.

  openAPIScopeNames builds the space-separated OAuth2 scope string from a spec's
  `scopes` object — a Go map. Map iteration order is RANDOM per range, so
  without the sort the scope string would differ between two imports of the same
  spec: the request would be non-deterministic on disk and every save would
  produce a spurious diff. The test ranges 50 times and asserts the result never
  moves, which is the only way to catch an unsorted map walk reliably.

  openAPILocalDriftMultipartNames builds the multipart side of the drift
  comparison. It encodes name AND kind, so swapping a text field for a file
  upload registers as drift; without the kind the two would look identical and
  the drift report would say nothing changed.

  MY TEST WAS WRONG AGAIN, AND THE CODE WAS RIGHT. I asserted input order; the
  function sorts. Sorting is correct — a drift fingerprint must be
  order-independent or reordering two form parts would report as a change.
  Corrected the test and added the order-independence property explicitly,
  rather than bending the code to my assumption. Third time this session that a
  failing test was my error rather than the code's; each time the right move was
  to read the code and understand WHY before touching it.

  Controls: unsorted scopes fails 1, unsorted drift fails 2, undetected file
  kind fails 2.

  0%-covered: 30 -> 11, and what remains is environment-bound without exception:
  DNS resolution, OS version, per-platform CWD, a live network interface, a real
  transport chain, and two trivial setter seams.

--- Percent-decoding fallback and the path CWD helpers ---

  hexDigit backs safeDecodeURIComponent, which runs when url.PathUnescape
  REFUSES a value — a URL with a stray % or a malformed escape. That is a path
  real users hit: a URL pasted out of a log, or built by a script from an
  unencoded value. A mis-decode sends the request to a different URL than the
  one shown in the bar.

  The case that earns its test: %2F and %2f are BOTH legal, and a decoder
  handling only lower case silently leaves half of all escapes encoded. Upper
  case is easy to omit and impossible to notice.

  The CWD helpers are environment-dependent, so the tests assert INVARIANTS
  rather than values — never empty, each using the separator its platform
  namespace promises, and both describing the same directory. A script calling
  path.posix.resolve() against a Windows-shaped cwd builds a path that cannot be
  opened.

  Controls: rejecting upper-case hex fails 1, win32 cwd keeping forward slashes
  fails 1, posix cwd using backslashes fails 1.
