--- FRONTEND coverage, measured for the first time ---

  The Go sweep was worth repeating on the frontend, and node's
  --experimental-test-coverage does it without adding a dependency.

  The lib modules were already excellent — most at 100%. ONE real gap:

      tabLifecycle.ts    61% lines, 33% FUNCTIONS

  planUnsavedClose was entirely untested. The existing test file covered only
  requestDeletionAction beside it.

  THAT FUNCTION DECIDES WHETHER CLOSING TABS WARNS THE USER. If it reports
  requiresConfirmation: false while a draft is open, the tabs close silently and
  the edits are gone — no error, no undo prompt. The user finds out on reopening
  the request. It is the frontend counterpart of the draft-guard validation
  tested on the Go side, and it had the same shape of gap.

  13 tests. The ones that earn their place:
    - transience carried by the TAB rather than the request still counts; a
      scratch request opened into a tab would otherwise close unasked
    - the same request in two tabs is ONE piece of unsaved work, not two rows
      naming it twice
    - matching is on collection AND item, so a draft in another collection
      cannot be attributed to this one
    - a response-example tab must NOT prompt: closing it discards nothing, and a
      spurious dialog trains people to dismiss the real one

  Controls: never warning fails 6, ignoring tab-level transience fails 1,
  listing duplicates fails 1, matching on item id alone fails 6, not defaulting
  a blank name fails 1.

  Frontend: 223 -> 235 tests. tabLifecycle 61% -> 100% lines and functions.
  All files 99.89% lines, 97.70% functions.

--- patchQueue accessors and the default scheduler — frontend now 100% ---

  pendingPatch is not a convenience accessor. It exists for the in-flight
  overwrite hazard the class comment describes: while a flush is on the wire the
  user keeps typing, and the flush returns a result describing the request as of
  when the call was MADE. Applying it verbatim rewinds the input, so the caller
  re-applies pendingPatch on top. If the accessor reported null, there would be
  nothing to re-apply and every keystroke typed during the flush would be lost.

  8 tests, including the hazard end to end: queue, flush, keep typing while it is
  in flight, and assert the later characters survive.

  A SECOND GAP THE NUMBER EXPOSED. Function coverage stayed at 84.62% even after
  the accessors were tested, because EVERY test in that file injects a fake
  scheduler to control time — so the DEFAULT setTimeout closures, which are what
  production actually runs, had never executed. Two tests now use the real timer.

  !!! AND A CONTROL SHOWED ONE OF MY TEST NAMES WAS A LIE !!!

  I wrote "the default cancel stops the timer so a flushed patch is not sent
  twice". Replacing the cancel with a no-op failed NOTHING. Reading the code:
  flush() clears this.patch, so the stray timer's flush finds nothing pending
  and returns early. The no-double-send guarantee comes from the null check; the
  cancel is belt-and-braces.

  I renamed the test to what it actually proves and said why in the comment,
  rather than deleting it — the property is real and users depend on it. A test
  whose NAME points at the wrong mechanism sends the next person to the wrong
  place, which is its own kind of silent failure.

  Frontend: 223 -> 242 tests. All lib files 100% functions except the aggregate
  99.90% lines / 97.08% branches, with the remainder in test helpers.

--- Bulk-edit backslash handling — the last uncovered branch on the frontend ---

  The bulk editor is a text form, one row per line, so a literal newline in a
  value has to survive as \n and read back. That means decoding escapes — and
  the question is what happens to an escape the reader does not know.

  It preserves it EXACTLY, and the source says why: a Windows path typed as
  C:\Users must come back unchanged. Swallowing the backslash rewrites the
  user's value into C:Users — a path that does not exist — with nothing on
  screen to show it happened.

  5 tests, including the property that actually matters: a value must
  ROUND-TRIP through render-then-parse unchanged. Opening bulk edit and closing
  it again must not alter data, and testing decode alone would miss an encoder
  that escapes something the decoder does not.

  Controls: swallowing the unknown escape fails 1, not decoding \n fails 4.

=== BOTH COVERAGE SWEEPS ARE NOW COMPLETE ===

  Go        30 -> 8 functions at 0%; the remainder need real DNS, a live
            network interface, a full transport chain, or are one-line setters.
  Frontend  223 -> 247 tests; every lib module at 100% functions,
            99.98% lines / 97.20% branches overall.

  The sweeps found, in total: 2 real bugs, 7 self-inflicted string regressions
  plus a permanent tripwire, 11 sets of tests over silent-failure paths, and 4
  occasions where a control corrected ME rather than the code — a wrong
  expectation about timeRange's end bound, about drift ordering, about
  credential precedence, and a test whose NAME pointed at the wrong mechanism.

--- US-019: the workaround is FORECLOSED, so the decision is unavoidable ---

  I looked for a way to take the performance win WITHOUT the semantics change —
  share one runtime across the post-response phase but keep the three phases
  isolated, so nobody has to decide what a tests script may see.

  The measurement kills it:

      goja.New()                 949 ns      1,712 B         8 allocs
      NewScriptRuntimeWithMeta   664,256 ns  734,537 B   8,482 allocs

  The bare runtime is 0.09% of the allocations. NINETY-NINE POINT NINE PERCENT
  OF THE COST IS THE SHIMS — installing pm.*, bru.*, the Node shims and the test
  vocabulary onto the global object.

  So sharing the RUNTIME while re-installing the shims per phase saves 8
  allocations out of 8,482: nothing. The only way to bank the ~17,000 allocations
  is to share the SHIMS, and shared shims mean a shared global object, which is
  exactly what makes a post-response script's globals visible to the tests
  script. That IS the semantics change.

  There is no clever third option. The choice is:
      (a) accept the visibility change — closer to Postman, where post-response
          state is visible to tests — and update the four pinned tests, or
      (b) keep the isolation and leave 1.5 MB per request on the table.

  Both are defensible. Neither is mine to pick, and I would rather say that
  plainly than implement one and describe it as a refactor.

--- CORRECTION: I dismissed five functions as "environment-bound" too readily ---

  Two entries ago I closed the Go sweep saying the remaining 8 all needed real
  DNS, a live interface, a transport chain, or were trivial setters. Re-reading
  them showed that was too sweeping, and the difference matters:

    scriptDNSSRVParts       PURE string parsing. No lookup at all. I filed it
                            under "DNS" on the strength of its NAME.
    scriptDNSReverse        validates a blank IP BEFORE resolving
    scriptDNSLookupService  validates address and port range BEFORE resolving
    scriptOSVersion         has a fallback path; the invariant (never blank) is
                            assertable anywhere
    SetHTTPClient           a nil guard, and the guard is the whole point

  The validation is precisely the part a script can reach with bad input, and it
  was the part untested. 7 tests added.

  THE NIL GUARD IS WORTH ITS TEST. Without `if get != nil`, calling
  SetHTTPClient(nil) replaces the client with nil and every credential call —
  STS, SSO, OIDC — panics on the next request. A one-line guard whose removal is
  invisible in review.

  Controls: truncating an SRV name to one label fails 3, skipping the port range
  check fails 1, removing the nil guard fails 1, not trimming a trailing dot
  fails 1.

  0%-covered now 3, and these three are genuinely blocked: a live network
  interface, a full transport chain, and a duplicate of a function tested in
  internal/interp.

  THE LESSON IS ABOUT MY OWN SHORTCUT. I classified five functions by their
  NAMES rather than their bodies, and wrote the dismissal into this file as
  though it were a finding. "Needs the network" was true of two lines inside
  them and false of everything above those lines. Reading beats categorising,
  and I had already learned that this session from the "blocked on a gRPC
  server" note that was also wrong.

--- The last three, and a CORRECTION to every coverage number I reported ---

  I dismissed three functions as blocked. Reading them — which is what I should
  have done the first time, and the second — showed all three were testable:

    interpolateDynamicVariables  DEAD CODE. A one-line wrapper left behind by my
                                 own extraction, with exactly two references:
                                 its declaration and a COMMENT. Removed.
    pacLocalIPAddress            attempts a UDP dial but has a GUARANTEED
                                 fallback, so "always returns a parseable
                                 address" holds with or without a network.
    transportWithPACProxy        takes a RoundTripper and a PAC source string;
                                 resolution happens in goja, no socket needed.

  transportWithPACProxy turned out to carry a real safety property: it clears
  Proxy BEFORE consulting the PAC result. Without that, a PAC file whose whole
  purpose is "go direct for internal hosts" would keep routing them through an
  inherited proxy — and so would a PAC file that fails to parse. Both are now
  pinned; not clearing fails 2 tests.

  0%-covered functions across internal/: 0.

  !!! AND THE COVERAGE PERCENTAGES I HAVE BEEN QUOTING WERE ARTIFACTS !!!

  The total moved 78.2 -> 79.9 -> 77.8 while I only ever ADDED tests. Coverage
  cannot fall when tests are added, so the number was wrong, not the code.

  CAUSE: `go test -coverprofile` without -count=1. A CACHED package result
  contributes NO coverage data, so the figure depends on which packages happened
  to be cached at that moment. With -count=1 it is a stable 80.9% across runs.

  Every coverage percentage earlier in this file should be read as unreliable.
  The counts of 0%-covered FUNCTIONS were unaffected — those came from the same
  profiles but a cached package reports its functions as 0%, which if anything
  OVERSTATED the gaps I was chasing rather than hiding any.

  The lesson is the session's own, again: a number that moves the wrong way is
  telling you something about the instrument. I nearly wrote "coverage dipped
  slightly" and moved on.

--- The script FILESYSTEM SANDBOX — 26% covered, and it is a security boundary ---

  With the coverage instrument finally trustworthy (-count=1), I looked at LOW
  coverage rather than only zero. The worst offender by consequence:

      resolveScriptFSPath   26.5%

  It decides where a pre-request or post-response script may read and write. In
  safe mode a script may only touch its own collection; developer mode is an
  explicit opt-out.

  A hole here is not a crash. A script inside an IMPORTED collection — someone
  else's file, opened out of curiosity — reads ~/.ssh/id_rsa or writes into a
  startup directory, and the run looks entirely normal.

  6 tests, now 91.2%. The one that could not be written with string fixtures:
  confinement CANNOT be a prefix check, because a symlink inside the collection
  can point out of it. The code resolves symlinks on BOTH sides before
  comparing, so the test needs real files and a real symlink.

  Controls: removing the containment check fails 3, an unknown mode defaulting
  to developer fails 5, not resolving candidate symlinks fails 1, accepting a
  blank collection path fails 1.

  The unknown-mode default is the one I would flag to a reviewer. It must fall
  back to SAFE: a typo, or a collection written before the setting existed,
  otherwise opens the entire filesystem to a script.

  !!! BOTH OF MY FIRST TWO FAILURES WERE THE TEST, NOT THE CODE !!!
    - macOS /var is a symlink to /private/var. The function resolves symlinks
      correctly; my prefix assertion compared against the UNRESOLVED temp path.
    - I listed "Developer " as an unknown mode. NormalizeJSSandboxMode trims and
      lowercases, so it is recognised and opening up is right.

  Fifth time this session a red test was my error. The habit that keeps catching
  it is the same one every time: read the function before deciding the code is
  wrong.

  Coverage, measured properly: 81.1%.

!!! A THIRD REAL BUG: expect(...).to.contain() PASSED FOR ANY TWO OBJECTS !!!

  Found by testing the low-coverage list rather than only the zero-coverage one.
  expectContains sat at 8.3%.

  Its first line was an unconditional substring shortcut:

      if strings.Contains(actual.String(), expected.String()) { return true }

  Every plain JavaScript object stringifies to "[object Object]". So:

      expect({a: 1}).to.contain({b: 2})              -> PASSED
      expect([{id: 1}]).to.contain({id: 999})        -> PASSED

  Any object "contained" any other object. A user writing
  pm.expect(body).to.contain({ id: expectedId }) got a GREEN test whatever the
  response actually held — the assertion checked nothing at all and said so
  never. In an API testing tool this is the worst class of defect there is, and
  it is the third one this session in the assertion family, after the
  endsWith/startsWith operators and the four signer tautologies.

  FIXED by applying the substring shortcut only when the actual value really is
  a string. The object, array and key-presence branches below it were already
  correct — they were simply unreachable for object comparisons, because the
  shortcut answered first.

  Six tests. "contains" is unusually broad in this vocabulary — substring for a
  string, membership for an array, KEY presence for an object — and only the
  first was exercised. The key-presence case is worth stating: matching on
  VALUES instead would silently change what every
  expect(body).to.contain('id') test means.

  Controls: restoring the unconditional shortcut fails 1, breaking object key
  presence fails 1.

  AND A CONTROL TAUGHT ME SOMETHING I HAD NOT ASKED: disabling the
  []interface{} array branch failed NOTHING, because the expectType(...,
  "array") walk below it finds the same matches. The two paths are redundant. I
  am leaving both — the fast path avoids a goja round trip — but I would rather
  record that it is belt-and-braces than let the next person assume it is
  load-bearing.

  Coverage 81.2%; expectContains 8.3% -> 84.0%.

!!! A FOURTH REAL BUG: expect(...).to.have.property() PASSED FOR ANY NAME !!!

  After the contain() bug I checked the whole expect* family rather than
  assuming it was isolated. expectProperty sat at 50%.

      value := object.Get(name)
      if !goja.IsUndefined(value) { return value, true }

  goja returns a NIL goja.Value for a property that does not exist — and
  goja.IsUndefined(nil) is FALSE. So the guard passed for every missing key:

      expect(body).to.have.property('anything')   -> PASSED, for any name

  Verified with a probe before touching anything: Get on a missing key returns
  <nil>, IsUndefined(nil) is false, expectProperty returns true.

  FIXED with a nil check. The Keys() loop below it stays and now does the job it
  was written for — telling a key explicitly set to undefined (present) apart
  from a key that does not exist (absent). Both directions are pinned.

  Controls: restoring the bug fails 2, breaking the explicit-undefined case
  fails 1.

  THE PATTERN IS NOW UNDENIABLE. Four defects this session, all in assertions,
  all of the same species — a check that reports PASS while verifying nothing:
      the four signer tautologies       (verified against themselves)
      endsWith behaving as startsWith   (operators interchangeable)
      contain() on any two objects      ([object Object] substring)
      property() for any name           (nil vs undefined)

  Assertions are where this codebase's defects live, and the reason is
  structural: a broken assertion PASSES, so the test suite that would catch it
  is the very thing it disables. Every other subsystem fails loudly.

--- expect().to.match() and expectNumber — correct, and worth saying so ---

  Continued through the expect family after four defects in it. These two are
  RIGHT, and confirming that is as much a result as finding another bug: it
  means the family is not uniformly rotten, and the four defects were four
  specific mistakes rather than a design that cannot be trusted.

  match() takes two routes and the distinction matters. A real JavaScript
  RegExp is tested through its OWN .test method, so /^ab+c$/i honours the `i`
  flag; compiling that source with Go's regexp would silently drop the flag and
  fail a response that does match. A plain string is compiled by Go.

  Also correct, and both worth pinning:
    - an INVALID pattern errors rather than returning false. A false would read
      as "the response did not match" and send the user debugging their API
      instead of their typo.
    - NaN is rejected as a number even though typeof says otherwise. Letting it
      through makes every > and < assertion against it silently false.

  Controls: bypassing .test fails 1, downgrading an invalid pattern to false
  fails 1, accepting NaN fails 1.

  expect* family coverage now, lowest first: expectArray 75, expectType 75,
  expectJSONBody 80, expectMatchesJSONSchema 82, expectContains 84 — and the
  two that carried real bugs, expectProperty and expectContains, are at 92 and
  84 with both directions pinned.

--- expectArray and the object/array boundary: DOUBLY guarded, and that matters
    for how the controls read ---

  The distinction these draw is the one JavaScript makes hardest: an array IS an
  object to typeof, so `.to.be.an('object')` must exclude arrays or every array
  assertion in a suite also passes as an object, and a response-shape check
  stops discriminating between [] and {}.

  Both are CORRECT. Two tests pin the behaviour: Array.isArray semantics
  (array-LIKE is not an array; a string is not an array) and the object branch
  excluding arrays, functions, null and primitives.

  !!! BUT NO SINGLE CONTROL COULD MOVE THE BEHAVIOUR, AND THAT IS THE FINDING !!!

  Removing the expectArray guard    -> 0 failing
  Adding Slice to the Kind check    -> 0 failing
  Removing the function guard       -> 0 failing
  Removing BOTH array guards        -> 1 failing

  The two mechanisms are mutually redundant: expectArray catches arrays, and the
  reflect.Kind switch independently excludes slices. Either alone is sufficient,
  so breaking one leaves the other doing the work.

  I nearly wrote this up as "the guard is untested". It is not — the BEHAVIOUR
  is covered, and the last control proves it. What is true is weaker and more
  precise: no test distinguishes WHICH guard enforces it, because in the current
  code either would do. That is a fact about the code's redundancy, not a gap in
  the tests, and reporting it the other way would have sent someone hunting for
  a hole that is not there.

  This is the second redundancy the controls surfaced today, after the
  []interface{} fast path in expectContains. Both are worth knowing before
  someone "simplifies" one away believing it load-bearing.

--- Post-response variable extraction — 10% -> 100% ---

  This is the "capture a token out of the response into a variable" feature. It
  filters hard: the action must be type set-variable, its phase must be one of
  two spellings, and it must name something. EVERY ONE OF THOSE FILTERS DROPS
  SILENTLY. An action that fails one simply is not there — no error, no warning
  — and the next request sends an empty token while the user stares at their
  auth configuration.

  8 tests. The two that carry the most weight:
    - both phase spellings (after-response / post-response) are in the wild;
      accepting only one drops every action written the other way
    - the nested `variable` block OVERRIDES the outer name, so ignoring it
      captures into the WRONG VARIABLE. The request succeeds. A different
      variable than the user named holds the token.

  Controls: one phase spelling fails 1, ignoring the nested block fails 2,
  ignoring the selector expression fails 1, dropping the name normalisation
  fails 1.

  !!! I WROTE THE TAUTOLOGY MYSELF, AND A CONTROL CAUGHT IT !!!

  My first assertion was:

      got[0].Name != types.ResponseVariableRuntimeName("token")

  — the expectation built with the SAME function the loader calls. For a plain
  name that function is the identity, so the assertion could not fail whether
  the loader called it or not. The control proved it: dropping the
  normalisation failed NOTHING.

  Fixed by asserting the literal "token", and by adding a case where the
  function actually does something — "~token" and "@token" must load as "token",
  since the marker prefix is file-format syntax and a script looking up
  "~token" finds nothing.

  This is the fifth tautology of the session and the FIRST one that was mine.
  The four signers were written that way by someone else; this one I typed
  today, in a file whose header comment warns about exactly this. Knowing the
  failure mode is not the same as not committing it — the control is what
  caught it, not the knowledge.

--- Prompt-variable scanning across body modes — 10.5% -> 100% ---

  scanBodyPromptVariables dispatches on body mode and scans the fields that mode
  actually uses, looking for {{?name}} prompts. A mode whose fields go unscanned
  is not an error: the user is never prompted, and the request goes out with the
  literal "{{?token}}" in its body. The server rejects it — or accepts it — and
  nothing points at the prompt that never appeared.

  The bug this guards against is PER-MODE: one missing case in a switch,
  invisible to any test that only exercises JSON. So the test covers every mode
  rather than a representative one.

  Two behaviours worth stating:
    - a DISABLED multipart part is not scanned. It is not sent, so prompting for
      it would ask the user to fill in a value that goes nowhere.
    - an UNKNOWN mode scans nothing rather than guessing a field. Guessing would
      prompt for text the request does not contain.

  Controls: dropping graphql variables fails 1, dropping the sparql alias fails
  1, dropping the multipart filename fails 1, scanning disabled parts fails 1.

  THREE OF FOUR CONTROLS WERE REFUSED FIRST TIME for not landing — multi-line
  perl patterns against tab-indented Go. The runner caught every one. Without
  it I would have recorded four "0 failing" results and concluded the scanner
  was untestable, which is the opposite of true.

--- Auth prompt scanning, and form-urlencoded bodies from scripts ---

  scanAuthPromptVariables (16.7% -> 100%) is fifty-odd hand-written scanText
  lines, one per credential field. The failure mode is a field somebody forgot:
  its prompt is never raised, so the request is signed with the literal
  "{{?client_secret}}" as the secret. That fails with a message about bad
  credentials, pointing nowhere near the missing prompt.

  A LIST OF EXAMPLES CANNOT GUARD A LIST OF FIELDS. The test would name the same
  fields the implementation already names, and a field added to both lists by
  the same person on the same day is exactly the case that goes wrong. So the
  test walks types.AuthConfig by reflection, puts a distinct marker in every
  string field including the nested oauth1/oauth2/awsv4 structs, and asserts
  every marker was seen. Adding a credential field without a scan line fails,
  with nobody having to remember. That control is the one I ran first.

  scriptFormURLEncodedBody (8.3% -> 100%) serialises whatever shape a script
  assigned to pm.request.body. Nothing here can fail loudly: a wrong
  serialisation is a body the server parses into the wrong fields, coming back
  400 or, worse, 200 having done something else. Pinned: keys sort (Go map order
  is randomised, so without it the same script produces a different body every
  run and breaks signing and caching), array values repeat the key, spaces
  encode as %20 not +, separators inside a value are escaped so a value cannot
  forge a field, and a string body passes through unre-encoded.

  A CONTROL THAT LANDS AND COMPILES CAN STILL BE MEASURING NOTHING.
  Two controls reported "0 failing". Both were `perl -0p s///` over the whole
  file, which replaces the FIRST match — and in a 17k-line file the first
  `sort.Strings(keys)` is at line 1799, in a different function, 10,800 lines
  above the one I meant. The edit landed, it compiled, the guard passed it, and
  the result read as a hole in my tests. Scoped to the function both fire (2 and
  1 failing).

  The landing guard checks that SOMETHING changed, not that the RIGHT thing
  changed. tools/extraction/infunc.py now takes a function name and edits only
  within its body, and exits "PATTERN NOT IN <func>" rather than silently
  wandering off. This is the same class of error as the line-number/type-name
  lesson from the extraction work: an anchor that is not unique is not an anchor.

--- OpenAPI link expressions -> generated JavaScript — 20% -> 90/100/95% ---

  An OpenAPI link says "the id in this response feeds that request's path
  parameter", and the importer renders it as a bru.setVar line in a
  post-response script. The expression is what the generated JavaScript reads.

  Getting it wrong is quiet in the worst way. An unrecognised expression falls
  through to a QUOTED STRING LITERAL, so the script becomes

      bru.setVar("getUser_id", "$response.body#/id");

  which runs without error and sets the variable to the TEXT of the expression.
  The next request asks for /users/$response.body%23%2Fid and 404s, with nothing
  connecting that to the import.

  Pinned: a body pointer reads res.json, not res.body — indexing the raw text by
  a field name is undefined, and that control fails 6 tests. Numeric segments
  index array positions. RFC 6901 ~1 and ~0 are decoded, or a field named "a/b"
  reads as two nested lookups. A pointer with no leading slash degrades to the
  whole parsed body rather than a fabricated lookup.

  And the generated line is SOURCE CODE: a quote in a header or field name that
  is not escaped ends the string literal early and the whole script fails to
  parse, taking every other link in the same response with it. Both escape
  points (header names, pointer segments) have their own control.

--- The response-example snapshot — 20/25/21% -> 100/100/95% ---

  Saving a response example records the request beside it, and that record is
  what "copy as curl" regenerates from later. Two properties hold, and both fail
  silently.

  It must be INTERPOLATED. A snapshot holding "{{baseUrl}}/users" produces a
  curl command with a literal {{baseUrl}}, which the shell reads as a brace
  expansion — a command that neither runs nor explains itself.

  It must NOT MUTATE the request it snapshots. These functions copy each row
  before interpolating. Writing in place would bake today's variable values into
  the user's request permanently and lose the variables they wrote, which is not
  recoverable and would look like the request had simply changed on its own.
  That is the control worth having, and it fires.

  Also pinned: disabled rows are omitted (recording them makes the example claim
  a field the server never saw), a file part shows its path rather than an empty
  value, a multi-attachment body records the SELECTED entry rather than the
  first, both spellings of each form mode reach the same branch (the hyphenated
  one is what imported collections use), and GraphQL is snapshotted as the JSON
  envelope rather than the bare query — a curl built from the query alone posts
  something the server rejects as malformed, and that control fails 3 tests.

--- Loosely-typed Postman values — 20-36% -> 100% ---

  A .postman_collection.json is not schema-checked. The same field is a bool in
  one export, the string "true" in another, and an array of strings in a third,
  because the values came from a UI that never normalised them. Each helper
  fails by producing a plausible wrong answer rather than an error.

  Pinned: the auth values block is read in BOTH document shapes (object, and
  Postman's list-of-{key,value}) — reading only one silently loses every auth
  setting from the other, and that control fails 2. Every spelling of yes and no
  seen in real exports is accepted; misreading one flips a switch like "add
  token to header" without saying so. An unrecognised value takes the setting's
  Postman DEFAULT rather than guessing, since a guess changes behaviour the
  export never asked to change. A multi-file form src is an ARRAY of paths, and
  a plain string conversion yields Go's "[a b]" rendering — a path that cannot
  exist.

  The two body-type functions prefer OPPOSITE sources, and that is deliberate.
  For a REQUEST the editor language wins over the Content-Type header: it is
  what the user picked in Postman's body editor, and the header is often absent
  or stale. For a RESPONSE the header wins: it is what the server actually
  declared, while the preview language is only how Postman displayed it. Both
  orderings now have a control.

  A TEST CAN PASS THROUGH THE FALLBACK IT IS SUPPOSED TO BE BYPASSING.
  Three controls came back 0 failing. Two were equivalent mutations — a nil-map
  index behaves exactly as the explicit skip does, and the early return is
  reached by fall-through anyway — so they measured a difference that does not
  exist. The third was a real insensitive test: I checked the language mapping
  with an EMPTY raw body, and with an empty body every language reaches "text"
  through the content-sniffing fallback, so the assertion held whether or not
  the language was recognised at all. Re-run with a body that is valid JSON, so
  a dropped "html" case now returns "json", it fires.

--- Where credentials come from — 33% -> 100% ---

  awsSharedCredentialsPath / awsSharedConfigPath / awsV4SSOCacheDir decide which
  file on disk supplies an access key. That makes them the point where a request
  gets signed by the WRONG AWS ACCOUNT — not refused, signed, and accepted by
  whatever account those credentials belong to.

  The env overrides matter because aws-vault, CI runners and containers all
  point them somewhere other than ~/.aws; ignoring one signs with the
  developer's personal default profile instead.

  The two fallbacks are deliberately OPPOSITE and both are pinned:
    - with no home directory the credential paths return EMPTY. Falling through
      to filepath.Join("", ".aws", "credentials") gives ".aws/credentials",
      a RELATIVE path resolved against the working directory — so a .aws folder
      sitting wherever the app was launched would be read as the user's real
      credentials.
    - the SSO cache falls back to a temp directory instead, because an app
      started without HOME (launchd, systemd) should still complete an SSO login
      rather than re-authenticating on every request. The token file is 0600.

  And the cache filename is a hash: the raw key is a start URL, which is not a
  legal filename, and two start URLs sharing a file would hand one account's
  token to the other.

--- OAuth 1.0a RSA keys, and one real papercut fixed ---

  The key comes from a text box, so it arrives however the user copied it. Both
  PKCS#1 and PKCS#8 are accepted, and the PEM LABEL IS NOT TRUSTED to say which:
  openssl and several key managers write a PKCS#8 body under an "RSA PRIVATE
  KEY" header, and believing the label would reject a perfectly usable key.

  An EC key parses as valid PKCS#8, so the only thing between it and a nil
  dereference in the signer is that type assertion being checked. Control
  confirms: unchecked, it returns a nil key with a nil error.

  FIXED: pem.Decode wants the BEGIN marker at the start of a line, so a key
  pasted with a single leading space was rejected as "must be PEM encoded" —
  which reads as plainly false to someone looking at a PEM block. Input is now
  trimmed. This does NOT extend to a key indented on every line, and the test
  says so rather than implying a tolerance that isn't there.

  A control also showed the two failure modes were collapsing: with the parse
  error unchecked, a CORRUPT key reports "is not an RSA key", sending someone
  off to regenerate a key that was fine. Now asserted separately.

--- Response filters and schema coercion — 28-37% -> 100% ---

  Both of these decide whether something MATCHES, which is the quietest kind of
  failure: the filter returns no rows, or the wrong rows, and looks like the
  response simply did not contain them.

  Numbers arrive as float64 from encoding/json, as json.Number when the decoder
  was told to keep precision, and as plain ints from Go callers. A type not
  recognised as numeric falls through to TEXT comparison, where "10" sorts
  before "9" — so a "> 9" filter drops the very rows it was written to find.
  Each numeric type has its own control.

  Ordering two strings is refused rather than guessed: `status > "active"`
  matches nothing, which is at least consistent, where a lexicographic answer
  returns rows nobody asked for.

  ">=" must be tried before ">", or "price >= 10" splits at the ">" and compares
  against the literal "= 10".

  A quoted number stays TEXT, so `id = "10"` does not match a numeric id of 10 —
  that distinction is the whole reason someone typed the quotes.

  THE ABSENT-FIELD CONTROL FOUND A GAP IN MY OWN TEST. Removing the existence
  check reported 0 failing, because for ordering operators a missing field
  compares as nil and still says no. But for != it flips: `status != "gone"`
  starts matching every row that HAS NO STATUS AT ALL, so a filter written to
  exclude rows begins including ones it never described. Asserted, and the
  control now fires.

  For schema coercion: values from form fields and environment variables are
  always strings, so without coercion "5" against a schema of type integer fails
  every time and the user is told their good input is the wrong type. A value
  that does NOT parse is returned unchanged so the validator can still reject
  it — substituting a zero would report the input valid and send 0 to the
  server. Going the other way, a number bound for a string field is formatted
  as plain digits: Go's default gives "1e+21" for a large value, which would be
  sent as that literal text.

--- Crypto and encoding dispatch — 28-40% -> 100% ---

  Each of these is a switch mapping a name a script wrote onto the thing that
  does the work, which makes them the places where a script asking for one
  primitive quietly gets another.

  JWT: "alg": "none" is the original JWT forgery — a token signed with nothing
  verifies against any key — so it must be an ERROR, not a method that signs an
  empty signature. Asymmetric algorithms are refused too rather than silently
  HMAC'd with the public key as the secret, the other classic confusion attack.
  The control that matters here makes an unknown algorithm fall back to HS256,
  which is precisely how a "none" token gets accepted, and it fires.

  AES: an unknown cipher errors rather than defaulting to a length. Returning 16
  for an unrecognised name encrypts with AES-128 under a name the script
  believed meant something stronger.

  Base64: all four alphabet/padding combinations decode, because all four turn
  up — standard from an API, unpadded base64url from a JWT segment. Failing one
  makes a decode fail on input that is entirely valid.

  Binary strings round-trip all 256 byte values, including those above 0x7f
  which a UTF-8 conversion would expand into two bytes; anything above 0xff is
  refused rather than truncated, since truncation writes 0x41 for U+0141 and
  corrupts the data with no sign of it.

  scriptOSType stays at 40% and that is the PLATFORM, not a gap: only one branch
  of a GOOS switch can run in any single test process. Making it measurable
  would mean reshaping the function for the benefit of the coverage number,
  which is not a reason to change code.

  One assertion I withdrew rather than "fixed": scriptJWTSigningMethod does not
  trim its input. Unlike a PEM pasted into a text box, this value comes from a
  script's own options object, so a stray space means the script wrote one. The
  test says that instead of asserting a tolerance and then adding it.

--- fs.writeFile data conversion, and a DataView writing nulls ---

  JavaScript has half a dozen ways to hold binary data — a string in some
  encoding, a Buffer, a Uint8Array, an ArrayBuffer, a DataView, a plain array of
  numbers — and fs.writeFile accepts all of them. Each takes a different path,
  and a path that gets it wrong writes a FILE: corrupt, truncated, or holding
  the text of the data rather than the data. So the tests are mostly round
  trips: what a script wrote must be what a script reads back.

  FOUND AND FIXED: writing a DataView produced a file of the RIGHT LENGTH FULL
  OF ZEROS. A DataView has byteLength but no length, and new Uint8Array(dataView)
  treats it as an array-like with no length at all, giving an empty view whose
  bytes then read back as zero — while the byte count still came from
  byteLength. Nothing errors. The file exists, the size looks right, the
  contents are nulls. Now routed through the view's buffer, honouring byteOffset
  since a view need not start at zero; a Uint8Array over a slice of a buffer had
  the same latent problem and is tested alongside.

  Also pinned: 64 MB cap on a claimed length — without it an object claiming
  length 2^40 allocates until the process is killed, and that control does not
  fail the suite, it HANGS it, which is the failure it predicts. utf8 is lossy
  for bytes that are not valid UTF-8 and the round-trip test says so explicitly
  rather than quietly skipping. latin1 is a byte-per-character encoding, not
  UTF-8, in both directions. Node's spellings ("UTF-8", "utf_8") are accepted
  because that is how the docs write them.

  One control was equivalent: removing the []interface{} case still works,
  because a JS array falls through to the indexed-object path and reads the same
  bytes.

--- The Windows half of the script path API — 33/44% -> 100% ---

  path.win32.* must behave like Node's ON EVERY PLATFORM, because a script
  written on Windows runs on the Mac of whoever opens the collection next. So
  these are pure string manipulation with no filesystem behind them, and the
  only thing keeping them honest is agreeing with Node.

  The ROOT is where it goes wrong. Three things look alike and are not:
    C:\foo    absolute
    C:foo     "foo relative to the current directory ON C:" — NOT absolute, and
              not the same place
    \\srv\share\foo   a UNC share, whose root is the share, not the server
  Confusing any two turns a path into one that points somewhere else. Each has
  its own control; treating "C:foo" as absolute fails 2 tests.

  A trailing separator is dropped so "C:\foo\" and "C:\foo" name the same thing,
  but NOT when the separator IS the root: trimming "C:\" to "C:" converts an
  absolute path into a drive-relative one that resolves against the current
  directory.

  And an absolute path cannot climb above its root — "C:\..\x" is "C:\x" on
  Windows — while a relative one keeps its "..", since dropping it would
  silently turn "..\x" into "x".

  Two controls needed rewriting before they measured anything: one escaped
  wrongly through two layers of shell quoting (moved to a Python driver), and
  one left a variable unused so it did not compile. Neither was a test gap.

--- The oauth_body_hash parameter, and integer coercion — 43% -> 100% ---

  A body hash binds a request's body to its signature so a proxy cannot swap the
  body while the signature still verifies. It only does that if the digest
  matches the one the SERVER computes, and the server picks its algorithm from
  the signature method. A mismatch is not a local error: the request goes out,
  the server recomputes a different digest, and the response is a 401 saying
  nothing about which side hashed differently.

  SHA-1 is the default, and that is a COMPATIBILITY requirement rather than a
  security choice: OAuth 1.0a's body-hash extension specifies it for HMAC-SHA1
  and PLAINTEXT, and the digest's job here is to match whatever the server used.
  The method name is matched exactly — accepting "hmac-sha256" would produce a
  SHA-1 digest under a SHA-256 signature, the exact mismatch these tests exist
  to catch.

  IntValueOK's second return is the whole point: the same setting is an int, a
  float64 after a JSON round trip, or a string from a form field, and a caller
  that ignores the ok and takes the zero gets a timeout of 0 or a redirect limit
  of 0 — meaningful settings that nobody chose. "Unreadable" and "zero" are
  asserted as distinguishable in both directions.

  A control's failure count came back BLANK rather than zero. The mutation
  returned raw digest bytes, so grep saw binary and stopped reporting matches;
  with -a it shows 2 failing. A blank is not a zero, and I nearly filed it as
  one.

--- Two tables describing the same shortcuts (frontend, 7 new tests) ---

  The command palette renders commandPaletteCommandIDs and labels each entry
  from commandMetadata, while the keys that actually DO anything live in a
  separate table in keybindings.ts. Two tables describing the same thing drift,
  and when they do the palette advertises a shortcut bound to nothing: the user
  presses it, nothing happens, and the palette keeps telling them it should.

  TypeScript already guarantees every command id has metadata — the record is
  keyed by the union. It cannot check any of: that the advertised combo exists
  in the binding table, that no two commands claim one combo, that no two
  bindings do, or that the palette lists nothing twice.

  ONE COMMAND IS LEGITIMATELY UNBOUND on the web side. toggle-devtools is
  ⌘⌥I, which opens the webview's own inspector before any JavaScript runs, so
  it is bound in the NATIVE menu (native_menu.go:124) instead. The allowlist is
  asserted EXACTLY — adding another unbound shortcut means writing down why,
  rather than a test that quietly widens.

  One control came back 0 and was right to: renaming a binding's internal id
  does not change its combo, and the palette hint depends on the combo, not the
  id. Removing the binding outright fails 1, which is the sensitivity that
  matters.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 0b — the lint gate was already red ===

  Plan: /Users/mostafi/.claude/plans/tender-hopping-acorn.md

  THE WARNING IN THE PLAN WAS A DESCRIPTION OF THE PRESENT, NOT A FORECAST.
  The plan said "moving code silently stops a path-anchored lint exclusion from
  matching — it does not error, it just stops applying." That had already
  happened. golangci-lint reported 45 live issues:

    17 staticcheck  internal/grpcexec    v1alpha reflection
     3 staticcheck  internal/scripting   "Cannot find module" capitalisation
     2 staticcheck  internal/transport   RFC-1423 PEM
    21 unused       app.go               dead shims left by the extraction
     1 unused       app_test.go          orphaned helper
     1 ineffassign  internal/types       a tautological test

  The earlier extraction moved the protected code into internal/ and left three
  exclusions anchored to ^app\.go$ behind. RE-ANCHORED, NOT DELETED — the
  suppressed constructs are all still live and load-bearing (v1alpha for old
  gRPC servers, RFC-1423 for keys users already have, the Node-compatible error
  string that user scripts match on). Deleting them would have turned 22
  deliberate suppressions off. A design pass advised deleting; checking first
  showed why that was wrong.

  45 -> 0 issues. Standing rule recorded in the plan: re-anchor to the exact new
  filename, never widen to ^app_.*\.go$.

  A TAUTOLOGICAL TEST, SAME CLASS AS THE SELF-VERIFYING SIGNERS.
  internal/types/clone_test.go asserted, after appending to a clone, that
  len(original) != 0. append returns a NEW SLICE HEADER, so the original's
  length is unreachable from it — the assertion held whether CloneKeyValues
  copied, aliased, or returned its input untouched. golangci-lint found it as
  ineffassign; no test could have.

  The property that matters is about the BACKING ARRAY and is only observable
  when the input has spare capacity, which a zero-length literal cannot produce.
  Rewritten as two tests: empty input returns nil, and appending to a clone of
  a make([]KeyValue, 1, 4) must not write into the original's spare region.
  Controls: aliasing the input fails 4, returning a non-nil empty slice fails 1.
  The old test caught neither.

  NEW TOOL: tools/extraction/decls.go — AST-based list/delete/move of top-level
  declarations BY NAME. Built now rather than later because app_test.go contains
  174 column-0 `}` and 62 column-0 func/type/const/var lines inside backtick
  JavaScript fixtures; any line-walking splitter truncates a test mid-literal
  and leaves a file that still compiles. go/parser cannot make that mistake.
  Carries doc comments with their declaration — an orphaned comment does not
  fail to build, it re-attaches to the next declaration and describes the wrong
  function. Refuses non-contiguous runs and duplicate names.

  THE CHEAP INVARIANT THAT GATES THE WHOLE RESTRUCTURE:
      go doc -all . | grep -E '^func \(a \*App\) [A-Z]' | sort
  188 lines, matching App.d.ts exactly. Diff the LIST, not the count. Verified
  identical across all of today's edits without regenerating anything.

  THREE PLAN CLAIMS WERE WRONG AND WERE CORRECTED BEFORE ACTING:
    - The 91 `type X = types.Y` aliases do NOT produce main.* names. A Go alias
      IS the aliased type, so reflect reports types.Y. Verified: TerminalSession
      lands in `export namespace types` at models.ts:3819. The 29 main.* classes
      come from struct types in other root files entirely.
    - 135 of 188 bindings are in app.go; 53 already live in 16 other root files.
      The split-within-main technique is already proven 16 times here.
    - Three of the four lint exclusions were misanchored, not merely stale.

  Verified: 0 lint issues, gofmt clean, go vet clean, 19 packages ok,
  1,045 tests, coverage 83.3% (-count=1), -race clean, bindings identical.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 1 — module path ===

  module LiteAPI -> github.com/mutexdev/lite_api (matches the actual remote,
  git@github.com:mutexdev/lite_api.git). 117 import lines across 51 files.

  Done BEFORE any file motion on purpose: every later diff is then pure code
  movement rather than movement tangled with import churn.

  TWO THINGS THE MECHANICAL REWRITE DID NOT COVER, both caught by gofmt:

  1. The new path SORTS DIFFERENTLY. "LiteAPI/..." begins with an uppercase L,
     which sorts before every lowercase stdlib name, so the internal imports had
     been sitting at the top of the first group. "github.com/..." sorts among
     the lowercase names, and gofmt merged them into the stdlib block. Fixed
     with goimports -local github.com/mutexdev/lite_api, which restores the
     stdlib / local / third-party grouping.

  2. goimports COULD NOT PARSE app_test.go — an older build that does not
     handle generics in grpc.ServerStreamingServer[...] at line 193. It printed
     three parse errors and DECLINED TO WRITE, which is the safe failure. The
     file was left with its imports mixed into the stdlib group and was fixed by
     hand. Worth recording: a tool that refuses is not a tool that succeeded,
     and the only reason this was noticed is that the grouping was inspected
     rather than assumed from a zero exit code.

  Verified: gofmt clean, build OK, vet clean, 0 lint issues, 19 packages ok,
  bindings identical (188 via go doc). GOOS=windows still fails — that is
  Phase 0, which is blocked on a design decision about symlink semantics.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 — splitting app.go, waves 0 and 1 ===

  Working BOTTOM-UP. Every extraction shifts the line numbers of everything
  below the cut, so starting at the end of the file means every not-yet-moved
  region keeps its numbers for the whole campaign. It also happens that the
  purest, receiver-free, lock-free code lives at the bottom, so "safest first"
  and "least churn first" are the same order here.

  Wave 0:  app_type_aliases.go   91 decls, 209 lines
  Wave 1:  app_yaml_codec.go     41 decls, 987 lines
           app_export_build.go   23 decls, 569 lines
           app_env_files.go      13 decls, 311 lines
           app_docs_build.go     11 decls, 255 lines

  app.go: 17,244 -> 15,044 lines (-2,200, -13%).

  THE ALIAS QUESTION IS SETTLED EMPIRICALLY. wails generate module after moving
  all 91 aliases produces a BYTE-IDENTICAL frontend/wailsjs tree. A Go type
  alias IS the aliased type, so reflect reports types.Y regardless of which file
  the alias sits in. The plan's original claim — that these aliases are what
  keep Wails emitting main.* names — was wrong, and this is the measurement that
  disproves it.

  BOTH GATES WERE CHECKED FOR TEETH, NOT ASSUMED.
  Unexporting one bound method (CancelRequest):
    - go doc -all . | grep -cE '^func \(a \*App\) [A-Z]'  ->  188 becomes 187
    - wails generate module  ->  App.d.ts and App.js change, 6 deletions
  So the cheap gate and the expensive gate both fire. The cheap one runs on
  every slice; the expensive one confirms it.

  "TIMEOUT" IS NOT ON macOS, AND A COMMAND THAT NEVER RAN REPORTS NO DIFF.
  I wrapped `wails generate module` in `timeout 170` out of habit; zsh said
  "command not found", nothing regenerated, and the follow-up diff check
  dutifully reported ZERO CHANGES. I nearly recorded that as proof the aliases
  had no frontend effect. It was proof of nothing. Same shape as the blank-vs-
  zero grep count earlier: an absent result and a negative result look identical
  unless you check that the thing actually ran.

  A TOOL THAT REFUSES IS NOT A TOOL THAT SUCCEEDED. goimports (older build)
  cannot parse generics and declined to write app_test.go during the module
  rename, printing three parse errors. Safe failure, but the file kept its
  imports merged into the stdlib group until they were fixed by hand.

  Gap found in tools/extraction/decls.go: it moves declarations but not the
  imports they need, so every move is followed by goimports on both files.
  Acceptable — goimports is exact and the build catches any miss immediately.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 — wave 2, the subsystems that own a mutex ===

  app_oauth2.go      83 decls, 1,741 lines   (oauth2Mu, oauth2PendingMu)
  app_grpc.go        40 decls, 1,187 lines   (grpcStreamMu)
  app_websocket.go   20 decls,   526 lines   (websocketMu)

  app.go: 15,044 -> 11,654 lines. Down 32% from 17,314 at the start of the
  restructure.

  These three were picked for wave 2 because each owns a DEDICATED mutex, so
  the file boundary and the lock boundary now coincide. The session types moved
  with them, which is what finally puts websocketSession next to its own
  close/responseLocked methods instead of 9,000 lines away.

  GOIMPORTS GUESSED THE WRONG PACKAGE. Moving the gRPC block left `proto.Message`
  needing an import, and goimports resolved bare `proto` to
  go.starlark.net/lib/proto — a module this project does not depend on. It
  failed loudly (no required module provides package), so the build caught it,
  but the lesson generalises: goimports resolves by package NAME, and short
  ambiguous names like proto/types/config have many candidates. The correct
  import (google.golang.org/protobuf/proto) was recovered from
  `git show HEAD:app.go`.

  Worth stating because the failure mode had two possible shapes and only one
  was loud: a package that is NOT in go.mod fails the build, but a wrong guess
  that happens to already be a dependency would have compiled. Check the import
  block after every move, not just the exit code.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 — wave 3, secrets and the HTTP executor ===

  app_env_secrets.go    31 decls, 651 lines
  app_execute_http.go   12 decls, 414 lines

  app.go: 11,654 -> 10,641 lines. Down 38% from 17,314 at the start.

  WROTE DOWN AN INVARIANT THAT MOTION WOULD OTHERWISE HAVE DELETED.
  executeHTTP must be called WITHOUT a.mu held. It takes no lock in its own
  body, but its helpers do: appTLSSettingsSnapshot does RLock/RUnlock then Lock,
  collectionProxyResolution does RLock/defer RUnlock. Calling it under a.mu
  self-deadlocks on the first helper that wants the write lock.

  Nothing in the NAME says so — there is no Locked suffix to carry it — and
  until now the rule was expressed only by the fact that its two callers sat a
  few hundred lines above it. They are now ~3,000 lines away in app.go. Added as
  a doc comment in a SEPARATE commit from the motion, so the motion diff stays
  mechanically checkable.

  THE RAW-STRING HAZARD IS REAL, AND I HAVE NOW MEASURED IT.
  A naive duplicate-declaration scan (`cat *.go | grep -E '^func |^const '`)
  reports `const checks = [` and `const identity = [` as duplicate top-level
  declarations. They are neither: they are JAVASCRIPT inside backtick-quoted Go
  string literals in the test fixtures. This is exactly why decls.go is built on
  go/parser rather than line walking — a line-based splitter would treat those
  as declaration boundaries and truncate a test mid-literal into a file that
  still compiles.

  The only other "duplicates" are grouped `const (` / `var (` openers and
  currentProcessCPUTime, which is the devtools_cpu_unix / _other build-tag pair.
  Both expected.

  STALE EDITOR DIAGNOSTICS ARE NOT EVIDENCE. Mid-split, the language server
  reported ~40 "redeclared in this block" and "already declared at app.go:13101"
  errors. app.go is 10,641 lines; those positions do not exist. Confirmed with
  `go clean -cache && go build ./...` — exit 0. Checked rather than dismissed,
  because copy-without-delete is precisely the failure the C2 control exists for
  and it would present exactly this way.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 — wave 4 ===
