  app_shims.go             36 decls,   121 lines  (one-line delegations to internal/)

  app.go: 10,641 -> 8,274 lines. Down 52% from 17,314 at the start of the
  restructure, and down 81% from the ~44,000 it was before the package
  extraction began.

  app_shims.go is 36 declarations in 121 lines — nearly all one-liners of the
  shape `func newID(p string) string { return scalar.NewID(p) }`. Collecting
  them in one file makes the remaining delegation surface countable, which is
  what makes it possible to retire them deliberately rather than discovering
  them one at a time. These are the LIVE ones; the 21 dead ones were deleted in
  Phase 0b.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 — wave 5, and a tripwire that caught the split ===

  app_send.go     28 decls, 771 lines  (SendRequest -> sendRequestWithControlsContext)
  app_runner.go   11 decls, 357 lines  (RunCollection, iterations, delay, bail)

  app.go: 8,274 -> 7,174 lines. Down 59% from 17,314.

  A SOURCE-SCANNING GUARD FIRED ON THE MOVE — WHICH IS BOTH RIGHT AND WRONG.
  TestRunnerResolvesWorkspaceAfterReacquiringTheLock (US-076) greps the source
  for

      scripting.ApplyScriptVariableContextToState(&a.state, liveWorkspace, ...)

  to catch anyone reintroducing the captured pre-I/O workspace pointer. Its
  helper read app.go and only app.go, so moving sendRequestWithControlsContext
  into app_send.go made it report a US-076 regression when nothing about the
  property had changed. The FIRST failing test of the whole restructure, and it
  was the guard rather than the code.

  Fixed by scanning every non-test file in package main rather than one
  filename. Exactly as strict about what it protects — the call must exist and
  must not pass `ws` — and silent about where that code lives. A guard that
  fires on code motion is a guard people learn to edit away.

  MY OWN CONTROL DOCTRINE, VIOLATED BY ME, CAUGHT BY THE DOCTRINE.
  The first negative control on the fixed guard reported 0 failures. I had
  counted '--- FAIL' lines instead of reading the output. The control had not
  compiled: swapping liveWorkspace for ws left liveWorkspace declared and not
  used, so the run ended in "FAIL [build failed]" — which contains no '--- FAIL'
  line at all. Zero failures and a broken build are indistinguishable to a
  counter.

  control.sh exists precisely to refuse a number unless the edit LANDED and
  COMPILED, and I bypassed it by inlining a one-off. Re-run with `_ =
  liveWorkspace` added so the mutation compiles: both assertions fire. Third
  time this session that an absent result has impersonated a negative one
  (blank grep count, un-run `timeout` command, now a failed build).

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 2 COMPLETE — app.go 17,314 -> 3,398 lines (-80%) ===

  24 app_*.go files. Final wave, deliberately last:
    app_openapi_sync.go     10 decls, 299 lines
    app_export_bindings.go   7 decls, 206 lines
    app_bootstrap.go        13 decls, 389 lines
    app_persist.go          26 decls, 554 lines   <- riskiest, done last

  app.go now holds only the spine: imports, the App struct, the session/secret
  types, construction and lifecycle, assertions, and the tab/notification
  internals.

  THE PERSISTENCE ENGINE WENT LAST ON PURPOSE. It holds the coalesced-write
  loop and the *Locked convention the whole a.mu discipline rests on. By the
  time it moved, the mechanics had been proven on sixteen uneventful slices.
  Race-tested specifically: go test -race on the persist/revision/flush tests
  passes.

  persistLocked was missed by the range selection (it sat one line outside the
  window) and moved in a follow-up call. The tool takes NAMES, so the fix was
  naming it — no line arithmetic, no second chance to be off by one.

  WROTE DOWN A SECOND INVARIANT THAT MOTION SEPARATED FROM ITS CODE.
  persistStop/persistDone/persistStopped stay on the App struct in app.go
  because they are App state, but every reader and writer of them
  (markDirty, persistWriterLoop, stopPersistWriter, persistOnce) is now in
  app_persist.go. The comment explaining that persistStopped is sticky now
  points at the file where that is actually enforced. Same treatment as the
  executeHTTP lock invariant: the split makes implicit knowledge explicit, or
  it loses it.

  GOIMPORTS DOES NOT RESTORE ALIASED IMPORTS. Moving the persist block left
  wailsruntime.BrowserOpenURL with no import; goimports could not infer the
  `wailsruntime` alias for wails/v2/pkg/runtime and left the file broken. Loud
  failure (undefined: wailsruntime), fixed by hand. Second goimports limitation
  this phase, after the go.starlark.net/lib/proto misresolution.

  Verified after every slice: gofmt, go vet, golangci-lint (0), full test suite
  (19 packages), the go doc binding invariant (188, byte-identical list), and
  wails generate module producing no change to frontend/wailsjs. Cold-cache
  build and vet re-run at the end.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 3a — internal/atomicfile ===

  writePrivateAtomic was defined in recovery_store.go but consumed from five
  other files: app_oauth2.go (credential storage), workspace_registry.go,
  window_session.go, workspace_window_lock.go, workspace_migration.go. Moving
  recovery_store.go first would have made all five — and app.go — import
  internal/recovery to write a file atomically, a dependency that says nothing
  true about the design. So this goes first and unblocks the rest of Phase 3.

  20 test packages now (was 19).

  SIX TESTS FOR A 40-LINE FUNCTION, BECAUSE IT WRITES THE SECRETS. Each step of
  the sequence is load-bearing and each failure is silent:
    - parent is created AND re-chmodded: MkdirAll no-ops on an existing
      directory, so a pre-existing world-readable parent would keep its mode and
      expose everything written into it. This control fails 1.
    - temp file chmodded before the write, not after.
    - Sync before Rename: rename is ordered, the data is not.

  A SKIPPED TEST IS WORTH NO MORE THAN NO TEST. My first failure-injection made
  the parent directory 0500 — and WritePrivate chmods its parent to 0700 on the
  way in, which is exactly what the neighbouring test asserts. So the function
  repaired my setup and succeeded, the test skipped on every run, and it looked
  green. Re-injected with a parent that is a regular FILE, which MkdirAll cannot
  repair.

  A CONTROL FOUND A REAL GAP, TWO DID NOT.
    - "temp file left behind" reported 0 failing. The deferred Remove only
      matters on the FAILURE path — on success the temp has already been renamed
      away — and I had only tested success. A write that fills the temp and then
      fails to rename leaves a FULL PLAINTEXT COPY OF THE SECRET under a dotfile
      name. Added a test that puts a directory at the destination so the rename
      fails after the content is written; the control now fails 1.
    - "file mode left at default" and "parent created world-readable" are
      equivalent mutations: os.CreateTemp already creates 0600, and the explicit
      Chmod(dir, 0700) tightens whatever mode MkdirAll used. Not test gaps.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 3b — HAR and Swagger 2 into internal/importers ===

  har_import.go    -> internal/importers/har.go       (420 lines)
  swagger2_import.go -> internal/importers/swagger2.go (631 lines)

  These were direct siblings of the postman/insomnia/openapi importers already
  in that package and had zero App coupling. internal/importers is now 4,593
  lines and holds every format the app can read.

  Exported the two entry points (ImportHAR, ConvertSwagger2ToOpenAPI3) plus
  IsSwagger2Document, which the detection dispatch in collection_import.go
  needs. Everything else stayed unexported — the package's surface grew by three
  names, not by the ~35 declarations that moved.

  TESTS DO NOT ALWAYS BELONG WITH THE CODE THEY WERE FILED UNDER. Four tests in
  the two files exercised detectCollectionImport, which lives in
  collection_import.go and stays in package main because it is entangled with
  App state. They moved BACK to the root as collection_import_har_test.go and
  collection_import_swagger2_test.go. Their subject was the detection dispatch,
  not the importer; the filename had just put them next to the fixture they
  happened to use.

  Two consequences of a package move that the compiler catches loudly and that
  are worth naming because both are easy to fix wrongly:
    - FIXTURE PATHS ARE RELATIVE TO THE TEST'S PACKAGE DIRECTORY. The moved
      tests read docs/qa/import-fixtures/*, which is now ../../ away. The
      root-side tests keep the original path. Two copies of a four-line helper
      is the honest answer here; sharing it would mean exporting a test helper
      across a package boundary to save nothing.
    - swagger2String, an unexported stringifier, was used by one relocated
      assertion. Replaced with scalar.YAMLString rather than exported — a
      helper does not become part of a package's API because one test outside it
      wanted it.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 3c — internal/workspacestate ===

  workspace_state_store.go -> internal/workspacestate/scoped.go
  workspace_registry.go    -> internal/workspacestate/registry.go
  window_session.go        -> internal/workspacestate/session.go
  canonicalWorkspaceIdentity + sameCanonicalWorkspacePath -> identity.go

  953 lines, 21 test packages (was 20).

  TWO FILES DELIBERATELY DID NOT MOVE, and the reason is worth recording rather
  than retrying later from scratch.

  shared_state.go needs encryptEnvironmentSecretString/decryptEnvironmentSecret-
  String, which live in app_env_secrets.go in package main. workspace_migration.go
  in turn needs ProjectSharedAppState/ReadSharedAppState/WriteSharedAppState from
  shared_state.go. So those two are one unit, and moving them requires extracting
  the environment-secret crypto into its own package first.

  The codebase's established seam pattern (a settable package-level func var)
  would have made them move today. REJECTED: that is exactly the shape of the
  scripting.SetHTTPClient bug already recorded in this file — a seam that
  production code never wires, where the symptom is silent (secrets written
  unencrypted) rather than a build error. A dependency the compiler enforces
  beats a dependency an init() is supposed to remember.

  THE SAME RENAMING TRAP, TWICE, IN ONE SESSION. Qualifying bare type names as
  types.X with a whole-line regex rewrote STRUCT FIELD NAMES:

      Workspace   WorkspaceScopedReference
   -> types.Workspace   WorkspaceScopedReference

  and then, after fixing that with a line-start rule, it rewrote INLINE STRUCT
  LITERAL KEYS that a line-start rule cannot see:

      types.AppState{Preferences: ...}  ->  {types.Preferences: ...}

  Final rule: skip the first token of an indented line (field declarations), and
  never qualify an identifier immediately followed by ':' (literal keys and
  labels). Both failures were loud syntax errors, which is the only reason this
  was cheap.

  I ALSO QUALIFIED A TYPE THIS PACKAGE DOES NOT OWN. SharedAppState is DEFINED
  in shared_state.go, which stayed in main, so the rewrite produced
  `type workspacestate.SharedAppState struct` — a declaration with a dot in its
  name. Being in the symbol list is not the same as having moved.

  GOIMPORTS FAILED SILENTLY ON GENERICS AGAIN. workspace_window_runtime.go has
  `func mergeSharedSlice[T any]`, which this goimports build cannot parse, so it
  declined to add the workspacestate import and the build reported "undefined:
  workspacestate" 7 times. Third goimports limitation this restructure
  (generics twice, wrong-package guess once, aliased imports once).

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 4 — internal/visualizer, internal/history ===

  visualizer.go   390 -> 66 lines   (internal/visualizer, 329 lines)
  history_store.go 476 -> ~120      (internal/history)
  writeFileAtomic -> internal/atomicfile.Write (second atomic writer, distinct
    from WritePrivate: takes a perm and does NOT create or re-chmod the parent,
    so it is usable on directories whose mode is already meaningful)

  23 test packages. First use of the FORWARDER PATTERN: a bound method cannot
  leave package main, but almost nothing it does has to stay with it.
  VisualizerDocument is now sixteen lines — take the lock, find the item, call
  visualizer.BuildDocument.

  THE BINDING INVARIANT FIRED, AND IT WAS RIGHT TO.
      go doc -all . | grep '^func (a \*App) [A-Z]'
  differed on two lines after the history move:
      GetHistoryEntry(id string) (HistoryEntry, error)
   -> GetHistoryEntry(id string) (history.HistoryEntry, error)
  The METHOD SET was identical and App.d.ts still declares 188. Only the
  signature spelling changed, because the types moved packages.

  This is worth writing down: the signature-level check and the method-set check
  catch DIFFERENT things and both are worth running.
    - method set  -> an accidentally lost or unexported binding
    - signatures  -> a type that moved packages, which ALWAYS needs frontend
                     follow-up (main.HistoryEntry -> history.HistoryEntry, 10
                     references across App.svelte and HistoryPanel.svelte, plus
                     two namespace imports)
  A check that fires only on the first would have let the frontend drift.

  THE FIELD-NAME TRAP, A THIRD TIME. `historyStore *historyStore` — field name
  and type name identical — and a plain re.sub rewrote both, producing
  `history.Store *history.Store`. I had already built the guard for this twice
  today and then used a bare substitution because it was "just one symbol".
  Loud syntax error, cheap fix, but the pattern is now unmistakable: never
  rewrite Go identifiers without the first-token-of-an-indented-line guard.

  Crossing a package boundary forced four helpers and four methods to be
  exported (Store.Append/List/Get/Clear, RedactHeaders, HeaderMapRows,
  EntryMatches, RedactedValue). Five tests that reach into Store's unexported
  path field moved into the package with it; the eight that exercise the App
  bindings stayed in main.

=== CORRECTION — the "script sandbox bypasses the shared transport" finding was WRONG ===

  I reported this three times, as: "internal/scripting exposes SetHTTPClient and
  package main never calls it, so fetch/sendRequest inside user scripts use a
  bare &http.Client{Timeout: 30s}, bypassing the transport cache, TLS posture,
  proxy configuration and client certificates that every other outbound request
  goes through."

  WRONG ON EVERY CLAUSE. Reading the call site and US-017 settles it:

  1. internal/scripting/scripting.go:2476 says, at the call site:
       "US-017: shared client. Posture deliberately unchanged: script
        sendRequest has never used the collection's proxy or TLS settings, and
        adopting them here would let a verify-off request reach a script's
        outbound call without anything in the UI saying so."

  2. http_transport_cache.go:614 (US-017) consolidated six one-off clients and
     names the sixth as "the script runtime's sendRequest", consolidated
     "WITHOUT adopting the user's proxy or TLS settings, and that is
     deliberate... letting a 'disable SSL verification' toggle reach a token
     endpoint would be a security regression dressed up as a refactor."

  3. The two clients are BEHAVIOURALLY IDENTICAL. scripting's default is
     &http.Client{Timeout: 30s} with a nil Transport, which IS
     http.DefaultTransport. sharedCredentialHTTPClient resolves a pristine spec,
     and TestTransportCachePristineSpecKeepsCallerTransport asserts that returns
     http.DefaultTransport and occupies no cache entry. Same object, same pool,
     same verified TLS, same ProxyFromEnvironment.

  So there is nothing to bypass: the credential client deliberately has no
  proxy, no custom CA and no client certificate either. Wiring the seam would
  change nothing observable.

  HOW I GOT IT WRONG: I took the init() comment ("must go through the shared
  transport cache like every other request") — which is about awsv4 and
  transport — and generalised it to a third package without reading that
  package's own call site. The seam exists for TESTABILITY, not because
  production forgot to connect it. An unwired seam looks identical to a
  forgotten one, and only the call site distinguishes them.

  Left the code alone and added internal/scripting/http_client_posture_test.go
  to pin what is actually true: the sandbox carries no TLS or proxy posture of
  its own, always has a timeout, and SetHTTPClient rejects nil. Controls:
  giving the sandbox its own Transport fails 1; removing the nil guard fails 1.
  The comment in that file explains the trap so the next reader does not repeat
  it.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 4 — internal/gitworkbench ===

  The pure half of git_workbench.go: the five bound types, the output-capping
  buffer, the redaction/sanitisation, the status and name-status parsers, and
  the branch/remote validators. 314 lines. The 28 App methods that shell out to
  git stay in main, because they need a.gitWorkbenchExecutable and the state
  lock; converting them into a Runner is a design change, not motion.

  24 test packages. Method set still 188, App.d.ts unchanged. 24 signature lines
  now name gitworkbench.CollectionGit* — 4 frontend references updated plus one
  namespace import. svelte-check 0/0, 254 frontend tests.

  I ALMOST CAUSED A SECURITY REGRESSION, AND THE BUILD IS THE ONLY REASON I DID
  NOT. There are TWO remote-URL validators and their names read like a base and
  an alias:

      NormalizeGitRemoteURL            scheme, host, path, no userinfo
      NormalizeCollectionRemoteURL     the above, THEN also rejects queries,
                                       fragments, embedded credentials and
                                       non-local file:// hosts

  A blanket rename pointed git_workbench.go's SetCollectionGitRemote at the
  PERMISSIVE one. That compiles. Every existing test passes. The only visible
  consequence would have been that a collection remote could carry a password,
  which is then persisted into the collection's config on disk and into any
  export of it. I noticed only because the build failed on an unrelated symbol
  and I re-read the diff.

  Restored per call site from `git show HEAD:` — git_workbench.go uses the
  strict one, app_workspace_crud.go's three sites use the base one, exactly as
  before. Added internal/gitworkbench/remote_url_test.go, whose discriminating
  cases are the four the two validators disagree on; the shared rejections are
  marked as NOT discriminating, since a test built only from those would pass
  with either function substituted.

  FOUND WHILE WRITING THAT TEST — reported, not fixed:
      git@github.com:owner/repo.git        ACCEPTED
      ssh://git@github.com/owner/repo.git  REJECTED, "must not embed credentials"
  The same remote, two spellings. For https the check is right: userinfo there
  is a password headed for disk. For ssh, "git" is the login name and auth
  happens over the key agent, so there is nothing embedded — and the scp form
  being accepted shows banning "git@" was never the intent. Left alone: it is
  validation on a security-adjacent path, the workaround is the spelling most
  people already use, and I had just demonstrated what happens when I change
  one of these on judgement. Pinned as a test so a future change is deliberate.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 4 — internal/localserver ===

  mock_server.go 458 -> 130, docs_server.go 199 -> 68. The two listeners, their
  routing and status types move; the App methods that own the sync.Once and the
  per-collection maps stay. 25 test packages.

  The tests reached past the API for `server.listener.Addr()` to assert
  LOOPBACK-ONLY BINDING. Rather than export the field, both types gained an
  Addr() accessor: the port alone cannot distinguish 127.0.0.1 from 0.0.0.0,
  and that difference is whether a mock API is local or published to the
  network. Three pure routing tests moved into the package with the code.

  I CORRUPTED EMBEDDED JAVASCRIPT WITH A BLANKET REGEX — the exact hazard I
  built decls.go on go/parser to avoid, then bypassed because it was "just a
  method rename".

      python3 -c "re.sub(r'\.update\(', '.Update(', s)"  over every *.go

  Two call sites needed it (MockServer.Update). ELEVEN did not: Node's crypto
  API is `.update()`, and app_test.go embeds JavaScript conformance fixtures in
  backtick literals —

      crypto.createHash("sha256").update("hello bruno").digest("hex")
   -> crypto.createHash("sha256").Update("hello bruno").digest("hex")

  which the shim answers with "Object has no member 'Update'".

  Caught by TestJavaScriptRuntimeSupportsCommonRequireShims, which is the only
  reason this did not ship. Note what the test suite did NOT catch: gofmt was
  clean, go vet was clean, golangci-lint was clean, and the code compiled — a
  string literal is valid Go whatever is inside it. Only a test that actually
  RUNS the embedded script could see it.

  The sibling renames (.stop() -> .Stop(), .status() -> .Status()) were audited
  the same way and are all genuine Go call sites; those names do not appear in
  the embedded JS. The lesson is not "regexes are bad" but that the raw-string
  hazard is specific and checkable: before a blanket rename, ask whether the
  identifier is also a JavaScript API name.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — first slice of App.svelte ===

  src/lib/variableResolution.ts — the {{variable}} resolution cluster, ~280
  lines pulled out of App.svelte (12,320 -> 12,163) with 18 tests where there
  were none. Frontend suite 254 -> 272.

  Follows the pattern the repo already proves (sidebarTree.ts, patchQueue.ts,
  variableTooltipState.ts): pure logic in a plain .ts module that node --test
  can exercise, with the .svelte file as the reactive shell. Nothing here was
  reactive; it was 280 lines of state-in/value-out sitting where no test could
  reach it.

  Three properties worth the tests, all silent when wrong:
    - PRECEDENCE. Six scopes can define one name, last-write-wins, order is
      global < collection < environment < folder < request < runtime. Get it
      wrong and the tooltip states one value while the request sends another —
      the feature whose only job is explaining the value lies about it.
    - SECRET PROPAGATION. A value is secret if it is marked secret OR if
      anything it interpolates is.
    - CYCLES. {{a}}->{{b}}->{{a}} terminates via a seen-set that is added before
      recursing and REMOVED after, so {{x}}-{{x}} still resolves twice.

  A CONTROL FOUND A GAP AT TWO LEVELS OF INDIRECTION. Deleting
  `containsSecret = containsSecret || resolved.containsSecret` failed nothing.
  One hop is already caught before recursing, by the matched variable's own
  secret flag, so my test (authHeader -> token) passed either way. The deleted
  line only matters at two hops: outer -> middle -> deep, where `middle` is not
  itself secret. Without it, a secret reached through an intermediate variable
  renders IN CLEARTEXT in the tooltip. Test added, control now fails 1.

  Also deleted the duplicate type declarations. Importing VariableTooltipInfo
  and VariableTooltipSource while ALSO declaring them locally left the local
  ones shadowing the imports — svelte-check was perfectly happy, and the two
  copies would have drifted apart with nothing to say so.

  App.svelte is still 12,163 lines. This is one cluster of several; the same
  treatment applies to the prompt scanning, URL/path-param and global-search
  clusters identified alongside it.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — second slice, request scanning ===

  src/lib/requestScanning.ts — 256 lines, 23 tests. App.svelte 12,163 -> 11,959.
  Frontend suite 272 -> 295.

  TWO SCANS THAT LOOK ALIKE AND DELIBERATELY ARE NOT:
    collectVariableNames / scanBodyVariables -> every {{name}}, EVERY body field
                                                regardless of mode
    collectPromptNames   / scanBodyPrompts   -> every {{?name}}, ONLY the fields
                                                the active mode sends
  The variable list drives a tooltip, so keeping a name from the JSON body while
  the user looks at the XML tab costs nothing. Prompts drive a DIALOG at send
  time: asking for a value from a body that is not being sent blocks the request
  on an irrelevant question. Both directions now have a test.

  scanBodyPrompts is the frontend twin of scanBodyPromptVariables in
  internal/scripting, tested earlier this session. Same per-mode dispatch, same
  silent failure, now the same coverage on both sides.

  AN OPTIONAL PARAMETER HID A REGRESSION FROM THE TYPE CHECKER.
  collectPromptNames closed over `activeGlobalEnvironment`, a $derived. Moving
  it to a module meant passing that in, and I declared it OPTIONAL. All three
  call sites then failed to pass it, svelte-check reported 0 errors, and prompts
  defined in a GLOBAL ENVIRONMENT silently stopped being collected — the user
  would never be asked, and the request would go out with a literal {{?name}}.
  Made the parameter required (`types.Environment | undefined`), which turns the
  same mistake into a compile error, and fixed the three call sites.

  Optional parameters are how a closure-to-parameter refactor loses data without
  the compiler noticing. If the caller must supply it, say so in the type.

  THE FIRST-MATCH TRAP AGAIN, IN TYPESCRIPT THIS TIME.
  `if (part.enabled === false) continue` appears TWICE — once in
  scanBodyVariables, once in scanBodyPrompts. A control using .replace(old,new,1)
  hit the first and reported 0 failing, which reads as a missing test. Same
  lesson as the perl -0p s/// that hit line 1799 instead of 12619 in app.go:
  an anchor that is not unique is not an anchor. Re-run against occurrence 2, it
  fails 1.

  Also: node --test's ESM resolver does not resolve extensionless imports the
  way Vite does, so a src/lib module importing another needs the .ts extension.
  Enabled allowImportingTsExtensions + noEmit, which satisfies tsc,
  svelte-check, Vite and node --test at once.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — third slice, global search ===

  src/lib/globalSearch.ts — 125 lines, 15 tests. App.svelte 11,959 -> 11,866.
  Frontend suite 295 -> 310.

  THE RANKING IS THE FEATURE. Someone types three characters, presses Enter and
  gets result zero, so the order decides which request opens. A ranking that put
  a URL match above a name match would send people somewhere they did not ask
  for while looking, from outside, like search is simply bad at its job. Ranks:

      0  collection
      1  folder, and every collection when the query is empty
      2  request matched on NAME
      3  request matched on URL
      4  request matched on path or method

  MY ASSUMPTION WAS WRONG, NOT THE CODE. I asserted that a request name match
  takes result zero for the query "invoice", and it does not: the FOLDER
  "invoices" is rank 1 and outranks the request "Create invoice" at rank 2.
  That is deliberate — the container is the broader answer to an ambiguous
  query. Test corrected to record the real ordering, and a second test added
  specifically for it, since it is surprising enough that the next person will
  assume what I assumed.

  The rank test now uses "health", which matches only a request name, because
  any query that also hits a folder cannot demonstrate the name-vs-url ordering
  at all.

  Controls: OR-ing the terms fails 2, swapping the name/url ranks fails 1,
  dropping the meaningless-query guard fails 1, disabling path matching fails 1,
  sorting by name instead of rank fails 2, not collapsing slashes fails 1, not
  lowercasing fails 1.

  Tightened the control runner: it now refuses a pattern that is NOT UNIQUE in
  the file rather than silently replacing the first occurrence. That is the trap
  that produced a false "0 failing" twice already — once in app.go at 10,800
  lines' distance, once in requestScanning.ts between two near-identical
  scanners.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 4 COMPLETE — the transport cache folds into internal/transport ===

  http_transport_cache.go 694 -> 160 lines. internal/transport/cache.go is 545.
  The 10 cache-internal tests moved with it.

  SPLIT ALONG A LINE THE CODE ITSELF DRAWS. The file had two halves:
    - the cache machinery (Spec, Cache, eviction, CA loading, proxy resolution)
      -> moved, it is transport's job
    - the WIRING: packageTransportCache, sharedCredentialHTTPClient,
      sharedPACHTTPClient and the init() that calls awsv4.SetHTTPClient
      -> STAYED, because its own comment says why: "Wiring it here rather than
         in the package keeps the dependency pointing one way."

  Moving the wiring too would have made internal/transport import
  internal/auth/awsv4 purely to connect a seam — a new edge in an otherwise
  clean DAG, added for no reason but proximity. The composition root belongs in
  main. Reading the comment before moving the file is what caught that.

  Spec's 12 fields had to be exported, which is a real widening of the package's
  surface and the honest cost of this move. They are a cache KEY description
  rather than behaviour, and every one carries a doc comment explaining what it
  contributes to the key, so the documentation travelled with them.

  A RENAME RULE THAT MATCHED PROSE AND LOCALS. Capitalising the Spec fields with
  a `(?<![\w.])proxyURL(?=[ ,)])` rule — added so the doc comments would read
  correctly — also renamed a LOCAL VARIABLE in two functions:
      ProxyURL, err := ManualProxyURL(...)
      spec.ProxyURL = proxyURL            // now undefined
  and separately renamed candidate.source in collection_import.go, an unrelated
  struct. Both were loud compile errors. Third variant of the same lesson: a
  rename rule wide enough to catch comments is wide enough to catch identifiers
  that only look alike.

  Also deduplicated the internal/transport import in app.go, which the move left
  imported twice (once plain, once as xport). staticcheck ST1019 caught it.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — fourth slice, URL segments ===

  src/lib/urlSegments.ts — 128 lines, 14 tests. App.svelte 11,866 -> 11,777.
  Frontend suite 310 -> 324.

  This is what the variable overlay renders on top of the URL input, so a wrong
  split highlights the wrong characters or points a tooltip at an editor that
  does not own the value.

  TWO TOKEN SYNTAXES THAT LOOK ALIKE AND RESOLVE FROM DIFFERENT PLACES:
    {{name}}  a variable, resolved through the scope chain
    /:name    a PATH PARAMETER, resolved from the request's own table
  Path tokens are recognised ONLY when the caller supplies pathParams. The URL
  bar does; the same overlay rendered over a header or body value does not,
  because a ":" there is ordinary text — a port number, a timestamp, a JSON key.
  Both directions are tested.

  The `key` carries the match OFFSET, not just the name. Two occurrences of one
  variable in a URL must be distinct rows or Svelte's keyed each reuses a node
  and the tooltip opens over the wrong occurrence. Control confirms.

  A property worth stating: the concatenated segment texts must reproduce the
  input EXACTLY, or the overlay drifts out of alignment with the text beneath
  it. Tested across five shapes; dropping the leading-text segment fails 3 and
  dropping the trailing one fails 5.

  Note the whitespace case: {{  host  }} reports the name as "host" but keeps
  its text as "{{  host  }}" — the name is for lookup, the text is for
  rendering, and conflating them shifts every character after it.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — fifth slice, and a DUPLICATE IMPLEMENTATION found ===

  App.svelte 11,777 -> 11,767. Frontend suite 324 -> 330.

  This slice was meant to be routine extraction and instead found that
  keyBindingSignature EXISTED TWICE: once in src/lib/keybindings.ts (tested,
  used by findKeyBindingCollisions for the preset checker) and once in
  App.svelte (untested, used by the settings-UI validator).

  THEY WERE NOT THE SAME FUNCTION. Two differences:
    1. App.svelte lowercased its input; the module did not. A case-sensitive
       signature means "Ctrl+K" and "ctrl+k" are DIFFERENT shortcuts, so the
       collision check waves a duplicate through.
    2. The module sorted the non-modifier keys; App.svelte did not. Only visible
       for a multi-key combo, which is exactly what the hidden tab-range binding
       (command+1 ... command+8) is.

  They agreed today only by a COINCIDENCE OF INPUT: every built-in combo is
  lowercase, and the capture handler does event.key.toLowerCase(), and every
  user-facing combo is single-keyed. Latent, not live — but two implementations
  of "is this the same shortcut" is a bug waiting for the first uppercase combo
  or the first multi-key binding.

  Unified on one, in the module, keeping BOTH stricter behaviours (lowercase and
  sorted keys). Deleted App.svelte's copy along with keyBindingParts,
  isKeyBindingModifier and keyBindingSeparator, which were duplicated alongside
  it. Six tests added pinning the two properties that differed. Controls:
  removing the lowercase fails 1, removing the key sort fails 1, removing the
  modifier sort fails 2, adding "meta" to the modifier set fails 1.

  Worth naming the pattern: this duplicate was invisible to every gate. It
  compiled, it type-checked, it linted, and both copies passed their own tests —
  because only one of them HAD tests. The way it surfaced was reading the target
  module before extracting into it, which is the same habit that caught the
  transport-wiring dependency direction two slices ago.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — sixth slice, the request command strip ===

  src/lib/workbench/commandState.ts — 124 lines, 15 tests.
  App.svelte 11,767 -> 11,689. Frontend suite 330 -> 345.

  I SAID THE PURE-LOGIC SEAM WAS "LARGELY MINED OUT" LAST SLICE. Re-scanning
  instead of trusting that produced a 51-line function still sitting there.
  Worth remembering: I asserted exhaustion from an impression rather than a
  measurement, and the measurement disagreed.

  TWO OF THE LABELS THIS COMPUTES ARE SECURITY POSTURE. The "TLS verify"/"TLS
  off" cue and the proxy cue are the ONLY place the UI says how a request will
  actually travel. A wrong label says verified when it is not, or direct when it
  is proxied, and nothing else on screen contradicts it.

  The TLS cue is AND-ed, not OR-ed: verification is on only when the request has
  not disabled it AND the global preference has not. Either switch being off
  means unverified traffic, so either must read "TLS off". Flipping that to OR
  fails a test.

  The proxy cue's precedence is collection-then-preference, with three distinct
  collection states — off / manual / inherit — where "entirely unset" means
  INHERIT rather than off. Reordering the disabled and inherit checks fails 2.

  Threaded activeWorkspace?.scratchCollectionId through as a parameter rather
  than closing over it, and left a two-line wrapper in App.svelte so the other
  requestIsTransient call sites did not all have to change. A wrapper that
  exists to keep a diff small is worth saying out loud rather than pretending
  the signature was always this shape.

=== PHASE 0 CLOSED — the Windows build compiles again ===

  GOOS=windows go build ./...  now succeeds. So do linux and darwin.

  PROCEEDED ON THE DOCUMENTED RECOMMENDATION WITHOUT AN ANSWER. I flagged this
  decision across many turns and got none, while release.yml kept building
  windows/amd64 on every push to main. Stating the assumption and proceeding
  beats leaving a broken release indefinitely; the assumption is written down
  here and in the plan, and every security test still passes.

  THREE BUILD-TAG PAIRS, one per capability:
    filelock_unix.go / filelock_windows.go
      flock(LOCK_EX) vs LockFileEx over the whole file. NOT identical: flock is
      advisory, LockFileEx is mandatory, so a second Windows window is refused
      rather than made to wait. Stricter, in the direction this lock exists for.
    process_alive_unix.go / process_alive_windows.go
      kill(pid, 0) vs OpenProcess + GetExitCodeProcess == STILL_ACTIVE.
      Deliberately opposite on failure: unix treats EPERM as ALIVE (the process
      exists, it is another user's, and stealing their lock would be wrong);
      Windows treats an OpenProcess failure as DEAD (the common cause is that
      the pid is gone, and an unbreakable stale lock is worse).
    workspace_dir_unix.go / workspace_dir_windows.go
      the symlink-safe path operations — the part that needed a decision.

  THE SECURITY PROPERTY IS NOT IDENTICAL ACROSS PLATFORMS, AND THAT IS WRITTEN
  DOWN IN THE CODE. unix opens each component with O_NOFOLLOW: the kernel
  refuses a symlinked component, ATOMICALLY, so there is no window between
  deciding a path is safe and using it. Windows has no openat/O_NOFOLLOW, so it
  uses os.Root — which constrains every operation to stay inside the opened
  directory at the syscall layer — PLUS an explicit Lstat rejection of any
  symlink, restoring the "no symlink at all" rule.

  Residual difference: a TOCTOU window between the Lstat and the open on
  Windows. An attacker who can create a symlink inside the workspace between
  those two calls could win it. They still cannot ESCAPE the workspace, because
  os.Root enforces that regardless. So the worst case is redirection within the
  workspace, not outside it. On unix there is no window at all. Documented in
  workspace_dir_windows.go rather than glossed.

  Also: no directory fsync on Windows, because there is no equivalent. The
  rename stays atomic with respect to readers; only the crash-durability of the
  directory entry is weaker.

  UNIX BEHAVIOUR IS BYTE-FOR-BYTE UNCHANGED. The four functions moved verbatim
  into workspace_dir_unix.go. All 14 recovery/containment security tests pass.
  Controls: dropping O_NOFOLLOW from the leading-path walk fails 1, from the
  file read fails 1, and dropping the existing-symlink rejection fails 1. The
  fourth control (O_NOFOLLOW on the temp-file create) is an EQUIVALENT mutation
  — O_EXCL plus a random UUID name already makes a planted symlink impossible
  at that name.

  ADDED THE GATE THAT DID NOT EXIST. ci.yml now cross-compiles windows/amd64 and
  darwin/arm64. The go job only ever built Linux, which is why four untagged
  unix imports could sit there while every check stayed green.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — seventh slice, sidebar filtering ===

  src/lib/sidebarFilter.ts — 72 lines, 15 tests. App.svelte 11,689 -> 11,653.
  Frontend suite 345 -> 360.

  THE RULE THAT SHAPES EVERYTHING HERE: a query matching the COLLECTION shows
  every request inside it. Someone typing a collection name is asking to see
  that collection, and narrowing to the few requests that happen to repeat the
  name in their own title would hide most of the answer.

  A CONTROL AT 0 THAT TAUGHT ME WHICH TEST WAS REAL. Removing the
  collectionMatches shortcut from filteredItems failed nothing, because
  requestMatches ALREADY includes collection.name in its candidate list — so a
  NAME query keeps everything with or without the shortcut. Testing that proved
  nothing at all.

  The shortcut also covers the collection's FORMAT and PATH, which requestMatches
  does not. Those are the queries it actually decides. Added
  filteredItems(collection, 'bru') and filteredItems(collection, '/repos/billing')
  as the discriminating cases; the control now fails 1.

  This is the same shape as the gitworkbench validators and the requestScanning
  scanners: two overlapping predicates where the obvious test exercises the
  overlap and proves nothing. Worth a habit — when a control comes back 0, ask
  what the two paths have IN COMMON before assuming the test is missing.

  Also recorded: searchHit lowercases the CANDIDATE, not the query, so callers
  must lowercase first. Written down because it is exactly the asymmetry that
  reads as a bug until you find the call site.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — eighth slice, auth and proxy defaults ===

  src/lib/authDefaults.ts — 58 lines, 13 tests. App.svelte 11,653 -> 11,621.
  Frontend suite 360 -> 373.

  These run on every keystroke in the auth and proxy editors, merging three
  things: what is stored, what the user just changed, and what the protocol
  requires when neither says anything.

  THE ORDER IS THE WHOLE THING, AND IT IS EASY TO GET BACKWARDS.
    merge first (stored <- update), THEN default the still-empty fields.
  Default-then-merge would overwrite what the user typed. Defaulting with ?? in
  place of || would make a CLEARED field impossible to clear — it would keep the
  default forever, because "" is not nullish. Both have controls; the ?? swap
  fails 1.

  The OAuth2 defaults are protocol REQUIREMENTS rather than preferences: a token
  request with no grant type, or a bearer token with no header prefix, is one
  the server rejects. Writing them at edit time means the stored request is
  always sendable.

  And they are written only when the request actually uses OAuth2 OR the oauth2
  block was the thing edited — otherwise every basic-auth request would grow a
  pointless oauth2 block. Both halves of that condition have a control.

  FIXED THE CONTROL RUNNER'S OWN REPORTING. It printed "NOT UNIQUE(0)" for a
  pattern that was not found at all, conflating "your anchor is ambiguous" with
  "your anchor is wrong". Four controls reported that here — all four were
  indentation drift from the dedent, not ambiguity. Now it says NOT FOUND or
  AMBIGUOUS(n). A control runner that misreports why it refused is a control
  runner that gets ignored.

=== RESTRUCTURE PHASE 6 — ninth slice, content-type derivation ===

  src/lib/contentTypes.ts — 42 lines, 8 tests. App.svelte 11,621 -> 11,592.
  Frontend suite 373 -> 381.

  Both functions run while someone is typing, and both get WRITTEN into the
  request rather than merely displayed. A wrong guess is saved and sent.

  Pinned: the query and fragment are stripped before reading the extension,
  because a path pasted from a browser carries them and "report.pdf?download=1"
  is still a PDF. An unknown extension yields "" rather than
  application/octet-stream — an empty content type lets the server sniff, a
  wrong one overrides it. Text types carry charset=utf-8 and binary ones do not,
  and JSON does not because the format defines UTF-8.

  RECORDED A LIMITATION RATHER THAN CLAIMING IT WAS CORRECT.
  responseExampleBodyTypeForContentType matches "application/json" as a
  SUBSTRING, so application/vnd.api+json falls through to 'text' and renders
  unformatted. That is a real thing someone will hit. The test asserts the
  current behaviour and says why it is wrong-looking, rather than either
  pretending it is right or quietly changing a matcher I have no failing report
  about. Changing it is a one-line fix whenever someone decides to.

=== CONSOLIDATION — testing what the restructure itself left thin ===

  Rather than keep extracting ever-smaller App.svelte functions (the last two
  slices moved 32 and 29 lines), measured what the six NEW Go packages actually
  cover and filled the weakest spots.

  visualizer     81.5% -> 93.4%
  workspacestate 81.6% -> 85.6%
  overall        83.3% -> 83.4%

  Worth noting the measurement trap: `go test ./internal/X -cover` reported
  gitworkbench at 25% and localserver at 21%, which looks alarming and is
  meaningless — those packages are largely exercised by package main's tests,
  and a per-package run cannot see them. The -coverpkg profile across the whole
  suite gives 86.6% and 91.2%. A coverage number without knowing which tests
  contributed is not a number.

  THE VISUALIZER'S VALUE SEMANTICS decide what a user's template renders.
  All three take interface{} straight out of encoding/json, so every value is
  nil, bool, float64, string, []interface{} or map[string]interface{} — there
  are no ints, and a test passing one exercises a case the renderer never sees.

  Pinned: numbers never render in scientific notation (%v turns 1000000 into
  1e+06, and an id shown that way is simply wrong on screen — control fails 2);
  a whole number stays whole, so an id is "42" not "42.0"; truthiness follows
  JavaScript for every JSON kind INCLUDING the surprise that the string "false"
  is truthy, kept deliberately so a template behaves as its author expects from
  the browser; {{#each}} over a single object iterates ONCE rather than
  rendering nothing, while nil iterates ZERO times — wrapping nil as one item
  would print empty rows for data that was never there.

  SameCanonicalWorkspacePath was at 0.0%. It decides whether two spellings name
  one workspace, and the interesting case is that an INVALID identity must not
  compare equal to anything — including another invalid one. Returning true
  there would make every malformed path look like the same workspace. Control
  confirms.

=== DUPLICATE SWEEP — hunting the keyBindingSignature pattern deliberately ===

  Finding keyBindingSignature implemented twice, with the copies DIVERGED, was
  luck: I happened to read the target module before extracting into it. So this
  pass looked for the same shape on purpose.

  FRONTEND: compared 646 function names defined in App.svelte against 100
  exported by src/lib. One hit — requestIsTransient — and that is the wrapper I
  added deliberately and documented. Clean.

  GO: compared 707 unexported functions in package main against 727 exported
  from internal/. 34 name matches, of which 32 are the intentional one-line
  delegations in app_shims.go. TWO WERE REAL SECOND IMPLEMENTATIONS:

    sameCanonicalWorkspacePath   workspace_window_runtime.go  (5 call sites)
      vs workspacestate.SameCanonicalWorkspacePath (1 call site)
    firstYAMLString              app_yaml_codec.go            (36 call sites)
      vs scalar.FirstYAMLString

  Neither had diverged. That is not reassurance — it is exactly what I would
  have said about keyBindingSignature the day before finding it had.

  PROVED EQUIVALENCE BEFORE COLLAPSING, rather than assuming it. A throwaway
  differential test ran both pairs over 100 path combinations and 54 map/keyset
  combinations, including the cases that would expose a difference: empty,
  whitespace-only, traversal, a NUL byte, a trailing slash, differing case,
  absent keys, non-string values, nested containers. Both pairs agreed
  everywhere. Then the main copies became delegations, so there is one
  implementation each.

  Worth doing this way round: had they differed, the differential test would
  have said WHICH inputs, instead of me discovering it from a bug report.

  Re-scanned afterwards: no non-shim duplicates remain outside app_shims.go.

=== CROSS-LANGUAGE PARITY — the scanner/sender invariant ===

  The same logic exists in Go and TypeScript for prompt scanning, and I had
  tested each side separately without ever asserting they AGREE. Compared the
  two dispatches field by field, then wrote the invariant that actually matters.

  IT IS ONE-DIRECTIONAL, AND ONLY ONE DIRECTION IS A BUG:
    scanner is a SUPERSET of sender  -> the user is asked for something unused.
                                        Annoying.
    scanner is a SUBSET of sender    -> a field goes out with a literal
                                        {{?name}} in it. The server rejects it,
                                        or accepts it, and nothing in the UI
                                        points at the prompt that never came.

  So the property is: EVERY FIELD THE SENDER USES IS A FIELD THE SCANNER
  VISITS. internal/scripting/prompt_send_parity_test.go states it per body mode
  with a distinct marker in every field, so a mode added to one switch and not
  the other fails there rather than in someone's request.

  THE TWO SIDES DIFFER ON DISABLED PARTS, AND IT ERRS SAFE:
    Go   `if !part.Enabled { continue }`   — absent enabled means FALSE (bool
                                             zero value), so the part is skipped
    TS   `if (part.enabled === false)`     — undefined is NOT false, so scanned
  A part with `enabled` absent is therefore scanned by the frontend and neither
  scanned nor SENT by Go. That is an extra question, never a missing one. Go's
  scanner and sender use the identical predicate (`!part.Enabled` against
  `if part.Enabled`), which is what makes the subset case impossible.

  One control was equivalent: dropping scanText(body.FilePath) and
  scanText(body.FileContentType) from the file case fails nothing, because
  FileBodyEntriesOf SYNTHESISES an entry from exactly those two fields for the
  legacy single-file shape, so the loop below already covers them. Redundant
  lines, not a test gap.

=== PHASE 6 — first COMPONENT extraction, and why it is safer here than expected ===

  src/lib/SidebarSearch.svelte — 36 lines. App.svelte 11,592 -> 11,582.

  Held this back for several turns wanting direction, then proceeded the same
  way as the Windows decision: flagged repeatedly, no answer, and the safest
  available approach is knowable.

  THE RISK I EXPECTED DOES NOT EXIST IN THIS CODEBASE. Moving markup into a
  child normally risks losing its styling, because Svelte scopes a component's
  <style> block to that component — the markup moves, the CSS does not, and the
  result renders unstyled with svelte-check, the build and every test still
  green.

  Checked instead of assumed: App.svelte has NO <style> block at all. Every
  class it uses comes from src/style.css, imported globally in main.ts. Only 11
  of 66 .svelte files scope styles, so global CSS is the convention here rather
  than the exception. Svelte components also add no wrapper element, so the DOM
  is byte-identical after the move, and there are no child combinators
  (`.workspace-rail > .search-section`) that a structural change could break.

  That makes component extraction in this repo materially lower risk than the
  general case — worth knowing before the next one, and worth having verified
  rather than discovered.

  Both props are $bindable, following KeyValueTable's precedent and for the same
  reason it documents: the parent reads `value` to filter the tree AND writes it
  from the command palette and the ⌘F handler, so a non-bindable prop would let
  typing update a local copy while the tree kept the old filter. `input` is
  bindable because a keyboard shortcut outside this component focuses it.

  `wails build -clean` succeeds: frontend dependencies installed, frontend

  bundle is a valid arm64 Mach-O with the frontend embedded.

  That is the check thirty-four commits of structural change had never had. Unit
  tests and static analysis say the parts are right; only this says the whole
  thing still assembles into a shippable app.

  The build's only tree change was FILE MODE (755 -> 644) on three generated
  wailsjs/runtime files, zero content. Restored.

  UNTRACKED THE LOOP HARNESS'S STATE FILE. .claude/ralph-loop.local.md holds
  session-local bookkeeping — an iteration counter and a machine-specific
  session id — and 22 of my commits carried it as pure churn because I used
  `git add -A` throughout. It was first committed in a previous session
  (cf8f513), so it is not something I introduced, but it is something I kept
  propagating. Now gitignored; the file stays on disk, it just stops being
  rewritten in every commit.

  Worth naming the habit rather than just the fix: `git add -A` commits whatever
  happens to be dirty, including things another process is writing underneath
  you. It is convenient and it is how unrelated churn gets into a history.

  REMAINING VERIFICATION GAP, stated plainly: nothing here has RUN the app. The
  Go side has real runtime coverage (tests spin up HTTP, gRPC and mock servers),
  and the build proves it assembles, but no click-through has exercised the
  frontend against the Go bridge. `wails dev` on localhost:34115 is how that
  would be done.

=== RUNTIME VERIFICATION — the gap I kept naming, now closed ===

  Ran `wails dev` and drove the app through Playwright. This is the check that
  no amount of svelte-check, tsc, eslint, `go test` or `wails build` provides:
  whether the thing actually WORKS.

  WHAT WAS EXERCISED, end to end against real Go-backed state:
    - the app loads and the sidebar renders
    - typing "Echo" into the extracted SidebarSearch component propagates to the
      parent — the $bindable prop round-trips
    - the clear button appears ({#if value})
    - the count renders "1 matching requests" — matchCount flows back DOWN into
      the component, and the $derived showCount gates it
    - the tree filters to just "Echo JSON", with "R7 Persist" gone — that is
      sidebarFilter.ts, extracted three commits earlier, doing its job
  So one interaction covered the component extraction, the bindable round-trip
  and the filter module together.

  A ReferenceError THAT WAS NOT A BUG, and how I established that.
  The console showed `openRequestMenus is not defined`. Everything static had
  passed, so this looked like exactly the silent failure I had been warning
  about. Three checks before believing it:

    1. Is it mine? `git show 6a0fbea:frontend/src/App.svelte` — the same
       use-before-declare ordering existed at my second commit. Pre-existing.
    2. Can it actually fire? Every caller of closeRequestActionMenus runs after
       init, so the temporal dead zone should be unreachable.
    3. Does it survive a fresh load? The failing module URL carried Vite's HMR
       cache-buster (?t=1785073224068), so I reloaded clean.

  Fresh load: ZERO errors. It was a hot-reload artifact from Vite swapping
  App.svelte while I was editing it, not a fault in the code. Reported as such
  rather than filed as a bug or waved away.

  The WebSocket ERR_CONNECTION_REFUSED spam is expected and benign: a plain
  browser has no Wails IPC bridge. The app still renders and filters because
  that path does not need Go.

  Cleaned up: dev server stopped, console artifacts removed, and the file-mode
  churn `wails dev` left on three generated wailsjs/runtime files restored.

=== PHASE 6 — second component extraction, verified at runtime ===

  src/lib/SidebarHeader.svelte — the brand block and the New button.

  A CALLBACK prop this time rather than a bindable one, which is the other
  convention lib/ already uses (CommandOverflowMenu's onSelect). The child owns

    - clicking "New" fires onNew -> openCreationFlow
    - the "New request" dialog opens, correctly scoped to "Sample API"
