=== PHASE 6 — validateKeyBinding, finishing the keybinding unification ===

  Frontend suite 381 -> 386. App.svelte 11,569 -> 11,561.

  MEASURED BEFORE CLAIMING, having been wrong about this once already. 272 PURE
  functions totalling 1,717 lines still sit in App.svelte's script, against 374
  reactive ones totalling 3,577. So the pure seam is NOT exhausted — it is
  fragmented: the largest remaining pure function is 24 lines. Clusters are what
  is worth extracting now, not size.

  Also measured: the script is 7,943 lines against 3,626 of markup, so the bulk
  is logic rather than template, and all modals are already in lib/modals/.

  validateKeyBinding is the settings-UI validator whose OTHER half
  (keyBindingSignature) I unified earlier. It could not move as-is because it
  reaches appState through mergedKeyBinding, so the RULE moved and the MERGE
  stayed: the module takes resolved bindings and an OS, App.svelte supplies them.

  A DELIBERATE DIFFERENCE FROM ITS NEIGHBOUR, now documented in both.
  findKeyBindingCollisions SKIPS hidden definitions, because the tab-number row
  is declared both as a hidden range and as eight individual actions and
  counting both reports a collision that does not exist. validateKeyBinding does
  NOT skip them, because a hidden binding still OWNS its combo — telling a user
  Cmd+1 is free when switchToTab1 has it hands them a shortcut that never fires.
  Same data, opposite treatment, both correct.

  ANOTHER VACUOUS TEST CAUGHT BY A CONTROL. My "modifier order does not defeat
  the collision check" test built its different-order combo with
  'shift+bind+command+bind+s'.replace('shift+bind+', '') — which is
  'command+bind+s', raw-identical to the stored one. It passed whether the
  comparison used signatures or raw strings. Rewritten with a genuinely
  reordered combo (stored alt+command+k, typed command+alt+k); the control that
  swaps signature comparison for string equality now fails 1.

  That is the fourth vacuous test this restructure, and every one was found the
  same way: a control reporting 0, then asking what the two paths have in common.

=== PHASE 6 — response-example edit transforms ===

  src/lib/responseExampleEdits.ts — 122 lines, 18 tests.
  App.svelte 11,561 -> 11,526. Frontend suite 386 -> 404.

  A RESHAPE rather than motion, and the first one this phase. The three
  updateResponseExample* functions wrap a mutable draft, so they could not move
  as-is. What moved is the part that DECIDES something; the draft plumbing
  stayed. Each wrapper is now four lines.

  An example is what "copy as curl" regenerates from AND what the mock server
  answers with, so an edit that quietly drops a field produces a mock that
  behaves differently from the request that was saved.

  Behaviours pinned, each of which a user would notice going wrong:
    - the method is upper-cased: "get" and "GET" are one verb
    - editing the URL re-derives the query table, so the params panel and the
      URL cannot disagree about what will be sent — while KEEPING rows the user
      disabled
    - switching body mode initialises that mode's collection, or the editor
      binds a table to undefined and the first row typed is lost
    - `selected` on a file row is a RADIO: exactly one attachment is sent, so
      leaving two selected makes the choice depend on iteration order
    - editing a file path re-derives its content type, because the old one
      described the old file
    - the body type is re-derived ONLY when the Content-Type header's VALUE
      changed. Retyping the same value must not clobber a body type the user
      picked by hand, and RENAMING a header is not a statement about the body.

  That last one needed three separate controls to pin, because there are three
  ways to get it wrong: react to any header edit, react to an unchanged value,
  or report a change when the derived type is the same. All three fail a test.

=== PHASE 6 — sidebar virtualisation, and a THIRD implementation of one idea ===

  sidebarGroupWindow joins computeWindow in lib/virtualList.ts. 8 tests.
  App.svelte 11,526 -> 11,520. Frontend suite 404 -> 412.

  sidebarItemWindow was a SECOND virtualisation implementation: computeWindow
  (tested, used by the devtools network table) and this one (untested, used by
  the sidebar). Found by the same habit as keyBindingSignature — reading the
  target module before extracting into it.

  BUT THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN THEM IS DELIBERATE, and I nearly "fixed" it.
  computeWindow CLAMPS scrollTop against the content height, with a comment
  explaining exactly why: "a scrollTop left from a longer list — exactly what a
  filter produces — would otherwise put the window past the end and render
  nothing." The sidebar version does not clamp, and the sidebar IS filtered by
  the search box, so this looked like the documented bug sitting unfixed in a
  second copy.

  It is not. computeWindow describes ONE list filling its scroller. The sidebar
  is many groups sharing one scroller, and a group legitimately sits outside the
  viewport. Clamping per group would drag every off-screen group's window back
  onto its own content and render rows nobody can see. The correct behaviour is
  the [0, count] clamp AFTER applying the offset, which collapses start and end
  for a group above or below the viewport — rendering nothing, which is right.

  Tested both collapse directions explicitly, plus the stale-scrollTop case that
  filtering produces: every group still yields non-negative padding, non-
  inverted indices, and a constant total height.

  THE INVARIANT WORTH HAVING: padTop + rendered*rowHeight + padBottom always
  equals count*rowHeight. If it ever fails the group's height changes as it
  scrolls and everything below it jumps. Asserted across 15 scroll/offset
  combinations; dropping either spacer fails.

  Two same-shaped findings now, opposite conclusions: keyBindingSignature was a
  duplicate that had DIVERGED and needed collapsing; this one is a variant that
  SHOULD differ and needed documenting. Reading the neighbour found both; only
  reading the comment told them apart.

=== PHASE 6 — the network table's tri-state sort ===

  src/lib/networkSort.ts — 6 tests. App.svelte 11,520 -> 11,503.
  Frontend suite 412 -> 418.

  THE THIRD STATE IS THE POINT. A column header cycles ascending -> descending
  -> OFF, and the off state is the only route back to the log's natural
  chronological order, which is what makes a request sequence readable. A cycle
  that wrapped straight back to ascending would strand the user in a sorted view
  with no way out. Control confirms.

  Clicking a DIFFERENT column always starts ascending rather than inheriting the
  previous column's direction: the direction was a statement about the old
  column, and carrying it over lands the first click on a new column in a state
  nobody chose.

  TWO NEAR-IDENTICAL FUNCTIONS THAT MUST NOT BE MERGED, now documented.
  networkSortLabel and networkSortAriaValue differ in exactly one place: the
  unsorted case returns "" for the visible label and the string "none" for
  aria-sort. That is not redundancy — aria-sort has a fixed vocabulary in which
  "none" is the value, so returning "" would be an invalid attribute, while
  returning "none" to the template would print the word beside the heading.

  Rather than assert that in prose only, a test walks all 7 columns x 8 active
  states x 3 directions and asserts they agree on WHICH column is sorted and
  disagree only on how they spell "not this one". That is the property that
  makes both worth keeping.

  Third time this phase that two similar functions needed a decision:
    keyBindingSignature   duplicated and DIVERGED  -> collapsed into one
    sidebarGroupWindow    variant that SHOULD differ -> documented
    networkSortLabel/Aria near-identical, both right -> tested against each other
  Same shape, three different right answers. Reading the code decided each one.

=== PHASE 6 — keyboard event normalisation, completing the keybinding module ===

  normalizeEventKey joins lib/keybindings.ts. 6 tests.
  App.svelte 11,503 -> 11,488. Frontend suite 418 -> 424.

  That module now owns the whole keybinding story: the default table, presets,
  signatures, collision detection, validation, and now the bridge from a
  KeyboardEvent to the vocabulary the rest of it uses. It started this session
  holding only the table.

  THE SUBTLE PART IS event.code VERSUS event.key, and it is a real
  cross-platform behaviour that had no test.
    event.key  = the character the LAYOUT PRODUCES
    event.code = the PHYSICAL key
  On a French AZERTY keyboard the position where QWERTY has Q reports
  event.key "a". Reading the key would make a shortcut stored as command+q fire
  on what the user sees as A, and command+a not fire at all. Reading the code
  means Cmd+Q is the same physical chord everywhere, which is what a keyboard
  shortcut IS.

  Tested with {key:'a', code:'KeyQ'} and, as a round trip, that a chord captured
  from {key:'ß', code:'KeyS'} produces the same SIGNATURE as the stored
  command+bind+s. That last one is the property that actually matters: capture
  and storage have to meet.

  Also asserted that the four modifier normalisations agree with
  isKeyBindingModifier — a captured "command" that the module did not recognise
  as a modifier would fail validation for a reason nobody could see.

  REMOVED A REDUNDANT BRANCH, ON EVIDENCE RATHER THAN TASTE. A control deleting
  `if (event.key.length === 1) return event.key.toLowerCase()` failed NOTHING,
  because the final line lower-cases the key anyway. Two fallbacks that agree;
  now one. The control is what justified the deletion — without it I would have
  been guessing that they were equivalent.

=== PHASE 6 — the cookie jar panel ===

  src/lib/cookieView.ts — 12 tests. App.svelte 11,488 -> 11,439.
  Frontend suite 424 -> 436.

  None of this decides what gets SENT — the Go cookie jar does that — but all of
  it decides what the user BELIEVES is stored, and a cookie hidden from the
  panel is one nobody can inspect or delete.

  Behaviours pinned, each with a reason:
    - flags render "none" rather than blank when nothing is set. A blank cell
      reads as "not loaded yet"; "none" states that the cookie carries no
      protections, which for a cookie is information rather than its absence.
    - SEARCHING INCLUDES THE FLAGS, so "httpOnly" or "sameSite=lax" finds every
      cookie carrying that protection. That is the query someone runs when
      auditing a jar, and matching only names would answer it with nothing.
    - a cookie with no domain groups under "(no domain)" rather than being
      dropped. It is still in the jar; hiding it means nobody can delete it.
    - within a domain the order is PATH then name, so two cookies of the same
      name scoped to different paths sit together and read distinctly. That pair
      is exactly the confusion the panel exists to resolve.
    - each group previews ONLY its own cookies. A control that made a group
      preview the whole jar fails — a domain must not advertise another
      domain's cookies as its own header.

  The form defaults are the safe reading of an unfinished cookie: path "/"
  because a cookie with no path is a cookie for nothing, session true because no
  expiry IS a session cookie, hostOnly true because the narrower scope is the
  one to opt out of rather than into.

=== PHASE 6 — the DevTools console aggregation ===

  src/lib/devToolsConsole.ts — 7 tests. App.svelte 11,439 -> 11,416.
  Frontend suite 436 -> 443.

  A pm.console.log() lands on the RESPONSE of whichever request produced it, so
  the logs are scattered across every collection and item. This console is the
  only place they are visible together, which is what makes "which request
  printed that?" answerable — and a log arriving without its origin is a log
  nobody can act on.

  Pinned:
    - the source breadcrumb OMITS an empty folder path rather than rendering
      "Collection /  / Request". A log whose origin reads with a gap in it looks
      like the origin was lost.
    - the original log fields survive the tagging. Dropping the spread fails 3
      tests, including the one asserting a script's `args` array is not lost.
    - order is collection, then item, then emission — NOT chronological across
      requests. Logs from one run belong together; interleaving two requests by
      timestamp makes a single script's output impossible to follow. Written
      down because it looks like a bug until you know why.

  scriptLogs is read through a cast because it is not on the generated Response
  type — the Go side attaches it and regenerating bindings has never surfaced
  it. Kept as a cast in ONE place rather than widening the shared type, and said
  so in the code, so the looseness is contained and findable.

=== PHASE 6 — the remaining response-example edits ===

  responseExampleEdits.ts gains the response-payload field edit and the file-row
  removal. 9 more tests (18 -> 27). App.svelte 11,416 -> 11,402.
  Frontend suite 443 -> 452.

  TWO NUMERIC FIELDS THAT MUST NOT BE STRINGS. status and size arrive from text
  inputs and are compared numerically everywhere downstream — a status stored as
  "404" sorts beside "40" and fails every `< 300` check the response tone uses.
  Control confirms: storing the raw value fails 2.

  `|| 0` RATHER THAN LEAVING NaN, and that is a real choice not a tic. A
  half-typed or cleared field parses to NaN; NaN in a saved example renders as
  the text "NaN" and silently breaks every comparison the number existed for.
  Zero is visible and honest. Control: dropping the fallback fails 1.

  DELETING AN ATTACHMENT HAS TO KEEP EXACTLY ONE SELECTED. Removing the selected
  file must promote another, or the example is left holding files with nothing
  to send. Removing an unselected one promotes nothing — UNLESS the list had no
  selection at all, which is a broken state worth repairing whenever it is
  noticed. Both halves of that condition have their own control, and the second
  (`removedSelected` alone, dropping the repair) fails 1.

=== PHASE 6 — cookie expiry, and a Go/JS boundary artefact ===

  cookieView.ts gains the expiry formatters and the edit-form loader.
  12 tests -> 20. App.svelte 11,402 -> 11,381. Frontend suite 452 -> 460.

  A ZERO time.Time CROSSES THE BOUNDARY AS A REAL DATE. Go marshals an unset
  time.Time as 0001-01-01T00:00:00Z, which JavaScript parses happily — it is not
  NaN, it is a date almost two millennia ago. So `Number.isNaN(...)` alone is
  not enough, and the `getFullYear() <= 1` guard is what stops the form filling
  with a value the user never set. Both call sites need it; both have a control,
  and both fail without the guard.

  This is the kind of thing that only shows up at a language boundary: neither
  side is wrong on its own terms. Go's zero value is a legitimate "unset", and
  JavaScript's Date is legitimately happy to represent year 1.

  Every route to "no usable expiry" ends at the word "session" rather than blank
  or "Invalid Date", because a cookie with no expiry IS a session cookie — the
  word is the accurate answer, not a fallback standing in for one.

  A session cookie loads with a BLANK expiry even when one is stored: a datetime
  input showing a date beside a ticked "session" box says two contradictory
  things, and whichever the user changes next, the other is a surprise.

=== PHASE 6 — notification presentation ===

  src/lib/notificationView.ts — 11 tests. App.svelte 11,381 -> 11,360.
  Frontend suite 460 -> 471.

  A notification is the app's only way to report something the user did not ask
  about — a failed background write, a completed run, an expired token. Every
  field has a fallback chain and the reason is always the same: a notification
  that renders blank is one nobody can act on.

  TWO SPELLINGS PER SEVERITY, BECAUSE THE PRODUCERS DISAGREE. The Go side and
  the script runtime emit both "warn"/"warning" and "error"/"danger". A severity
  that fell through to "info" would colour an ERROR the same as a routine
  message — the one case where the colour is the whole point. Each spelling has
  its own control; dropping "warn" fails 2.

  AN INCONSISTENT COMPARATOR IS WORSE THAN A WRONG ORDER. An unparseable
  timestamp returns 0 rather than NaN, because NaN in a sort comparator makes
  the result implementation-defined in V8 — unrelated entries move. Sorting is
  also over a COPY: sorting in place would reorder live app state under the UI.

  Blank rather than "Invalid Date" for an unusable timestamp: blank reads as "no
  time recorded", which is true, while "Invalid Date" reads as a bug in the app
  and tells the user nothing about their notification.

  One asymmetry worth having: the TITLE falls back all the way to the literal
  word "Notification", but the DESCRIPTION stops at "". An empty description is
  legitimate when the title already says everything, and inventing text there
  would put words in the notification's mouth.

=== PHASE 6 — the .env parser ===

  src/lib/dotEnv.ts — 13 tests. App.svelte 11,360 -> 11,342.
  Frontend suite 471 -> 484.

  These rows become the process.env values a request interpolates, so a line
  parsed wrongly means a request sends the wrong secret, or none.

  Pinned:
    - the split is at the FIRST "=" only. Connection strings, base64 and JWTs
      all contain them, and splitting on every one truncates the secret at its
      first padding character. Control fails 2.
    - a leading `export ` is stripped, but only as a whole word — a variable
      genuinely named `exported` must not lose its first characters.
    - lineIndex counts EVERY line including skipped ones, so writing an edit
      back lands on the line it came from.
    - a leading "=" is skipped: an empty variable name is not addressable.

  A FIFTH VACUOUS TEST, SAME DISCOVERY ROUTE. Removing the "#" comment skip
  failed NOTHING, because my comment fixtures ("# a comment") have no "=" and
  the malformed-line check already drops them. The shape that discriminates is a
  COMMENTED-OUT VARIABLE — "# TOKEN=old" — which without the rule becomes an
  editable row named "# TOKEN". That is the common case too: people comment a
  secret out rather than delete it. Added; the control now fails 1.

  Five vacuous tests this restructure, every one found by a control reporting 0
  and then asking what the two paths have in common. It is the single most
  productive check I have used here — more than coverage, more than lint.

=== PHASE 6 — network row sorting, completing networkSort.ts ===

  networkSort.ts gains the sort values and the row sort. 6 tests -> 17.
  App.svelte 11,342 -> 11,302. Frontend suite 484 -> 495.

  Pinned:
    - NUMBERS COMPARE NUMERICALLY. As strings, 404 sorts before 5 — the one
      ordering a status column must never produce. Control fails 2.
    - the path KEEPS its query string, because two rows to one path with
      different parameters are different requests and a table rendering them
      identically is useless for what the log is opened for. Control fails 2.
    - sorting OFF returns the SAME ARRAY, not a copy. The off state exists to
      show arrival order; returning a copy would let a caller re-sort it and
      lose the thing the state is for.
    - sorting is over a copy otherwise, since sorting in place reorders the live
      log under the table.
    - a malformed URL still yields a domain and path. The request happened, and
      showing what was attempted beats a blank cell.

  A SIXTH GAP OF THE SAME KIND. `row.status ?? 0` could be removed with no test
  failing, because my missing-value test used `size`. Each numeric column needs
  its own case: a missing value falls out of the typeof-number branch into
  STRING comparison, which still produces a deterministic — and wrong — order,
  so one column proves nothing about the others. Now looped over size, status
  and duration; the control fails.

=== PHASE 6 — workbench layout: the two draggable dividers ===

  New src/lib/workbench/layout.ts, 16 tests. App.svelte 11,302 -> 11,280.
  Frontend suite 495 -> 511.

  Pinned:
    - AN UNKNOWN SCOPE PRODUCES NO KEY. The scope arrives from an async
      GetWebStorageScope(), so it is "" for the first frames of startup. An
      unscoped fallback key would be SHARED BY EVERY WORKSPACE WINDOW, and their
      layouts would overwrite each other on every drag. Control fails 1.
    - AN ABSENT ENTRY IS NOT A NUMBER. Number(null) and Number('') are both 0,
      which is finite — so treating a missing entry as a number clamps it to the
      MINIMUM, and a window that never saved a layout opens with the narrowest
      possible sidebar instead of the default. Control fails 2.
    - a non-finite width clamps instead of propagating. Math.max returns NaN for
      a NaN argument, so the clamp does not stop it on its own, and a NaN width
      is dropped by CSS — the shell renders with NO SIDEBAR. Control fails 1.
    - the split is NOT rounded, unlike the width. Rounding a fraction collapses
      every divider position to 0 or 1. Control fails 9.
    - the two orientations read different axes. Compact CSS always stacks the
      panes whatever the persisted preference says, so both branches are live.
      Control fails 2.
    - a zero-sized workbench leaves the split alone. Measured before layout it
      has height 0, and the NaN from dividing by it sticks in the flex-basis
      until the next successful drag. Control fails 1.
    - restore takes the CURRENT values, not the defaults, so half a saved layout
      leaves the other divider where the user just dragged it. Control fails 2.

  Seven controls, seven failures. No vacuous test this round.

=== PHASE 6 — the OpenAPI sync panel ===

  New src/lib/openApiSync.ts, 22 tests. App.svelte 11,280 -> 11,221.
  Frontend suite 511 -> 533.

  The decisions are the reason this is worth isolating. Each one chooses whether
  an endpoint's local edits survive the next apply, and a wrong one has no
  symptom except a request the user had customised coming back as the spec's.

  Pinned:
    - AN UNRECOGNISED DECISION IS REPLACED, NOT CARRIED. The map is rebuilt from
      whatever it held before, so a value left by an older build or a renamed
      decision would ride along and be handed to the backend as an endpoint's
      disposition. Control fails 1.
    - IDS NO LONGER PRESENT ARE DROPPED, because the map is built fresh rather
      than merged. A decision for an endpoint the spec no longer has would
      accumulate forever and re-apply if the id came back. Control fails 1.
    - a decision the user made SURVIVES a re-check, since the dialog is often
      reopened between deciding and applying.
    - autoCheck is `!== false`, not truthy. A config saved before the flag
      existed has it undefined; reading it as truthy silently stops polling for
      every collection connected by an older build. Control fails 4.
    - a 0 or negative interval falls back. It becomes a setInterval that fires
      continuously — a request flood at the spec's host. Control fails 1.
    - A FAILED CHECK REPORTS THE FAILURE AND NOTHING ELSE. Beside it, "Updates
      found" reads as though the spec were still tracked. Control fails 1.
    - the drift labels are stated from the COLLECTION's point of view. "missing"
      and "local-only" are written from the spec's; rendering them raw tells the
      user their new request was deleted. Control fails 1.

  A SEVENTH GAP OF THE SAME KIND, and this one was a WRONG COMMENT as much as a
  weak test. I claimed the leading-`{` test keeps YAML out of the formatter. It
  does not: a YAML mapping is not valid JSON, so it reaches the same catch and
  is returned unchanged either way. My "YAML" fixture proved nothing — control
  fails 0.

  What is actually true: the `{` test is a cheap PRE-FILTER that avoids running
  the parser over every large spec, and its one observable effect is on
  top-level JSON that is not an object. The trimStart is the load-bearing part —
  a spec served with a leading newline is ordinary, and without it every such
  document renders as the unformatted single line it arrived as. Comment
  rewritten to say that; both now control at 1.

=== PHASE 6 — the persisted-preference normalizers ===

  New src/lib/preferences.ts (18 tests) and seven more exports on networkSort.ts
  (17 -> 24 tests). App.svelte 11,221 -> 11,172. Suite 533 -> 558.

  What these share is not a feature but a THREAT. Every one of them reads a
  value some earlier version of the app wrote to disk, and every one is used
  without further checking — as a CSS zoom, a setTimeout period, an
  http.Client.Timeout.

  THE REASON THEY BELONG IN ONE FILE IS THAT THEY ARE NOT UNIFORM:
    - ZERO IS A LEGITIMATE REQUEST TIMEOUT ("no timeout"), and an UNSET autosave
      interval. Same input, two correct answers. Collapsing them into a shared
      guard either caps requests the user deliberately left uncapped or lets the
      autosave fire continuously. Control fails 2.
    - zero zoom and zero font size read as UNSET, not as the minimum. An unset
      preference is not a request for the smallest possible interface, and a 1px
      editor is unreadable. Controls fail 1 and 1.
    - a positive autosave period is then FLOORED at 500ms. A stored 1 would be
      honoured as one write per millisecond — a continuous rewrite of the
      collection on disk for as long as the app is open. Control fails 1.
    - a NEGATIVE timeout must not pass: it reaches Go's http.Client.Timeout,
      where it makes every request fail instantly rather than being ignored.
    - "ws" is what an earlier build persisted for WebSocket presets. Dropping it
      turns every preset saved by that build into a no-op. Control fails 1.
    - a theme variant or tab id removed between versions falls back. Otherwise
      the id reaches dataset.themeVariant and matches no stylesheet, leaving the
      app with NO THEME COLOURS; or the drawer opens with no panel inside it.
      Controls fail 1 and 1.

  On networkSort.ts:
    - COLUMN WIDTHS ARE POSITIONAL, so a stored array of the wrong length is not
      partially usable — applying it shifts every column onto the wrong header.
      A build that adds or removes a column must RESET, not merge, which is why
      the length check is equality and not a minimum. Control fails 1.
    - the fallback widths are a FRESH ARRAY. The caller mutates them as the user
      drags, so returning the module's own array lets one resize rewrite the
      fallback every later reset restores to. Control fails 1.
    - the persisted sort keeps its two halves consistent. A direction with no
      key describes a state the table cannot be in, and restoring it shows a
      header marked as sorted over rows in arrival order. Control fails 1.

  Thirteen controls, thirteen failures.

=== PHASE 6 — filesystem name validation ===

  New src/lib/filesystemNames.ts, 16 tests. App.svelte 11,172 -> 11,150.
  Suite 558 -> 574. Four byte-identical *IsReserved copies collapse into one.

  These are not display names. Each becomes a real directory or file inside the
  collection on disk, so this is the last thing between a typed string and a
  path the backend will open. The backend checks again — but by then the dialog
  has already told the user their name was fine.

  Pinned:
    - THE TRAILING-DOT RULE IS WHAT REJECTS "." AND "..". Traversal segments
      never reach the backend as a directory name, and that is a CONSEQUENCE of
      the rule rather than its stated purpose — anyone relaxing the trailing-dot
      rule reopens it. Asserted directly for that reason. Control fails 2.
    - a path separator does not make a name invalid to a filesystem; it makes it
      a different PATH. "auth/tokens" in a rename dialog would write into a
      directory the user never chose.
    - windows device names are rejected ON EVERY PLATFORM. A collection created
      on macOS with a folder named "aux" is simply unopenable once synced to
      Windows, and nothing on the authoring machine says so. Control fails 2.
    - "collection" and "folder" are this app's own metadata basenames. A request
      saved as "folder" produces folder.bru, which the reader treats as the
      enclosing folder's SETTINGS — the request disappears and the folder's
      configuration is replaced by it. Compared case-insensitively, because the
      filesystems this runs on mostly are. Control fails 1.
    - THE TWO HALVES ARE INDEPENDENT AND BOTH TESTED SEPARATELY. "folder" is a
      perfectly legal path segment and a device name is not app metadata, so a
      test of either alone would let the other regress. Controls fail 2 and 1.
    - the environments collision applies ONLY at the collection root; nested
      folders have no environments directory beside them. Control fails 1.
    - that match is a SUBSTRING, so "environments backup" is rejected too. That
      is broader than the collision it guards. PRESERVED AS IT BEHAVED — a
      narrowing control also fails, which is the point: the test pins current
      behaviour, and changing it is a decision, not a move.
    - the extension is stripped only at the END, so a request named
      "v1.bru.backup" keeps its name. Control fails 1.
    - the suggested basename is SANITIZED, so a display name full of separators
      does not become a path when the user accepts the default unedited.

  Twelve controls, twelve failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the keyboard dispatcher ===

  New src/lib/shortcuts.ts, 19 tests. The 158-line `shortcut` function — the
  largest left in the file — becomes a resolve step plus a switch.
  App.svelte 11,150 -> 11,052. Suite 574 -> 593.

  THE ORDER OF THE CHECKS IS THE WHOLE BEHAVIOUR, and order is invisible in a
  chain of early-returning ifs. Every property below is a statement about which
  check runs before which, and not one of them errors when it changes — the
  wrong thing simply happens on a keypress.

  Pinned:
    - ESCAPE CLOSES THE PALETTE BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE CAN CLAIM IT. Reached by a
      lower handler first, the palette stays open with its input focused,
      swallowing every subsequent key. Control fails 2.
    - THE FOUR FIXED KEYS RUN BEFORE THE KEYBINDINGS-ENABLED GATE. Turning
      custom keybindings off must not leave the user unable to dismiss the
      palette or stop a running request — those keys are not configurable, so
      they are not the user's to disable. Control fails 1.
    - a modal OWNS Escape while open, and the guard RETURNS rather than falling
      through: Escape in a dialog must do exactly one thing, and also matching a
      user binding is how a dialog closes and fires an unrelated action on the
      same keypress. Controls fail 3 and 1.
    - switchToLastTab is matched BEFORE the numbered tabs. The two can be bound
      to overlapping combos and whichever is checked first wins. Control fails 2.
    - the action list is the behaviour; a dropped entry produces no error, the
      key just stops working and reads as a binding the user mis-set. Fails 1.

  A REGRESSION I INTRODUCED AND CAUGHT IN THE SAME SLICE. Building the context
  eagerly moved two document-wide querySelectors OUT of the Escape branch and
  INTO the path of every keystroke, ordinary typing included. The original only
  ran them on Escape. Fixed with getters, and the laziness is now pinned by two
  tests that count reads — because a property of the CALL SITE is worthless if
  the resolver stops honouring it. Control fails 2.

  Ten controls, ten failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the native menu dispatcher ===

  New src/lib/nativeMenu.ts, 9 tests. App.svelte 11,052 -> 10,991 — UNDER 11k.
  Suite 593 -> 602.

  THE FIRST CROSS-LANGUAGE CONTRACT TEST IN THE REPO. The native menu is built
  in Go and reaches the frontend as bare command strings over an event. NOTHING
  TYPE-CHECKS ACROSS THAT BOUNDARY: a menu item whose string the frontend does
  not handle is still drawn, still enabled, still clickable — it just does
  nothing, with no error anywhere. So the test reads native_menu.go, extracts
  the constant block, and asserts every declared command is handled. Control
  fails 1.

  Pinned:
    - THREE NAMES DIFFER ACROSS THE BOUNDARY and only those three break on a
      rename: open-git -> open-git-workbench, open-workspace-in-new-window ->
      open-workspace, and both import and open-collection -> import. Matching
      names survive a careless find-and-replace; these do not, and nothing but a
      runtime click would say so. Controls fail 1, 1 and 2.
    - send-or-start is the one context-sensitive item: "Send" over a request,
      "Run" over the runner. Two items would be the alternative and one would
      always be greyed out. Control fails 1.
    - save and save-all are DIFFERENT. One writes the active request, the other
      writes every open tab; collapsing them silently writes files the user did
      not have in front of them. Control fails 1.
    - the reverse direction is asserted too: open-history is handled but never
      emitted. Dead, harmless, and now a DECISION rather than an accumulation.

  A CONTROL THAT FAILED 0 BECAUSE MY MUTATION WAS WRONG, NOT THE TEST. I tried
  to break "every workbench target has a case in the runner" by repointing a
  target at cancel-run — which HAS a case. Every id in the union has one, so the
  test cannot be violated from inside this module at all. The real mutation is
  in the other file: deleting a case from runWorkbenchCommand. Ran that instead;
  it compiles cleanly (the union only constrains the id, not that anyone acts on
  it) and the test fails 1. A forward to an unhandled id is the same silent
  nothing as an unhandled menu string, one layer further in.

  Also worth recording: the first version of that test checked
  commandPaletteCommandIDs, and FAILED ON A COMMAND THAT WORKS. That list is
  what the palette DISPLAYS; import, new-collection and command-palette itself
  are correctly absent from it. Wrong oracle, not a wrong mapping.

  Nine controls, nine failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the proxy and certificate helpers ===

  Six exports onto src/lib/authDefaults.ts (12 -> 25 tests) and one onto
  filesystemNames.ts (16 -> 18). App.svelte 10,991 -> 10,950. Suite 602 -> 616.

  These went into EXISTING modules rather than a new one. authDefaults.ts
  already owned proxyConfigWithDefaults; a seventh lib file for the other half
  of the same merge would be splitting for the sake of a count.

  Pinned:
    - AN UNCONFIGURED COLLECTION PROXY INHERITS; the preferences-level one does
      not. That single field is why both functions exist. Defaulting it to false
      would opt EVERY EXISTING COLLECTION out of the user's system proxy the
      first time this ran. Control fails 2.
    - THE AUTH BLOCK IS MERGED AFTER THE SPREAD, so an override carrying only a
      username keeps the stored PASSWORD. A single spread drops every field the
      caller did not resend — silently clearing proxy credentials on an
      unrelated edit. Control fails 1.
    - off and inherit leave the manual settings ALONE. The user is choosing not
      to use them, not discarding them, and coming back to manual must restore
      what they typed. Control fails 2.
    - the legacy `proxyMode` field is read when `source` is absent — `source`
      replaced it, and ignoring the old one lets an upgrade silently reset a
      configured PAC or manual proxy to the system one. But a stored `source`
      still WINS. Controls fail 1 and 1.
    - DISABLED IS CHECKED FIRST and beats any configured source. Turning the
      proxy off has to mean off, whatever settings are saved beside it.
      Control fails 1.
    - the label is NOT the mode name: "manual" reads as "On", because the
      control it labels is a toggle over the manual settings shown beside it.
      Stored value and visible word are two vocabularies. Control fails 1, and a
      second control proves no two modes share a label — a duplicate would make
      the control ambiguous rather than blank, which is worse.

  Ten controls, ten failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the import decision planner ===

  Seven exports onto src/lib/importPlanning.ts (2 -> 15 tests).
  App.svelte 10,950 -> 10,917. Suite 616 -> 629.

  A REAL DUPLICATE, AND CONSOLIDATING IT CHANGED BEHAVIOUR — stated rather than
  hidden. Two near-identical default-decision constructions existed: the one in
  previewImportSources included `row.conflict !== 'unavailable'`, the fallback
  in importDecisionFor did NOT. Now one function, including the check. That
  makes the fallback STRICTER, and since selectedImportRows already excludes
  unavailable rows the observable behaviour is unchanged — but it is a change,
  and the safe direction, so it is recorded as one.

  Pinned — three defaults, each chosen against the destructive direction:
    - a row that failed to parse, or whose destination is unavailable, is NOT
      selected. Apply would otherwise write a half-read source, or write into a
      place the app already knows it cannot use. Control fails 2.
    - EVERY CHILD IS SELECTED. Importing a collection minus a request the user
      never saw is worse than importing one they must prune. Control fails 2.
    - A CONFLICT DEFAULTS TO RENAME, NEVER REPLACE. Replace destroys a
      collection already on disk, and no default should be able to do that.
      Control fails 1.

  And on re-preview:
    - a prior decision is discarded when the row stops parsing OR the source's
      kind override changed — a source re-read as a different format has
      entirely different children and its old ids mean nothing. Fails 1 and 1.
    - surviving ids are filtered against the new preview. Passing an id the
      backend no longer knows is NOT an error it reports; the import just
      proceeds without that item. Control fails 1.
    - expectedContentHash is an OPTIMISTIC-CONCURRENCY GUARD. The backend
      compares it at apply time and refuses when the file changed since the
      preview, so a source edited between the two steps is never imported as
      something the user never saw. Control fails 1.

  AN EIGHTH GAP OF THE SAME KIND. Rewiring filterFolders to read the requests
  list failed NOTHING: my partial test checked that filterRequests flipped and
  that filterEnvironments did not — never filterFolders. A flag reading another
  kind's counts is false whenever that kind is untouched, which is most of the
  time. Now every kind is deselected in turn and ALL THREE flags are asserted
  each time; the three rewiring controls fail 1, 1 and 2.

  Fourteen controls, fourteen failures after the fix.

=== PHASE 6 — the environment editor and path params ===

  New src/lib/environmentVariables.ts (8 tests), normalizedSearch onto
  sidebarFilter.ts beside searchHit, syncPathParamsForURL onto
  requestScanning.ts beside pathParamNamesFromURL (+5 tests).
  App.svelte 10,917 -> 10,897. Suite 629 -> 642.

  THE PROPERTY THE MODULE EXISTS FOR: THE ROW INDEX IS THE POSITION IN THE
  UNFILTERED LIST. Every edit handler in the editor writes back by index, so an
  index taken after filtering renumbers the rows to their filtered positions —
  and editing a row while a search or the secrets tab is active would silently
  modify a DIFFERENT variable. Control fails 3.

  Also pinned:
    - the two tabs PARTITION the list. Showing a secret in the plain tab puts
      its value on screen in a field with no masking. Control fails 2.
    - the search covers the VALUE, not just the name. Control fails 1.
    - normalizedSearch moved next to searchHit, which was the point: searchHit
      lowercases the CANDIDATE but not the NEEDLE, so an uppercase query passed
      straight in matches nothing and reads as an empty result rather than a
      bug. The pairing makes the requirement findable, and a test now asserts
      it. Control fails 2.
    - path params PRESERVE what the user typed. This runs on every keystroke in
      the URL bar; rebuilding from scratch clears every value the moment any
      other part of the URL is edited. Control fails 2.
    - and the result is ORDERED BY THE URL rather than merged into the old list,
      so a parameter removed from the URL is dropped instead of sitting in the
      table with nowhere to go. Control fails 3.

  A BUG THAT ONLY THE REAL RUNNER FOUND. I wrote `from './sidebarFilter'` with
  no extension. tsc accepted it, svelte-check accepted it, vite built it, and
  the file passed under tsx — but `node --test`, which is what `npm test`
  actually runs, could not resolve it. Every other lib module in the repo uses
  the explicit '.ts'; mine was the only one that did not. Running the slice's
  own test file with the convenient runner would have missed it entirely.

  Eight controls, eight failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the keybinding display layer ===

  Twelve exports onto src/lib/keybindings.ts (34 -> 46 tests).
  App.svelte 10,851 (was 10,897). Suite 642 -> 658.

  Pinned:
    - US-057, NOW TESTED RATHER THAN COMMENTED. Defaults, then preset, then
      override. A shortcut somebody DELIBERATELY SET must not be silently
      replaced by switching preset — which is exactly what merging the other way
      round does. The comment said so; nothing checked it. Control fails 1.
    - an override with no name keeps the BASE name. An override that only
      changes a combo has no name to contribute, and an unnamed row renders as a
      blank line in the shortcuts sheet. `||` not `??`, because the empty string
      is the case. Control fails 1.
    - MODIFIERS ARE EMITTED IN A FIXED ORDER regardless of which was pressed
      first. The stored bindings are written in that order and the two are
      compared AS STRINGS. Control fails 1.
    - a bare modifier contributes NO key part, so holding Shift alone does not
      match a binding whose combo is "shift" — otherwise every modified shortcut
      fires the moment the modifier goes down. Control fails 1.
    - command and alt are the ONLY platform-specific labels, and the test walks
      every other token asserting they agree. Control fails 1.
    - A CHORD IS TWO COMBOS IN SEQUENCE. Splitting on " - " before the parts
      split keeps them separate; collapsing them renders one impossible
      combination. Control fails 1.
    - the flattened defaults must cover EVERY action in EVERY section, or a
      shortcut has no default to reset to and the reset button does nothing.

  Eleven controls, eleven failures — one after a second attempt: my first
