=== PHASE 6 — the key/value row operations ===

  New src/lib/rowEdits.ts, 15 tests. App.svelte 10,851 -> 10,825.
  Suite 658 -> 673.

  Every key/value table in the app — params, headers, form fields, multipart
  parts, file rows, and the same six again inside a response example — drives
  its buttons and its drag handle through these four functions. The bounds
  arithmetic is the same arithmetic in a dozen places, and an off-by-one in it
  DOES NOT THROW: it drops a row, or duplicates one, or ignores a drag.

  Pinned:
    - EVERY PATH RETURNS A NEW ARRAY, including the ones that change nothing.
      Svelte's reactivity is identity-based, so a handler returning the same
      array leaves the table showing its PRE-DRAG order until something else
      invalidates it. Controls fail 6, 1 and 1 — the strongest signal in the
      slice, and the property least visible in the code.
    - a move off either end leaves the order alone rather than WRAPPING. The
      up-arrow on the first row is visible and clickable. Control fails 1.
    - THE Math.min AFTER THE REMOVAL. The array is one shorter once the dragged
      row is spliced out, so dropping onto the LAST position gives an insertion
      index equal to the new length. Two controls — removing the clamp, and
      tightening the bound by one — each drop the row entirely. Fail 1 and 1.
    - an edit past the end fills a BLANK ROW rather than being dropped. That
      path is reachable: a background refresh can shorten the list between the
      render and the keystroke, and losing the character the user just typed is
      worse than gaining a row they can delete. Control fails 1.
    - bulk rows gain `secret` and `description`, which the "name: value" bulk
      format cannot express. Undefined is not the same as absent — it writes a
      DIFFERENT FILE each time the same table is saved. Controls fail 1 and 1.

  Eleven controls, eleven failures.

=== PHASE 6 — tab presentation and the sidebar folder key ===

  New src/lib/workbench/tabPresentation.ts, 15 tests.
  App.svelte 10,825 -> 10,807. Suite 673 -> 688.

  A tab stores REFERENCES — a collection id, an item id, an example id — and
  resolves them against live state on every render. Every fallback here exists
  because one of those references can be missing or stale, and the wrong answer
  is not an error: it is a tab labelled after a different request than the one
  it opens.

  Pinned:
    - THE SIDEBAR FOLDER KEY IS SEPARATED BY NUL, the one byte that cannot
      appear in a collection id or a folder path. A printable separator lets
      collection "a" folder "b/c" and collection "a/b" folder "c" produce the
      SAME KEY, and the two folders then collapse and expand together. Three
      controls — slash, colon, dash — each fail 2.
    - an example with no id is identified by its NAME. Examples written by
      earlier versions have none, and dropping the fallback makes every one of
      them unaddressable: they render, and nothing can be opened or renamed.
      Control fails 2.
    - the tab lookup matches identifier OR bare name, so a tab opened before the
      example gained an id still resolves after an upgrade rewrites the file.
      One comparison leaves those tabs on a BLANK PANE rather than an error.
      Control fails 1.
    - "Scratch request" vs "Request" is the one thing a user needs to know
      before closing a tab. Control fails 1.
    - only DELETE and OPTIONS are abbreviated; the test walks every other method
      asserting it is NOT. Control fails 1.
    - a scratch collection is recognised from EITHER source, because one loaded
      before the flag existed has only the workspace's id and one from another
      workspace has only its own flag. Controls fail 1 and 2. And an absent
      scratch id must match nothing — otherwise every collection reads as
      scratch, and scratch collections are excluded from git, export and the
      collection list.

  I WROTE THE BUG THIS MODULE DOCUMENTS AGAINST. My first draft of
  sidebarFolderKey used a literal SPACE instead of NUL. Then the fix left a RAW
  NUL BYTE in the test file — invisible in an editor, and a byte a later edit
  would silently drop. Both corrected; the escape sequence is now in both files
  and no raw NUL remains in either.

  Eleven controls, eleven failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the code font family and the example name ===

  codeFontFamilyFor onto preferences.ts (18 -> 22 tests); prettifyJSON and
  suggestedResponseExampleName onto responseExampleEdits.ts (27 -> 33).
  App.svelte 10,807 -> 10,792. Suite 688 -> 698.

  THE FONT ESCAPING IS A CSS INJECTION GUARD, which is not obvious from the
  code. The result goes straight into a custom property via
  style.setProperty('--code-font-family', ...), and the name is FREE TEXT the
  user types into a preferences field. A name containing a double quote closes
  the quoted string early and everything after it parses as CSS: a font called
  `a", x: y; --z: "b` is a declaration, not a font. Control fails 2.

    - backslashes are escaped for the same reason and MUST BE HANDLED IN THE
      SAME PASS, which the single character class guarantees. Escaping quotes
      and then backslashes doubles the backslash just inserted and leaves the
      quote bare — the exact thing being guarded against. Controls fail 1 and 2.
    - the default name short-circuits instead of being quoted: the fallback is
      SEVERAL families, and quoting it whole names one font that does not exist,
      so every code surface renders in the browser default. Controls fail 1, 2.

  Also pinned:
    - prettify returns the INPUT on a parse failure, so the button is safe to
      press on XML, HTML or a truncated body. Control fails 1.
    - the first example is offered a plain "example", and the scan then SKIPS
      TAKEN SUFFIXES rather than counting. Deleting the middle of a run would
      otherwise suggest a name already in use, and create fails on a duplicate.
      Controls fail 2 and 1.

  A NO-OP GUARD REMOVED RATHER THAN TESTED. `filter(Boolean)` on the taken-names
  set could not change any answer — every candidate generated is non-empty — and
  the control confirmed it: fails 0. My own comment had already admitted it
  "would not change the answer". A guard that changes no answer is worse than
  none, because it reads as load-bearing. Deleted, and the test restated to
  document that blank names pass through harmlessly rather than pretending to
  pin a filter.

  Nine controls, eight failures and one deletion.

=== PHASE 6 — runner selection and the git remote/branch reconcilers ===

  New src/lib/runnerSelection.ts (14 tests); reconcileGitRemoteSelection and
  reconcileGitBranch onto gitWorkbench.ts (6 -> 10).
  App.svelte 10,792 -> 10,776. Suite 698 -> 718.

  THE SELECTION IS A LIST, NOT A SET, AND THE RUNNER EXECUTES IT IN ORDER. That
  single fact is why adding an id is a REBUILD and not a push: appending would
  order the run by the sequence the user happened to CLICK checkboxes, and a run
  that fires the login request third because it was ticked third fails in a way
  that looks like a broken API rather than a broken selection. Control fails 4.

    - deselecting may filter, because removing an id cannot disturb the order of
      the ones that remain. The asymmetry is deliberate.
    - stale ids are dropped on any select — a request deleted while the runner
      screen was open cannot survive into the run. Control fails 3.
    - AN ITEM WITH NO TYPE IS RUNNABLE. Requests written before the field
      existed are HTTP, and excluding them silently empties the runner for every
      older collection. Control fails 2.
    - WEBSOCKET IS EXCLUDED: a socket is a session, not a request-response pair,
      so there is nothing for a sequential runner to wait for. Control fails 2.
    - the count ignores ids that are gone, and the test asserts the count and
      select-all AGREE after a deletion — a stale id counted as selected makes
      the count equal the length and the click CLEARS instead of selecting.
      Controls fail 2 and 4.

  On the git side:
    - the selected remote SURVIVES a refresh, so it does not move the user off
      the remote they were about to push to; and falls back to the first, since
      remotes with none selected offer a push button that cannot work. 1 and 1.
    - with NO remotes the URL is CLEARED. A stale URL beside an empty list reads
      as a configured remote, and connect opens pre-filled with an address the
      repository no longer has. Control fails 1.
    - a branch that no longer exists falls back to head, or checkout targets a
      ref deleted elsewhere and git refuses with a message about a ref rather
      than about the picker. Controls fail 2 and 1.

  Eleven controls, eleven failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the DevTools resize arithmetic ===

  resizeAdjacentColumns onto networkSort.ts (24 -> 30 tests) and
  sizeFromTrailingEdgeDrag onto preferences.ts (22 -> 24).
  App.svelte 10,776 -> 10,774. Suite 718 -> 726.

  Only two lines came out of App.svelte, and that is the point: the ARITHMETIC
  moved, not the DOM plumbing. A drag handler cannot be tested without a
  browser; the sums inside it can.

  Pinned:
    - COLUMN RESIZE IS A PAIRED OPERATION. The two columns move together and by
      the same amount, so the table's TOTAL WIDTH IS INVARIANT across a drag.
      That is what keeps the header aligned with the rows under it — a total
      that grows pushes the last column off the panel with no way to drag it
      back. Controls fail 4 and 5.
    - THE DELTA IS CLAMPED AGAINST BOTH MINIMUMS, NOT ONE. Clamping only the
      dragged column lets the NEIGHBOUR collapse to nothing; clamping only the
      neighbour lets the DRAGGED column go negative, which flips the layout.
      Each bound is the distance that column has left before it hits the floor.
      Two controls, one per side, fail 2 each — and a third test walks every
      delta from -9999 to 9999 asserting the total is preserved regardless.
    - a drag on the LAST column is a no-op, because there is no neighbour to
      take the width from. Without the bound the code reads index+1 past the end
      and produces NaN. Control fails 1.
    - THE TRAILING-EDGE DRAG SUBTRACTS, and that inversion is the whole
      function. The details panel is pinned to the RIGHT edge and the drawer to
      the BOTTOM, so dragging the handle LEFT or UP makes them BIGGER. Adding
      instead compiles, runs, and moves the panel the wrong way — a sign error
      whose only symptom is a handle that feels backwards. Control fails 2.

  Seven controls, seven failures.

=== PHASE 6 — the optimistic patch ===

  New src/lib/optimisticPatch.ts, 10 tests. App.svelte 10,774 -> 10,760.
  Suite 726 -> 736.

  This runs on EVERY KEYSTROKE in the request editor: the edit is written into
  the state tree first and persisted asynchronously behind the coalescer.

  Pinned:
    - `draft: true` IS NOT COSMETIC. The tab strip reads it to show the unsaved
      dot and the close guard reads it before letting a tab go. An edit applied
      without it LOOKS SAVED, and closing the tab discards it with no prompt.
      Control fails 3.
    - and it is set AFTER the patch spread, so a patch carrying draft:false
      cannot unmark the request mid-edit — the one value it must never take
      here. Control fails 1.
    - COLLECTIONS AND ITEMS WITHOUT THE TARGET ARE RETURNED BY IDENTITY, not
      copied. Per keystroke, every $derived and {#each} keyed on a collection
      would re-run for all of them otherwise — one character becoming a
      re-render of the whole sidebar. Controls fail 1 and 1.
    - EVERY workspace is searched, not just the first. A collection id is unique
      across the tree, and a request can be patched from a window whose active
      workspace is not the one that owns it. Control fails 3.
    - the original tree is never mutated. Assigning in place would make the
      "before" and "after" the same object, and Svelte would see no change at
      all. Control fails 1.

  Seven controls, seven failures.

  Go side re-verified this round: 25 packages ok, golangci-lint 0 issues,
  binding surface 188.

=== PHASE 6 — RUNTIME VERIFICATION (plan step 7) ===

  `wails dev` with the Go bridge attached, driven through Playwright. This had
  not run since ~15 slices ago — before the keyboard dispatcher rewrite, the
  native menu mapping, the optimistic patch, and every preference normalizer.
  NONE of those can be exercised by node --test, and svelte-check cannot tell
  me whether Cmd+K still opens anything.

  Verified live, each against the module that now owns it:
    - the app boots and renders fully. ONE console error in the whole session,
      a favicon 404. No application errors at any point.
    - Cmd+K opens Global Search, Escape closes it — the resolve+switch
      dispatcher that replaced the 158-line if-chain works.
    - --sidebar-width, --code-font-family, data-theme and data-theme-variant
      all read exactly what layout.ts, preferences.ts (codeFontFamilyFor) and
      the theme normalizers produce.
    - THE SIDEBAR CLAMP IS EXACT IN PRODUCTION: dragging to 9999 gives 420px,
      to 10 gives 220px — the module's two constants, observed through CSS.
    - writeWorkbenchLayout persists under the SCOPED key
      `liteapi.workbench.v3.<scope>.sidebar-width`, both halves together. The
      empty-scope guard is doing its job: a real scope exists, so writes happen.
    - normalizedSearch + searchHit: "ECHO", "echo" and "  EcHo  " all match the
      same request and "zzzz" matches none. That is the exact contract I pinned
      when moving normalizedSearch next to searchHit.
    - tabLabel's fallback is VISIBLE IN PRODUCTION — two open tabs read
      "Request", the label for a tab whose item does not resolve.
    - a real HTTP round trip: 501, 14 ms, 497 B, body rendered. And the failure
      path first: "connection refused" surfaced correctly with timing intact.
    - the network log recorded both, the failed one with status 0 — the same
      `?? 0` fallback the looped numeric-column test pins.

  Nothing regressed. The one 404 is a missing favicon, which predates all of it.

=== FINDING 3 — the misfiled helpers and their spurious package edges ===

  Two new packages: internal/urlbuild (9 tests) and internal/wsmessage (6).
  Go packages 25 -> 27. FIVE spurious import edges deleted, not the four the
  plan estimated — scripting was importing codegen for the same reason and had
  been missed.

  WHAT THE EDGES SAID THAT WAS UNTRUE:
    - internal/cookiejar imported a CODE GENERATOR to decide which jar a request
      belongs to.
    - internal/wsexec imported one to dial a socket.
    - internal/scripting imported one to hand a URL to a user script.
      None of the three generates code. The URL builders now live in
      internal/urlbuild.
    - internal/store/bru and internal/store/yamlstore — two FILE FORMAT READERS
      — imported a WEBSOCKET EXECUTOR to read a field off disk. Neither opens a
      socket. The message vocabulary now lives in internal/wsmessage.

  codegen and wsexec keep re-export vars, so no caller outside had to change and
  the old names still resolve.

  A SIXTH INSTANCE DELIBERATELY LEFT ALONE. store/bru still imports codegen for
  NormalizedBodyMode, which looks like exactly the same finding. It is a TRAP,
  and the repo already carries the scar: yamlstore_test.go records that a
  mechanical rewrite once pointed ITS body-mode call sites at
  codegen.NormalizedBodyMode because the names were near-identical. THE TWO
  FUNCTIONS ARE NOT THE SAME — yamlstore's canonicalises the whole vocabulary
  ("" -> "none", four urlencoded spellings collapse), codegen's rewrites two
  form modes and passes everything else through. The redirect COMPILED and every
  test passed. Moving NormalizedBodyMode into a shared package would recreate
  precisely that naming proximity, one import closer. Left where it is.

  That is the fourth same-shaped finding in this project with a different right
  answer, after keyBindingSignature (collapse), sidebarGroupWindow (keep both,
  document) and networkSortLabel/AriaValue (keep both, test against each other).

  Pinned in urlbuild:
    - an unmatched :token is LEFT IN PLACE, not blanked. A URL still showing
      :id is visibly wrong; one collapsed to "//" requests a DIFFERENT RESOURCE
      and the server answers it. Control fails 1.
    - PATH PARAMS ARE SUBSTITUTED BEFORE THE QUERY IS APPENDED, so a path value
      containing "?" cannot silently become the start of the query string.
      Control fails 1.
    - the URL is INTERPOLATED FIRST, so a variable expanding to a path token is
      itself substitutable. Control fails 1.
    - the separator is chosen from what the URL already ends with. Wrong gives
      "??" or "&&", which most servers accept and parse differently from what
      was meant. Controls fail 2 and 1.
    - a DISABLED param is not a param, in either position. Controls fail 1, 1.

  Pinned in wsmessage:
    - an unknown type reads as TEXT rather than failing. This runs while READING
      A FILE; refusing would cost the user every other request in it. Control
      fails 1.
    - EVERY branch interpolates, including the default — a message with a
      literal {{token}} is worse than one whose mode was guessed. Control 1.
    - "bin" and "binary" agree; the empty mode reads the TEXT field. 2 and 1.

  Eleven controls, eleven failures.

  Findings 1 and 2 were already resolved: the scripting sandbox's separate HTTP
  client is DELIBERATE (documented at two call sites), and the tautological
  clone test was rewritten to assert nil-ness with the reason recorded.
  ALL THREE PLAN FINDINGS ARE NOW CLOSED.

=== A CORRECTION: I HAVE BEEN QUOTING A COVERAGE NUMBER I CANNOT REPRODUCE ===

  Every summary I wrote this session said "Go coverage 81.5% -> 83.4%". Trying
  to re-measure it after adding two packages, I got FOUR DIFFERENT ANSWERS:

    go test -coverprofile ./...                     50.8%
    mean of per-package `-cover` figures            47.5%  (31 packages)
    root package alone                              75.4%
    go test -coverpkg=./internal/... ./...          29.4%

  None is 83.4%, and the spread is far too wide for any of them to be "the"
  number. This file ALREADY RECORDS this exact trap — an earlier entry corrects
  a run of coverage figures that moved 78.2 -> 79.9 -> 77.8 while only tests
  were added, and identifies the cause as a caching artifact. I read that entry
  only after producing the four numbers above.

  So: THE COVERAGE PERCENTAGE IS WITHDRAWN. I should not have carried a figure
  forward across summaries without re-deriving it, and I will not quote one
  again without stating the exact command beside it.

  What IS reproducible, and is what the restructure actually needed to hold:
    - 27 Go packages, ALL with tests, ALL passing (0 packages without test files)
    - internal/urlbuild and internal/wsmessage: 100.0% each
    - golangci-lint: 0 issues
    - -race: clean on the new packages
    - GOOS=windows and GOOS=darwin/arm64: both build
    - binding surface: 188, unchanged
    - frontend: 736 tests, svelte-check 0/0, eslint clean, vite build ok

  The number was never the point of this work; the negative controls were. Those
  are per-slice, reproducible, and recorded above — a claim only counted here
  once a deliberate break proved the check would catch it.

=== THE COVERAGE INSTRUMENT, FIXED — and the previous entry CORRECTED ===

  I withdrew the 83.4% figure in the entry above. THAT WITHDRAWAL WAS WRONG.
  The number was right; my four re-measurements were the artifacts.

  THE CAUSE, found by counting lines in the profile itself: with -coverpkg over
  a multi-package build, EVERY test binary instruments EVERY listed package and
  writes a FULL profile. `go test -coverprofile` concatenates them, so one
  source block appears ONCE PER BINARY — here 10,485 unique blocks became
  283,457 lines across 28 binaries, some blocks repeated 28 times.

  `go tool cover -func` treats each occurrence as a SEPARATE BLOCK. A block
  exercised by one binary and not the other 27 reads as 1 covered out of 28
  rather than 1 out of 1. So THE REPORTED PERCENTAGE FALLS AS TEST BINARIES ARE
  ADDED, which is exactly backwards — and it is why this file already contains
  one correction about figures that moved while only tests were added. The
  earlier entry blamed caching; caching is a real second cause (-count=1 fixes
  that one) but the concatenation is the larger one, and it was never found.

  FIXED, not just documented:
    tools/coverage/mergeprofile.go   collapses the profile so each block appears
                                     once, taking the highest count any binary
                                     recorded. Output is sorted, so two profiles
                                     can be DIFFED and not merely compared.
    qa/coverage.sh                   runs it for both scopes and prints both.

  Verified: two full runs produce BYTE-IDENTICAL merged profiles.

  AND THE SCOPE HAS TO BE STATED, which is the other half of why I got four
  answers. These measure different things and I had been quoting them as if
  interchangeable:

    internal   83.4%  (10,485 blocks)  the extracted, unit-tested packages
    all        78.6%  (20,071 blocks)  plus root package main

  internal/urlbuild and internal/wsmessage are at 100.0% each.

  The rule for this file from here: NEVER quote a coverage percentage without
  the scope beside it, and never by hand — run qa/coverage.sh.

=== THE BINDING SURFACE GATE — the plan's "single cheapest guard", implemented ===

  The plan asked for the FULL bound-method list to be snapshotted and diffed,
  and explicitly warned that a count is not enough. I had been checking only the
  count (188) for every slice this session. A count of 188 survives:
    - a changed parameter or return type
    - one method unexported while another is added
    - a rename in Go that was never regenerated into the frontend
  All three compile on both sides.

  qa/bindings.sh now makes THREE checks, because each catches what the others
  cannot:
    1. the Go signature list vs qa/baseline/bindings.txt
    2. the Go method NAMES vs frontend/wailsjs/go/main/App.d.ts — the generated
       bindings are COMMITTED, so a Go rename without `wails generate module`
       leaves the frontend calling a method that no longer exists, and NOTHING
       IN EITHER BUILD SAYS SO because the .d.ts still type-checks against
       itself
    3. App.d.ts vs App.js, which are generated separately

  Wired into ci.yml, beside the cross-compile gate added earlier.

  Controls — five, all caught:
    - a renamed bound method                                      caught
    - the generated TypeScript drifted from Go                    caught
    - App.js drifted from App.d.ts                                caught
    - a method unexported (188 -> 187)                            caught
    - A SIGNATURE CHANGE THAT COMPILES CLEANLY                    caught
      (extra parameter on a frontend-only method; the gate prints the exact
      -/+ line. THIS IS THE CASE A COUNT MISSES ENTIRELY, and the reason the
      plan called for a list rather than a number.)

  AND A FAILURE IN MY OWN CONTROL HARNESS, worth recording because it produced a
  CONFIDENT WRONG ANSWER. My first run reported "PASSED — GATE IS BLIND" twice.
  Both mutations had targeted app.go; the method lives in request_lifecycle.go
  after the file split, so NEITHER EDIT LANDED — the harness judged a gate
  against a change that was never made. The runner now refuses to report a
  verdict unless the substitution matched exactly once. Same doctrine as
  tools/extraction/control.sh, which I had not applied here.

  Verified today: Go 188 == App.d.ts 188 == App.js 188, name sets identical.

=== FRONTEND COVERAGE SWEEP + full -race ===

  Full `go test -count=1 -race ./...`: 27 packages, 0 races. Not run across all
  packages this session before now; the plan lists it as verification step 3.

  Frontend coverage measured with node --experimental-test-coverage:
  99.71% lines / 98.36% functions before, 99.84% / 98.37% after. The only module
  below 95% was requestScanning.ts at 92.11% lines — 98.25% now, and every
  uncovered range there was a REAL scan path:

    multipart part filePath/contentType    a {{env}}.json filename is ordinary,
                                           and unscanned it uploads a file
                                           literally named "{{env}}.json"
    file body rows                         both shapes reach the wire
    websocket / grpc message fields        a literal token over a socket has no
                                           status code to notice it
    the malformed-URL fallback             a URL the constructor rejects still
                                           has a path the user typed into

  9 tests. Suite 736 -> 745.

  !!! I READ AN EMPTY RESULT AS A BUG AND IT WAS MY TEST !!!

  My first version called variableNamesForRequest for the message and array
  cases and got []. I wrote that up as "WebSocket and gRPC message variables
  return nothing — that is the module's stated failure mode." THAT WAS WRONG.
  Lines 147-180 belong to collectPromptNames, a DIFFERENT and larger scanner:
  variableNamesForRequest reads the request's own fields for {{name}};
  collectPromptNames walks collection, folders, environment and messages for
  {{?name}} only. The module's header says exactly this in its first paragraph.
  The scanner was right; the test asked the wrong thing. Rewritten against
  collectPromptNames, and the reason recorded in the test file.

  THREE CONTROLS THEN FAILED 0, and each was a different answer:

    scan(body.filePath)      NOT redundant, though it looks it. fileBodyRows
                             synthesises a row from those two fields for the
                             single-file shape — but when files[] is ALSO
                             populated it returns only the array, and a filePath
                             left over from an earlier single-file body goes
                             unscanned. Test added for that exact shape; the
                             control now fails 1.
    the malformed-URL catch  my URL "{{base}}/users/:id?x=1" PARSES FINE once
                             http:// is prepended, so the catch never ran.
                             Replaced with "http://[bad/:id" and "://:id",
                             which genuinely throw. Control now fails 1.
    the Array.isArray branch GENUINELY REDUNDANT. Object.values on an array
                             returns its elements, so the object branch below
                             handles arrays identically. NO TEST CAN
                             DISTINGUISH IT. Kept — it says what it means and
                             avoids materialising a values array for what is
                             usually the largest structure here — but the
                             comment now says it is a fast path and that no
                             test covers it, rather than leaving it looking
                             load-bearing.

  That last one is the second time this session a guard turned out to change no
  answer. The first (filter(Boolean) on example names) was deleted. This one is
  kept and labelled, because unlike that one it earns its place on cost.

--- the last uncovered range: OData-style path parameters ---

  requestScanning.ts 98.25% -> 100.00% lines, 100.00% functions. Suite 745 -> 748.

  The last gap was a SECOND, SEPARATE path-parameter pass I had not noticed:
  a segment shaped like a FUNCTION CALL — Users(:id) — which is how OData-style
  APIs express a key. The plain :name scan cannot reach inside it because the
  whole segment is not a token, so there is a dedicated pass for that shape.

  Without it those parameters are INVISIBLE: the request sends a literal ":id"
  inside the path and the server 404s on a resource whose name contains a colon.
  Control fails 2.

  Pinned alongside:
    - one segment can carry SEVERAL, as a composite key does: f(:a,:b) -> a, b
    - the segment must be NAME(...). A bare "(:id)" is NOT a function call and
      is left alone rather than guessed at. Relaxing the name to optional makes
      it match, and the control fails 1.

  This is also the answer to something I got wrong earlier in the session: I had
  guessed "https://api.test/(:id)" should yield ['id'] and treated the empty
  result as suspicious. It is correct — there is no function name, so it is not
  that form.

--- branch coverage: globalSearch, the lowest module at 83.67% ---

  Lines and functions were already near 100% everywhere, so the remaining gaps
  are BRANCHES — which is where an untested fallback hides. globalSearch.ts was
  the lowest: 83.67% -> 94.34% branch, 100% lines. Suite 748 -> 755.

  Every uncovered branch was a FALLBACK THE USER SEES:
    - a collection with no path is subtitled by its REQUEST COUNT, and one with
      no format labels itself "collection". An unfilled fallback renders a blank
      cell, which reads like a rendering bug rather than like missing data.
    - THOSE TWO FALLBACKS ARE WRITTEN TWICE — once for the empty-query listing
      and once for a matched collection — and must agree, or the same collection
      describes itself differently depending on whether the user has typed
      anything. Now tested against each other rather than separately.
      Control fails 1.
    - a request with no method falls back through its TYPE to a constant. The
      meta chip is what tells a GET row from a POST row at a glance. Fails 1.
    - a request subtitle names its folder only when it HAS one; repeating the
      collection name twice would be noise. Fails 1.
    - A ONE-CHARACTER QUERY IS VALID ONLY IF ALPHANUMERIC. "a" usefully narrows
      a long list; "." matches on substring and would return every request with
      a dot anywhere in a URL, which is all of them. BOTH DIRECTIONS control:
      accepting punctuation fails 2, rejecting letters fails 2.
    - the sort's NAME tiebreak. Without it two results equal on rank and type
      keep whatever order the collection was stored in, so the list reshuffles
      between renders. Fails 1.

  Seven controls, seven failures.

  Frontend totals: 99.87% lines, 96.94% branch, 98.38% functions, 755 tests.

--- branch coverage: responseExampleEdits, 85.45% -> 94.74% ---

  Suite 755 -> 761. The existing 33 tests were thorough on the main paths, so
  every remaining branch was an edge — and two of them are behaviours nobody had
  written down.

  THE ORDER YOU TYPE THE TWO CELLS IN DECIDES WHETHER THE BODY TYPE FOLLOWS.
  Editing the VALUE of a row already named content-type re-derives the body
  type. NAMING a row content-type does not, because the guard requires a
  content-type header to have existed BEFORE the edit. The doc says only a value
  change is a statement about the body, so this is deliberate — but it means
  typing name-then-value gives one result and value-then-name gives another.
  PINNED AS IT BEHAVES; changing it is a decision, not a test. Control fails 2.

  THE FIRST FILE ROW AUTO-SELECTS AND LATER ONES DO NOT. A list with attachments
  and no selection sends nothing, so the first must be selected; but a second
  attachment must not silently steal the selection from the file the user
  already chose. Both directions control: always-select fails 1, never-select
  fails 2.

  Also pinned: a header row with no name at all does not throw on the lowercase
  lookup (fails 1); an out-of-range removal changes nothing rather than splicing
  from the end (fails 1); an absent file list normalises to [] so the caller can
  render a table without a null check.

  Six controls, five failures and one rejected for not compiling — the absent-
  request mutation could not be expressed without a type error, which is the
  compiler already enforcing what the test would have.

  Frontend totals: 99.87% lines, 97.12% branch, 98.39% functions, 761 tests.

--- branch coverage: bulkEdit, 86.54% -> 96.08% ---

  Suite 761 -> 766. The 25 existing tests already covered the round-trip story
  thoroughly, so the gaps were the defensive edges:

    - A ROW WITH NO enabled FLAG IS ENABLED. The check is `=== false`, not
      falsy, and that matters: rows built by an import, a paste or a generated
      row omit the flag, and a falsy read would render every one of them with
      the disabled marker and bring them back SWITCHED OFF. Control fails 1.
    - a half-built row (no name, no value) still renders and parses back. The
      bulk editor is reached from a table that can hold an empty row, and
      throwing there makes the button unusable on an ordinary table. Fails 1.
    - AN EQUALS SEPARATES A LINE WITH NO COLON AT ALL. The existing test covered
      an equals BEFORE a colon; this is the case with no colon to compare
      against — the ordinary shape of a query string or a .env line pasted
      straight in. Control fails 3.
    - the disabled marker is stripped BEFORE the separator is looked for, so a
      disabled line splits at the right place instead of folding the marker into
      the name. Control fails 5 — the widest blast radius in the module.

  A THIRD DEAD GUARD, AND THE THIRD DIFFERENT DISPOSITION.

    `previous ?? []` inside parseBulkText could be removed with nothing failing.
    The parameter is already declared `previous: BulkRow[] = []`, so a DEFAULT
    PARAMETER fires for an explicit undefined and the nullish guard never runs.
    It would only catch an explicit null, which TypeScript forbids and no caller
    passes. DELETED.

    The three this session, and why they were treated differently:
      filter(Boolean) on example names   deleted — no cost, no effect
      Array.isArray in the scanner       KEPT and labelled — redundant for
                                         correctness but avoids materialising a
                                         values array for a large JSON body
      previous ?? [] here                deleted — shadowed by a default
                                         parameter three lines above it

    "Redundant" was not one answer three times. It was three.

  Frontend totals: 99.87% lines, 97.29% branch, 98.39% functions, 766 tests.

--- branch coverage: urlSegments and the tooltip readOnly/editable pair ---

  Suite 766 -> 774. variableResolution 92.42% -> 94.37% branch.

  On urlSegments:
    - an EMPTY token "{{}}" stays literal text; the pattern needs a character
      between the braces, and rendering it as a variable would put a tooltip on
      something with no name to resolve. Control fails 1.
    - PATH TOKENS ARE ENABLED BY THE PRESENCE of the pathParams argument, not
      its length. An empty array means "this field can hold path parameters,
      there just are none yet", and a :name typed into an empty table must still
      render so the tooltip can offer to create it. Control fails 3.
    - THE OVERLAY AND THE PROMPT SCANNER AGREE ON WHITESPACE. "{{? name }}" is
      not a prompt to either. If the overlay rendered it as one while
      requestScanning did not collect it, the user would see a prompt token in
      the URL bar and NEVER BE ASKED for its value — the request goes out with
      the literal text in it. Cross-module test; controls fail 2 and 1.

  !!! I ASSERTED AN INVARIANT THAT WAS NOT ONE, AND THE TEST CAUGHT ME !!!

  I wrote a test asserting readOnly and editable are ALWAYS opposite across all
  six tooltip producers, having read `{#if info.readOnly}` and
  `{:else if info.editable}` as a complementary pair. IT FAILED — and the
  failure was right. The markup is:

      {#if editing}       a textarea
      {:else if editable} an edit button
      {:else}             the value, shown plainly      <- a real third arm
      {/if}
      {#if readOnly}      a "read-only" note            <- a SEPARATE if

  I had read two `{#if}` lines out of context and missed the `{:else}` between
  them. `readOnly: false, editable: false` is a LEGITIMATE THIRD STATE — shown
  plainly, not labelled — and pathParamTooltipInfo produces exactly that for a
  :name the params table has no row for yet. That is correct: the parameter is
  not read-only in principle, there is just nothing there to edit.

  The invariant that IS real is one-directional: never BOTH. readOnly and
  editable together renders an edit button beside a note saying the value cannot
  be edited, and clicking it opens an editor whose save has nowhere to go.
  Rewritten that way, plus tests for the third state and for the invalid-name
  case. Four controls, four failures (3, 2, 2, 2).

  Second time this session a test failure was RIGHT and my premise was wrong;
  the first was reading an empty scan result as a bug when I had called the
  wrong function. Both times the cheap move would have been to "fix" the code.

  Frontend totals: 99.88% lines, 97.41% branch, 98.40% functions, 774 tests.

--- urlSegments 88.89% -> 94.12% branch: two guards, two opposite answers ---

  Lines were already 100%, so the remaining branches were conditions no input
  can take both ways. Two nullish guards, and the controls disagreed about them:

    pathParams ?? []   DID NOT COMPILE without it. Reaching that line means the
                       regex matched group 2, which only exists when pathParams
                       was supplied — but that correlation is beyond the type
                       system, so the narrowing must be written out. KEPT, and
                       now commented as being FOR THE COMPILER: it is a branch
                       no test can ever cover, and that is not a gap.

    match[1] ?? ''     compiles without it and fails NOTHING. Group 1 is
                       guaranteed at that point because group 2 is handled above
                       and `continue`s. DELETED — it read as protection against
                       an undefined group when what actually guarantees it is
                       the control flow three lines up. Replaced with a comment
                       saying so.

  FIVE REDUNDANT-LOOKING GUARDS THIS SESSION, FIVE DIFFERENT DISPOSITIONS:

    filter(Boolean) on example names   deleted — no cost, no effect
    Array.isArray in the scanner       kept — redundant for correctness, but
                                       avoids materialising a values array for
                                       a large JSON body
    previous ?? [] in bulkEdit         deleted — shadowed by the parameter's own
                                       default three lines above
    match[1] ?? '' here                deleted — guaranteed by control flow, and
                                       misleading about what guarantees it
    pathParams ?? [] here              KEPT — unreachable at runtime but
                                       REQUIRED BY THE COMPILER

  "It fails no test" was never once the whole answer. The question each time was
  what the code would cost if removed, and to whom.

  Frontend totals: 99.88% lines, 97.44% branch, 98.40% functions, 774 tests.

--- networkSort and importPlanning: the defensive arms ---

  Suite 774 -> 778. importPlanning.ts reaches 100/100/100.
